Sir Charles could understand the sufferings, the difficulties, the humiliations of poverty. But the Marquis might have known that, far or near, he could have counted on the assistance of his friend. For some years past he had never hesitated to dip into his purse. But that was for those mysterious journeys and those secret and important missions his Princes had never hesitated to entrust him with without ever troubling their heads about the means. Such was the nature of Princes, Sir Charles reflected with complete bitterness. And now came this﻿ ﻿… A whole young life thrown away perhaps, in its innocence, in its ignorance.﻿ ﻿… How old could Adèle be now? Eighteen or nineteen. Not so very much younger than her mother was when he used to see so much of her in Paris and Versailles, when she had managed to put such an impress on his heart that later he did not care whom he married or where he lived.﻿ ﻿… Inevitable!﻿ ﻿… Sir Charles could not be angry with the Marquise, now a mere languid shadow of that invincible charm that his heart had not been able to resist. She and her husband must have given up all their hopes, all their loyal royalist hopes before they could bow like this to the Inevitable. It had not been difficult for him to learn to love that fascinating French child as though she had been another daughter of his own. For a moment he experienced an anguish so acute that it made him move slightly in his chair. Half aloud he muttered the thought that came into his mind:

“Austerlitz has done it.”

===Chinese===
随后，仿佛再也按捺不住要剖白心曲的冲动，他以下面这句话开始了新的一行：

这一切原是女儿自己的主意，这点我自不必向您多说。求亲一事是循规蹈矩地通过G勋爵（Lord G.）向我们提出的——G勋爵是我的至交，虽说他属于福克斯先生（Mr. Fox）那一派，而蒙特韦索伯爵多数的英国朋友也都出自那一派。可我们刚把这门亲事告知阿黛勒，她便迈出了一步——您或许会觉得难以置信；从某种角度去看，甚至可以称之为不孝，倘若「不孝」二字当真可以用在我们阿黛勒这样一贯温柔孝顺的孩子身上的话。是她亲自提出请求，并未与她母亲或我商量；G勋爵竟也软弱到应允在他自己府上为她与伯爵安排了一次会面。那两人彼此之间能说些什么，我实在无从想象。待我们风闻此事，事情已然定到这般田地，我们除了接受这不可避免的结局，再无别的可为……

查尔斯爵士又一次让那双白皙贵气的大手落回膝头。女儿那颗深色发色的头颅低垂在绣架上方，而他眼前却浮现出另一颗头颅——与之截然不同，一头极浅的金发——就偎在它旁边。那颗头颅曾是他隐居生活的一部分，也曾得到他极大的一份钟爱。那一份爱究竟有多大，直到此刻他才发觉——就在这一刻，他感到它在某种意义上已永远地失去了。「不可避免，」他喃喃自语，语调半是讥诮、半是痛楚。查尔斯爵士能够理解贫乏的种种苦楚、艰难与屈辱。可侯爵本该知道，不论远近，他都可以指望这位朋友的襄助。这些年来，每逢那些神秘的旅程、那些机密而重要的使命——他的亲王们从不犹豫地交付给他，却从不过问其中花销的来源——他便毫不犹豫地慷慨解囊。亲王们的脾性便是如此，查尔斯爵士满心苦涩地想。而如今又来了这个……一整个年轻的命运，或许就此葬送了，在她天真无知之中。……阿黛勒如今能有多大呢？十八，抑或十九。比她母亲当年并不小多少——当年他在巴黎和凡尔赛（Versailles）时常与她相见，她竟在他心上烙下了那样深的印记，以致后来他娶谁为妻、住在哪里，他都全不在意了。……不可避免！……查尔斯爵士无法生侯爵夫人的气——她如今只不过是当年那不可抗拒的魅力的一个慵懒影子，而他的心当年竟无力抵挡。她与丈夫必定是先放弃了全部的希望、放弃了一切忠诚的保皇党的希望，才能这般向「不可避免」低头。当年他学着去爱那个迷人的法国小女孩，爱得如同自己的另一个女儿，并不曾费什么力气。有一瞬，他感到一阵如此尖锐的痛楚，竟使他在椅中微微一动。他半出声地喃喃念出了心头涌起的那句话：

「是奥斯特里茨（Austerlitz）造成的。」

===Original===
Miss Latham raised her lustrous dark eyes with an enquiring expression and murmured, “Papa?”

Sir Charles got up and seized his stick. “Nothing, my dear, nothing.” He wanted to be alone. But on going out of the room he stopped by the embroidery frame and, bending down, kissed the forehead of his daughter﻿—his English daughter. No issue of a great battle could affect her future. As to the other girl, she was lost to him and it couldn’t be helped. A battle had destroyed the fairness of her life. This was the disadvantage of having been born French or indeed belonging to any other nation of the continent. There were forces there that pushed people to rash or unseemly actions; actions that seemed dictated by despair and therefore wore an immoral aspect. Sir Charles understood Adèle d’Armand even better than he understood his own daughter, or at least he understood her with greater sympathy. She had a generous nature. She was too young, too inexperienced to know what she was doing when she took in hand the disposal of her own person in favour of that apparently Piedmontese upstart with his obscure name and his mysteriously acquired fortune. “I only hope the fortune is there,” thought Sir Charles with grim scepticism. But as to that there could be no doubt, judging from the further letters he received from his old friend. After a short but brilliant period of London life the upstart had carried Adèle off to France. He had bought an estate in Piedmont, which was his native country, and another with a splendid house, near Paris. Sir Charles was not surprised to hear a little later that the Marquise and the Marquis had also returned to France. The time of persecution was over; most of the great royalist families were returning, unreconciled in sentiment if wavering in their purposes. That his old friend should ever be dazzled by imperial grandeurs Sir Charles could not believe. Though he had abandoned his daughter to an upstart, he was too good a royalist to abandon his principles, for which certainly he would have died if that had been of any use. But he had returned to France. Most of his exiled friends had returned too, and Sir Charles understood very well that the Marquis and his wife wanted to be somewhere near their daughter. This departure closed a long chapter in his life, and afterwards Sir Charles hardly ever mentioned his French friends. The only positive thing which Henrietta knew was that Adèle d’Armand had married an upstart and had returned to France. She had communicated that knowledge to her brother, who had stared with evident surprise but had made no comment. Living away from home at school and afterwards in Cambridge, his father’s French friends had remained for him as shadowy figures on the shifting background of a very poignant, very real, and intense drama of contemporary history, dominated by one enormously vital and in its greatness immensely mysterious individuality﻿—the only man of his time.

===Chinese===
莱瑟姆小姐抬起那双明亮的深色眼睛，带着探询的神情，低唤了一声：「爸爸？」

查尔斯爵士站起身来，抓起手杖。「没什么，亲爱的，没什么。」他想独处。可走出房间时，他在绣花架旁停下，弯下腰，亲吻了女儿的额头——他的英国女儿。任何一场大战的胜负都无从影响她的未来。至于另一个姑娘，她已从他这里失去了，且无可挽回。一场战役毁了她一生的美好。这便是生为法国人、或竟是大陆上任何一个别国的人，所带的缺陷。那里有着种种力量，推着人去做鲁莽或失当的举动；那些举动看似由绝望所驱使，因而带着一层不道德的色彩。查尔斯爵士对阿黛勒·达蒙的理解，甚至胜过对他自己女儿的理解；至少，他是怀着更深的同情去理解她的。她天性宽厚。她太年轻、太没有阅历，竟自己着手安排自己的终身，托付给那个表面上皮埃蒙特出身的暴发户，和他那个晦暗的姓氏、那笔来路不明的财产——她当时实在不知自己在做什么。「我只盼那笔财产当真在那里，」查尔斯爵士带着冷酷的怀疑想。不过这一点倒无可怀疑——这从他后来收到的老友的几封信里便可看出。在伦敦度过了一段短暂却辉煌的社交生活之后，那暴发户便带着阿黛勒去了法国。他在故土皮埃蒙特买下一处产业，又在巴黎近郊置了另一处，带一座富丽堂皇的宅邸。不久之后，查尔斯爵士听说侯爵夫人和侯爵也回到了法国，并不感到惊讶。迫害的时代已经过去；大多数显赫的保皇党家族都已归来——感情上虽未妥协，意志上却已动摇。要他的老友被帝国的煊赫所眩惑，查尔斯爵士是绝不相信的。他虽把女儿交给了一个暴发户，却是个太好的保皇党，绝不会抛弃自己的原则——为了那些原则，倘若死能有所助益，他定然死而无憾。可他终究回到了法国。他大多数流亡的朋友也都回来了；查尔斯爵士心里很明白，侯爵夫妇想住得离女儿近些。这一别，便结束了他人生中长长的一章；此后，查尔斯爵士几乎不再提起他的那些法国朋友。亨利艾塔所确切知道的，只有一件事：阿黛勒·达蒙嫁给了一个暴发户，并且回了法国。她把这一消息告诉了哥哥，哥哥显见地吃了一惊，却未作任何评论。他自幼离家求学，先在学校、后至剑桥（Cambridge），父亲的那些法国朋友在他眼里，始终只是那个惊心动魄、真实而激烈的当代历史大戏的变幻背景上的几个模糊身影——那出大戏由一个生命力强盛无比、在其伟大之中又神秘莫测的人物所主宰——那个时代独一无二的人。

===Original===
Cosmo Latham at the threshold of life had adopted neither of the contrasted views of Napoleon Emperor entertained by his contemporaries. For him as for his father before him, the world offered a scene of conflicting emotions in which facts appraised by reason preserved a mysterious complexity and a dual character. One evening during an artless discussion with young men of his own age, it had occurred to him to say that Bonaparte seemed to be the only man amongst a lot of old scarecrows. “Look how he knocks them over,” he had explained. A moment of silence followed. Then a voice objected:

“Then perhaps he is not so great as some of you try to make him out.”

“I didn’t mean that exactly,” said Cosmo in a sobered tone. “Nobody can admire that man more than I do. Perhaps the world may be none the worse for a scarecrow here and there left on the borders of what is right or just. I only wished to express my sense of the moving force in his genius.”

“What does he stand for?” asked the same voice.

Cosmo shook his head. “Many things, and some of them too obvious to mention. But I can’t help thinking that there are some which we cannot see yet.”

“And some of them that are dead already,” retorted his interlocutor. “They died in his very hands. But there is one thing for which he stands and that will never die. You seem to have forgotten it. It is the spirit of hostility to this nation; to what we in this room, with our different views and opinions, stand for in the last instance.”

“Oh, that!” said Cosmo confidently. “What we stand for isn’t an old scarecrow. Great as he is he will never knock that over. His arm is not long enough, however far his thoughts may go. He has got to work with common men.”

“I don’t know what you mean. What else are we? I believe you admire him.”

“I do,” confessed Cosmo sturdily.

===Chinese===
科斯莫·莱瑟姆（Cosmo Latham）站在人生的门槛上，并未采纳同代人对皇帝拿破仑所持的两种截然对立的看法中的任何一种。在他看来——一如他父亲在他之前那样——世界呈现的是一幕情感彼此冲突的场景，在其中，凭理性去估量的事实，依然保留着一种神秘的复杂性和双重性格。有一天傍晚，在与几个同龄青年的天真辩论中，他脱口说出，波拿巴（Bonaparte）似乎是一群旧稻草人里头唯一算得上是个人物的人。「瞧他怎样把他们一个个打翻，」他解释道。随之是片刻的沉默。然后一个声音反驳道：

「那么也许他并不像你们中有些人竭力渲染的那样伟大呢。」

「我倒不完全是那个意思，」科斯莫语气收敛了些说，「没有人比我更钦佩那个人了。也许，在是与非、公与不公的边沿上，这里那里留着几个稻草人，对这世界并无损害。我不过是想说说，我所感到的他那天才之中那股推动的力量。」

「他所代表的又是什么？」同一个声音问。

科斯莫摇了摇头。「许多东西，其中有些太过明显，不消细说。但我忍不住想，还有些是我们眼下还看不见的。」

「还有些却早已死了，」对方反驳道，「就死在他自己手里。但有一样是他所代表、并且永远不会死的。你似乎忘了它。那便是对这个国家的敌意；是对我们这间屋子里的人——尽管见解各异——归根结底所代表的东西的敌意。」

「哦，那个！」科斯莫自信地说，「我们所代表的可不是个旧稻草人。他再伟大，也打不翻这个。他的手臂不够长，任他的思想能伸多远。他总得与凡夫俗子共事。」

「我不懂你的意思。我们难道不也是凡夫俗子？我看你是钦佩他的。」

「我是钦佩他，」科斯莫坦然承认。

===Original===
This did not prevent him from joining the army in Spain before the year was out, and that without asking for Sir Charles’s approval. Sir Charles condemned severely the policy of using the forces of the Crown in the Peninsula. He did not like the ministry of the day and he had a strong prejudice against all the Wellesleys to whose aggrandizement this whole policy seemed effected. But when at the end of a year and a half, after the final victory of Toulouse, his son appeared in Yorkshire, the two made up for the past coolness by shaking hands warmly for nearly a whole minute. Cosmo really had done very little campaigning and soon declared to his father the wish to leave the army. There would be no more fighting for years and years, he argued, and though he did not dislike fighting in a good cause, he had no taste for mere soldiering. He wanted to see something of the world which had been closed to us for so long. Sir Charles, ageing and dignified, leaned on his stick on the long terrace.

“All the world was never closed to us,” he said.

“I wasn’t thinking of the East, sir,” explained Cosmo. “I heard some people talk about its mystery, but I think Europe is mysterious enough just now, and even more interesting.”

Sir Charles nodded his bare gray head in the chill evening breeze.

“France, Germany,” he murmured.

Cosmo thought that he would prefer to see something of Italy first. He would go north afterwards.

“Through Vienna, I suppose,” suggested Sir Charles with an impassive face.

“I don’t think so, sir,” said Cosmo frankly. “I don’t care much for the work which is going on there and perhaps still less for the men who are putting their hands to it.”

This time Sir Charles’s slow nod expressed complete agreement. He too had no liking for the work that was about to begin there. But no objection could be raised against Italy. He had known Italy well thirty or more years ago, but it must have been changed out of his knowledge. He remained silent, gazing at the wide landscape of blue wooded rises and dark hollows under the gorgeous colours of the sunset. They began to die out.

“You may travel far before you see anything like this,” he observed to his son. “And don’t be in a hurry to leave us. You have only just come home. Remember I am well over sixty.”

===Chinese===
这并不妨碍他在那年尚未过完之前便投军去了西班牙，而且并未征得查尔斯爵士的允准。查尔斯爵士严厉谴责了将王军用于半岛的政策。他不喜欢当朝的内阁，对所有韦尔斯利一家（the Wellesleys）抱有强烈的成见——整套政策似乎正是为了扩张他们一门的势力而施行的。可是一年半之后，在图卢兹（Toulouse）的最终胜利之后，儿子回到约克郡（Yorkshire）时，父子俩热烈地握着手，握了将近整整一分钟，弥补了先前的疏淡。科斯莫其实并未经历多少战阵，不久便向父亲表示想要离开军队。他陈说道，往后许多年都不会再有战事了；虽然他并不厌恶为正当的事业而战，却对纯粹的行伍生涯毫无兴趣。他想看一看那个长期以来对我们关闭的外部世界。查尔斯爵士年事已高、气度端严，拄着手杖立在长长的露台上。

「整个世界，从未对我们关闭过，」他说。

「我指的不是东方，父亲，」科斯莫解释道，「我听人谈起过东方的神秘，可我觉得，眼下的欧洲已经够神秘了，甚至更有意思。」

查尔斯爵士在那傍晚寒凉的微风里点了点他那灰白的裸头。

「法兰西，德意志，」他低声道。

科斯莫心想，他倒宁愿先看一看意大利。之后再北上也未迟。

「取道维也纳吧，我想，」查尔斯爵士面无表情地提议。

「我不这么想，父亲，」科斯莫坦率地说，「我不大喜欢那边正在进行的勾当，或许更不喜欢在那里插手其事的那些人。」

这一回，查尔斯爵士那缓缓的一点头，表达了完全的赞同。他自己对那边即将开始的勾当也无好感。但意大利却无可指摘。三十多年前他对意大利了如指掌，可如今它必定已变得他再认不得了。他默然不语，凝望着夕阳绚烂的色彩下那一片广阔的景色——林木葱茏的蓝色冈峦与幽暗的凹谷。那些色彩开始渐渐消隐。

「你或许要走得远远的，才能再见到这样的景色，」他对儿子说，「还有，别急着离开我们。你才刚到家。记着我已是年过六十的人了。」

===Original===
Cosmo was quite ready to surrender himself to the peace of his Yorkshire home, so different from the strenuous atmosphere of the last campaign in the South of France. Autumn was well advanced before he fixed the day for his departure. On his last day at home Sir Charles addressed him with perfect calmness.

“When you pass into Italy you must not fail to see my old friend the Marquis d’Armand. The French King has appointed him as ambassador in Turin. It’s a sign of high favour, I believe. He will be either in Turin or Genoa.﻿ ﻿…” Sir Charles paused, then after a perfectly audible sigh added with an effort: “The Marquise is dead. I knew her in her youth. She was a marvellous woman.﻿ ﻿…” Sir Charles checked himself, and then with another effort, “But the daughter of my old friend is I believe with her father now, a married daughter, the Countess of Montevesso.”

“You mean little Adèle, sir,” said Cosmo, with interest, but on Sir Charles’s face there passed a distinct shade of distress.

“Oh, you remember the child,” he said, and his tone was tender but it changed to contempt as he went on. “I don’t know whether the fellow, I mean the man she married, is staying with them or whether they are living with him, or whether﻿ ﻿… I know nothing!”

The word “upstart,” heard many years ago from his sister Henrietta, crossed Cosmo’s mind. He thought to himself, “There is something wrong there,” and to his father he said, “I will be able to tell you all about it.”

“I don’t want to know,” Sir Charles replied with a surprising solemnity of tone and manner which hid some deeper feeling. “But give the Marquis my love and tell him that when he gets tired of all his grandeurs he may remember that there is a large place for him in this house as long as I live.”

===Chinese===
科斯莫倒十分愿意把自己交给约克郡故居的宁静——那与法国南部最后一役紧张气氛截然不同的宁静。直到深秋，他才定下启程的日子。临行前一日在家里，查尔斯爵士以极度的平静对他开口了。

「你进入意大利之后，务必去看看我的老友达蒙侯爵（Marquis d’Armand）。法兰西国王已任命他为驻都灵（Turin）大使。我相信这是格外恩宠的表示。他不是在都灵，便是在热那亚（Genoa）……」查尔斯爵士停了停，随后一声清晰可闻的叹息，又费力地加了一句：「侯爵夫人已经去世了。我认识她年轻的时候。她是个了不起的女人……」查尔斯爵士顿住了，随后又费了番力气：「不过我那老友的女儿，我相信此刻是跟着她父亲的——一个已经出阁的女儿，蒙特韦索伯爵夫人（the Countess of Montevesso）。」

「您是说小阿黛勒吧，父亲，」科斯莫颇感兴趣地说，可查尔斯爵士脸上却明显掠过一层不安的神色。

「哦，你还记得那孩子，」他说，语气温柔，可接下去便转为轻蔑，「我不知道那家伙——我是说她嫁的那个男人——是和他们住在一起呢，还是他们和他住在一起，抑或是……我什么都不知道！」

多年前从妹妹亨利艾塔那里听来的那个词——「暴发户」——掠过科斯莫的心头。他暗自想：「那里头有些不对。」他对父亲说：「我会把一切都告诉您的。」

「我不想知道，」查尔斯爵士答道，语气与态度中带着一种出人意料的郑重，那下面藏着某种更深的情感，「不过替我向侯爵致意，告诉他：等他把那些荣华富贵都过腻了，也许会记起，只要我活着，这宅子里总有他一个宽大的位置。」

===Original===
Late that evening Cosmo, saying goodbye to his sister, took her in his arms, kissed her forehead, and holding her out at arm’s length said:

“You have grown into a charming girl, Henrietta.”

“I am glad you think so,” she said. “Alas, I am too dark. I can never be as charming as Adèle must have been at my age. You seem to have forgotten her.”

“Oh no,” protested Cosmo carelessly. “A marvel of fairness, wasn’t she? I remember you telling me years ago that she married an upstart.”

“That was Father’s expression. You know what that means, Cosmo.”

“I do know what it means, exactly,” he said, laughing. “But from what Father said this afternoon it seems as if he were a rather nasty upstart. What made Adèle do it?”

“I am awed,” confessed Henrietta. “I don’t know what made her do it. I was never told. Father never talked much about the D’Armands afterwards. I was with him in the yellow drawing room the evening he got the letter from the Marquis. After he read it he said something very extraordinary. You know it’s full nine years ago and I was yet a child, yet I could not have dreamed it. I heard it distinctly. He dropped his hands and said, ‘Austerlitz has done it.’ What could he have meant?”

“It would be hard to guess the connection,” said Cosmo, smiling at his sister’s puzzled face. “Father must have been thinking of something else.”

“Father was thinking of nothing else for days,” affirmed Henrietta positively.

“You must have been a very observant child,” remarked her brother. “But I believe you were always a clever girl, Henrietta. Well, I am going to see Adèle.”

“Oh yes, you start in the morning to travel ever so far and for ever so long,” said Miss Latham enviously. “Oh Cosmo, you are going to write to me﻿—lots?”

He looked at her appreciatively and gave her another brotherly hug.

“Certainly I will write, whole reams,” he said.

===Chinese===
那天深夜，科斯莫向妹妹道别，把她拥进怀里，吻了她的额头，又把她伸直手臂推开些，说道：

「你长成一个迷人的姑娘了，亨利艾塔。」

「你这么想，我真高兴，」她说，「哎，只可惜我肤色太深了。我永远也比不上阿黛勒在我这年纪时该有的那般迷人。你似乎把她忘了。」

「哦，没有，」科斯莫漫不经心地分辩道，「一个白皙得惊人的奇迹，不是么？我记得你多年前告诉过我，她嫁了个暴发户。」

「那是父亲的措辞。你明白那意味着什么，科斯莫。」

「我当然明白，一清二楚，」他笑着说，「不过照父亲今天下午所说，那人似乎是个相当可恶的暴发户。阿黛勒怎么会做出这种事来呢？」

「我也为之骇然，」亨利艾塔承认道，「我不知道她为何如此。从来没人告诉过我。父亲此后便不大谈起达蒙一家了。侯爵那封信送到的那个傍晚，我正和他一起在那间黄色客厅里。他读完信后说了一句极为古怪的话。你知道，那已是足足九年之前，我还是个孩子，可我绝不可能做梦编出这种话来。我听得清清楚楚。他放下双手，说道：『是奥斯特里茨造成的。』他会是什么意思呢？」

「这其中的关联，恐怕很难猜，」科斯莫说，对着妹妹那张困惑的脸笑了笑，「父亲当时一定是在想别的事情。」

「连着好几天，父亲想的全是这件事，」亨利艾塔肯定地断言。

「你当年一定是个极善于观察的孩子，」哥哥说道，「不过我一向相信你是个聪明的姑娘，亨利艾塔。好吧，我要去看看阿黛勒了。」

「哦，是了，你一早就要启程，走得远远的，一去就是好长好长的时日，」莱瑟姆小姐羡慕地说，「哦，科斯莫，你会给我写信的——写好多好多吧？」

他含着欣赏之情看了她一眼，又给了她一个兄长式的拥抱。

「我当然会写，整捆整捆地写，」他说。

### IV / IV

===Original===
On his way from the harbour to the upper part of the town where his inn was situated Cosmo Latham met very few people. He had to pass through a sort of covered way; its arch yawned in front of him very black with only a feeble glimmer of a light in its depths. It did not occur to him that it was a place where one could very well be knocked on the head by evil-intentioned men if there were any prowling about in that early part of the evening, for it was early yet, though the last gleams of sunset had gone out completely off the earth and out of the sky. On issuing from the dark passage a maze of narrow streets presented itself to his choice, but he knew that as long as he kept walking uphill he could not fail to reach the middle of the town. Projecting at long intervals from the continuous mass of thick walls, wrought-iron arms held lanterns containing dim gleams of light. The enormous doors of the lofty gateways he passed were closed, and the only sound he could hear was that of his own deliberate footsteps. At a wider spot where several of those lanes met he stopped, and looking about him asked himself whether all those enormous and palatial houses were empty, or whether it was the thickness of walls that killed all the signs of life within; for as to the population being already asleep he could not believe it for a moment. All at once he caught sight of a muffled feminine form. In the heavy shadow she seemed to emerge out of one wall and gliding on seemed to disappear into another. It was undoubtedly a woman. Cosmo was startled by this noiseless apparition and had a momentary feeling of being lost in an enchanted city. Presently the enchanted silence was broken by the increasing sound of an iron-shod stick tapping the flagstones, till there walked out of one of the dark and tortuous lanes a man who by his rolling gait, general outline, and the characteristic shape of the hat, Cosmo could not doubt, was a seaman belonging to the English man-of-war in the harbour. The tapping of his stick ceased suddenly and Cosmo hailed him in English, asking for the way.

The sturdy figure in the tarpaulin hat put its cudgel under its arm and answered him in a deep pleasant voice. Yes, he knew the inn. He was just coming from there. If His Honour followed the street before him he would come to a large open space and His Honour’s inn would be across the square. In the deep shadows Cosmo could make out of the seaman’s face nothing but the bushy whiskers and the gleam of the eyes. He was pleased at meeting the very day he had reached the Mediterranean shore (he had come down to Genoa from Turin) such a fine specimen of a man-of-war’s man. He thanked him for the direction and the sailor, touching his hat, went off at his slightly rolling gait. Cosmo observed that he took a turning very near the spot where the muffled woman had a moment before vanished from his sight. It was a very dark and a very narrow passage between two towering buildings. Cosmo, continuing on his way, arrived at a broad thoroughfare badly lighted but full of people. He knew where he was then. In a very few moments he found himself at the door of his inn in a great square which in comparison with the rest of the town might have been said to blaze with lights.

===Chinese===
从港口往城上半部他那客栈所在的路上，科斯莫·莱瑟姆几乎没遇上什么人。他得穿过一道带顶的廊道；那拱洞在他面前洞开着，黑黢黢的，只在深处有一星微弱的灯火。他并未想到，这种地方若是有心怀叵测之徒在天色初暗时游荡出没，一个人是很有可能在此吃一记闷棍的——因为天色尚早，尽管落日最后的一抹余晖已从地上、从天上彻底熄灭。出了那段暗廊，一片纵横交错的小街呈现在他眼前任其择路，可他知道，只要一路往上坡走，便准能到得城中心。厚墙连绵不断，每隔好一段才凸出一根熟铁臂膀，托着一盏灯火昏暗的灯笼。他经过的那些高大宅门洞开的门扉都已紧闭，他唯一听得见的声响，只是自己那不疾不徐的脚步声。在几条小巷交汇的一处稍宽的地方，他停下来，四下打量，心里纳闷：这一座座硕大而宫阙似的宅子，到底是空无人居呢，还是那厚墙把里头一切人烟的声息都闷死了？因为要说居民们已经入睡，他可一刻也不信。蓦地里，他瞥见一个裹得严严的女子身影。在浓重的阴影里，她仿佛从一面墙里钻出来，悄无声息地滑行过去，又消失到另一面墙里去了。那无疑是个女人。科斯莫被这无声无息的出现惊了一惊，有一刹那竟觉得自己是迷失在一座中了魔的城市里。随即，那中了魔似的寂静被一阵越来越响的、铁包头的拐杖敲击石板路的声音打破——从一条幽暗曲折的小巷里走出一个男人。凭他那略带摇晃的步态、大致的轮廓，还有那顶帽子的独特式样，科斯莫毫不怀疑，他是港里那艘英国军舰上的水手。那拐杖的敲击声戛然而止，科斯莫用英语招呼他，向他问路。

那个戴着油布帽的壮实身影把短棍往腋下一夹，用低沉悦耳的声音答起话来。是的，他认得那家客栈。他正是打那里来。要是这位老爷顺着眼前这条街往前走，便会走到一片开阔的大空场，老爷要找的那家客栈就在广场对面。在浓重的暗影里，科斯莫只能辨出那水手脸上浓密的络腮胡子和一双闪闪发亮的眼睛。他到达地中海（Mediterranean）岸边（他是从都灵南下热那亚的）的头一天，便遇上了军舰水手中这样出色的一号人物，心里颇为欢喜。他道了谢，那水手触了触帽檐，迈着略带摇晃的步子走开了。科斯莫留意到，他拐了个弯，那转弯处离方才那裹披风的女子从他眼前消失的地方极近。那是两座高楼之间一条极暗、极窄的夹道。科斯莫继续赶路，来到一条灯火稀暗却满是行人的宽阔大街上。他这才明白自己到了何处。不消片刻，他便走到了自己客栈的门口——那是在一个大广场上，与城里其余地方相比，简直可以说是灯火通明了。

===Original===
Under an iron lantern swung above a flight of three broad steps, Cosmo recognized his servant gazing into the square with a worried expression which changed at once into one of relief on perceiving his master. He touched his cap and followed Cosmo into a large hall with several doors opening into it and furnished with many wooden chairs and tables. At one of them bearing four candlesticks several British naval officers sat talking and laughing in subdued tones. A compactly built clean-shaven person with slightly sunken cheeks, wearing black breeches and a maroon waistcoat with sleeves, but displaying a very elaborate frill to his white shirt, stood in the middle of the floor, glancing about with vigilance, and bowed hurriedly to his latest client. Cosmo returned the greeting of Signor Cantelucci, who, snatching up the nearest candlestick, began to ascend a broad stone staircase with an air of performing a solemn duty. Cosmo followed him, and Cosmo’s servant followed his master. They went up and up. At every flight broad archways gave a view of dark perspective in which nothing but a few drops of dim fire were forlornly visible. At last Signor Cantelucci threw open a door on a landing and bowing again:

“See, milord! There is a fire. I know the customs and habits of the English.”

Cosmo stepped into a large and lofty room where in the play of bright flames under a heavy and tall mantelpiece the shadows seemed very much disturbed by his entrance. Cosmo approached the blaze with satisfaction.

“I had enough trouble to get them to light it,” remarked the valet in a resentful tone. “If it hadn’t been for a jack-tar with big whiskers I found down in the hall it wouldn’t be done yet. He came up from the ship with one of these sea officers downstairs. He drove the fellows with the wood in fine style up here for me. He knows the people here. He cursed them each separately by their Christian names, and then had a glass of wine in the kitchen with me.”

===Chinese===
一道三级宽台阶上方悬着一盏铁灯笼，灯笼下，科斯莫认出了自己的仆人正忧心忡忡地朝广场上张望；一瞧见主人，那神色顿时化为释然。他触了触帽，跟着科斯莫走进一间宽敞的大堂——堂里开着好几道门，摆着许多木椅木桌。其中一张桌上立着四支烛台，几位英国海军军官正坐着，低声谈笑。一个身材敦实、两颊微陷、刮净了脸的人，穿着黑色马裤和一件带袖的栗色马甲，白衬衫前却翻出一道极考究的褶边，立在屋子当中，机警地四下打量着，见新客到，匆忙一躬身。科斯莫回过了坎泰洛奇先生（Signor Cantelucci）的礼；后者一把抓起最近的一支烛台，便开始登上一道宽阔的石阶，那神情仿佛在履行一桩庄严的职责。科斯莫跟在他身后，科斯莫的仆人又跟在主人身后。他们一程又一程地往上走。每一层楼梯口都有宽阔的拱门洞，望进去是幽暗的纵深，里面只孤零零地看得见几点昏黄的灯火。终于，坎泰洛奇先生在一段楼梯口推开一扇门，再次一躬身：

「您瞧，老爷！火已经生好了。我懂得英国人的规矩和习惯。」

科斯莫跨进一间又大又高的屋子，高大的壁炉下，明亮的火苗正跳荡着，他这一进来，那满屋的阴影似乎都被大大地搅动了。科斯莫满意地走到炉火前。

「我费了好大的劲才叫他们把火生起来，」贴身男仆带着怨气道，「要不是我在楼下大厅里撞见一个留着大络腮胡子的水手，这会儿还没生上呢。他是跟着楼下哪位海军军官一块儿从船上来的。他可真有本事，连喝带骂地替我把那帮搬木头的家伙赶到楼上来了。他认得这儿的人。他把他们一个个按着教名骂了个遍，完了还跟我一道在厨房里喝了一杯酒。」

===Original===
Meantime Signor Cantelucci, wearing the aspect of a deaf man, had lighted, on two separate tables, two clusters of candles which drove the restless gloom of the large apartment halfway up to the ceiling, and retired with noiseless footsteps. He stopped in the doorway to cast a keen glance at the master and the man standing by the fire. Those two turned their heads only at the sound of the closing door.

“I couldn’t think what became of you, sir. I was getting quite worried about you. You disappeared without saying anything to me.”

“I went for a walk down to the sea,” said Cosmo while the man moved off to where several cowhide trunks were ranged against the wall. “I like to take a look round on arriving at a new place.”

“Yes, sir; but when it got dark I wondered.”

“I tarried on a tower to watch the sunset,” murmured Cosmo.

“I have been doing some unpacking,” said the servant, “but not knowing how long you mean to stay﻿ ﻿…”

“It may be a long stay.”

“Then I will go on, sir; that is if you are going to keep this room.”

“Yes. The room will do, Spire. It’s big enough.”

Spire took up one of the two candelabras and retired into the neighbourhood of a sort of state bed heavily draped at the other end of the room. There, throwing open the trunks and the doors of closets, he busied himself systematically, without noise, till he heard the quiet voice of his young master.

“Spire.”

“Yes, sir,” he answered, standing still with a pile of shirts on his arm.

“Is this inn very full?”

“Yes, very,” said Spire. “The whole town is full of travellers and people from the country. A lot of our nobility and gentry are passing this way.”

He deposited the shirts on a shelf in the depths of the wall and turned round again.

“Have you heard any names, Spire?”

Spire stooped over a trunk and lifted up from it carefully a lot of white neckcloths folded neatly one within the other.

“I haven’t had much time yet, sir. I heard a few.”

===Chinese===
这当儿，坎泰洛奇先生做出一副耳聋之人的模样，在两张桌子上分别点起两簇蜡烛，把这间大屋里那躁动不安的幽暗驱赶到天花板半腰处，然后便蹑手蹑脚地退了出去。他在门口停了一停，朝炉前站着的主仆二人投去一道锐利的目光。那二人直到门关上的声响传来，才回过头去。

「我都不知道您上哪儿去了，老爷。我都快急坏了。您一句话不说就没影了。」

「我下海边去散了散步，」科斯莫说，那仆人则挪到墙边几口牛皮箱排列的地方。「我每到一个新地方，总爱四处张望一番。」

「是，老爷；可天一黑我就犯了嘀咕。」

「我在一座塔楼上耽了一会儿，看日落，」科斯莫低声说。

「我已经开了几口箱，」仆人说，「可不知道您打算住多久……」

「也许要住得久些。」

「那我就接着收拾，老爷；这自然是您要留下这间房了。」

「是的。这间房可以，斯派尔（Spire）。够大了。」

斯派尔拿起两座烛台中的一座，挪到屋子另一头一张挂着厚重帷幔、颇有几分气派的床边。他在那儿打开箱子和壁橱的门，有条不紊、悄无声息地忙活起来，直到听见少东家平静的声音。

「斯派尔。」

「在，老爷，」他答道，手臂上搭着一摞衬衫站住了。

「这客栈住得满么？」

「满，很满，」斯派尔说，「全城都住满了旅客和从乡间来的人。咱们有好多贵族和乡绅都打这儿过。」

他把衬衫放到墙深处的一层搁板上，又转过身来。

「你听到什么名字了么，斯派尔？」

斯派尔俯身到一口箱子前，小心翼翼地从里头捧出一沓白颈巾——一条套一条地叠得齐齐整整。

「我还没多少工夫呢，老爷。听见了几个人名。」

===Original===
He laid down the neckcloths by the side of the shirts while Cosmo, with his elbow on the mantelpiece, asked down the whole length of the room:

“Anybody I know?”

“Not in this place, sir. There is generally a party of officers from the man-of-war staying here. They come and go. I have seen some Italian gentlemen in square-cut coats and powdered hair. Very old-fashioned, sir. There are some Austrians too, I think; but I haven’t seen any ladies.﻿ ﻿… I am afraid, sir, this isn’t the right sort of inn. There is another about a hundred yards from here on the other side of the square.”

“I don’t want to meet anybody I know,” said Cosmo Latham in a low voice.

Spire thought that this would make his stay in Genoa very dull. At the same time he was convinced that his young master would alter his mind before very long and change to that other inn patronized by travellers of fashion. For himself he was not averse from a little quiet time. Spire was no longer young. Thirty years ago, before the War and before the Revolution, he had travelled with Sir Charles in France and Italy. He was then only eighteen, but being a steady and trustworthy lad was taken abroad to look after the horses. Sir Charles kept four horses in Florence, and Spire had often ridden on Tuscan roads behind Sir Charles and the two Misses Aston, of whom one later became Lady Latham. After the family settled in Yorkshire he passed from the stables to the house, acquired a confidential position, and whenever Lady Latham took a journey he sat in the rumble with a pair of double-barrelled pistols in the pockets of his greatcoat and ordered all things on the road. Later he became intermediary between Sir Charles and the stables, the gardens, and in all out-of-door things about the house. He attended Lady Latham on her very last drive, all the details for that lady’s funeral having been left to his management. He was also a very good valet. He had been called one evening into the library where Sir Charles, very gouty that day, leaning with one hand on a thick stick and with the other on the edge of a table, had said to him: “I am lending you to Mr. Cosmo for his travels in France and Italy. You will know your way about. And mind you draw the charges of the pistols in the carriage every morning and load them afresh.”

===Chinese===
他把那沓颈巾放在衬衫旁，科斯莫则肘支在壁炉架上，隔着整间屋子的长度问过来：

「有我认识的人么？」

「这地方没有，老爷。通常总有一拨军舰上的军官住在这儿。他们来来去去的。我瞧见几位意大利先生，穿着方下摆的外套，扑着发粉。很是老派呢，老爷。我想还有几位奥地利人；可我没瞧见什么太太小姐。……我怕，老爷，这客栈不大对您的路。广场那边，离这儿百来码远，还有一家。」

「我不想碰见任何熟人，」科斯莫·莱瑟姆低声说。

斯派尔心想，这样一来，他在热那亚的日子可就太冷清了。可同时他又深信，少东家过不了多久就会改变主意，搬到那家有时髦旅客光顾的客栈去。至于他自己，倒并不排斥清静一段时日。斯派尔已不再年轻。三十年前，打仗之前、革命之前，他曾随查尔斯爵士游历法兰西与意大利。那时他不过十八岁，却因为是个沉稳可靠的小伙子，被带出国去看管马匹。查尔斯爵士在佛罗伦萨（Florence）养着四匹马，斯派尔常骑着马，在托斯卡纳（Tuscan）的路上跟在查尔斯爵士和阿斯顿家两位小姐（the two Misses Aston）后面——其中一位后来便成了莱瑟姆夫人（Lady Latham）。一家在约克郡安顿下来之后，他便从马厩调入内宅，得了个亲信的职位；莱瑟姆夫人但凡出门远行，他便坐在车后的跟座上，大衣口袋里揣着一双双管手枪，一路上发号施令、照管一切。后来他又成了查尔斯爵士与马厩、花园以及宅中一切户外事务之间的居间人。莱瑟姆夫人最后一次乘车出门，便是他随侍在侧的；那位夫人丧礼上的一切细节，都交由他来料理。他也是个极称职的贴身男仆。有一天傍晚，他被叫进书房，查尔斯爵士那天痛风犯得很厉害，一只手拄着粗手杖，另一只手撑着桌沿，对他说：「我把你借给科斯莫少爷，随他去法兰西和意大利游历。这些路你熟。记着每天早上把车里的手枪退了膛，重新装填过。」

===Original===
Spire was then requested to help Sir Charles up the stairs and had a few more words said to him when Sir Charles stopped at the door of his bedroom.

“Mr. Cosmo has plenty of sense. You are not to make yourself a nuisance to him.”

“No, Sir Charles,” said the imperturbable Spire. “I will know how to look after Mr. Cosmo.”

And if he had been asked, Spire would have been able to say that during the stay in Paris and all through France and Switzerland on the way to Genoa Mr. Cosmo had given him no trouble at all.

Spire, still busy unpacking, glanced at his young master. He certainly looked very quiet now, leaning on his elbow with the firelight playing from below on his young thoughtful face with its smooth and pale complexion. “Very good-looking indeed,” thought Spire. In that thoughtful mood he recalled very much the Sir Charles of thirty or thirty-five years ago. Would he too find his wife abroad? There had been women enough in Paris of every kind and degree, English and French and all sorts. But it was a fact that Mr. Cosmo sought most of his company amongst men, of whom also there had been no lack and of every degree. In that, too, the young man resembled very much his father. Men’s company. But were he to get caught he would get caught properly; at any rate for a time, reflected Spire, remembering Sir Charles Latham’s rush back to Italy, the inwardness of which had been no more revealed to him than to the rest of the world.

Spire, approaching the candelabra, unfolded partly a very fine coat, then refolded it before putting it away on a convenient shelf. He had a moment of regret for his own young days. He had never married, not because there had been any lack of women to set their caps at him, but from a sort of half-conscious prudence. Moreover, he had a notion somehow or other that Sir Charles would not have liked it. Perhaps it was just as well. Now he was carefree, attending on Mr. Cosmo without troubling his head about who had remained at home.

Spire, arranging the contents of a dressing case on the table, cast another sidelong look at the figure by the fire. Very handsome. Something like Sir Charles and yet not like. There was a touch of something unusual, perhaps foreign, and yet no one with a pair of eyes in his head could mistake Mr. Cosmo for anything but an English gentleman.

Spire’s memories of his tour with Sir Charles had been growing dim. But he remembered enough of the old-time atmosphere to have become aware of a feeling of tension, of a suggestion of restlessness which certainly was new to him.

===Chinese===
随后斯派尔被叫去搀查尔斯爵士上楼，在查尔斯爵士于自己卧室门口停步时，又吩咐了他几句。

「科斯莫少爷是个很有分寸的人。你别去招他嫌。」

「是，查尔斯爵士，」处变不惊的斯派尔说，「我会知道怎样照料科斯莫少爷的。」

倘若有人问他，斯派尔定能答得上：从在巴黎盘桓，到取道法兰西全境、瑞士（Switzerland）一路前来热那亚，科斯莫少爷一路上从未给他添过半点麻烦。

斯派尔一面继续忙着开箱，一面瞧了少东家一眼。他此刻果然十分安静，肘支着，炉火从下方映上他年轻而若有所思的脸，那张脸肤色光洁白净。「当真生得很好看呢，」斯派尔心想。这般沉思的模样，倒极像三十或三十五年前的查尔斯爵士。他也会在国外觅得自己的妻子么？巴黎城里什么品类的女人都有，英国的、法国的，各式各样，不一而足。可事实是，科斯莫少爷多半更爱与男子为伍——而男子也并不缺，各色人等都有。在这一点上，这年轻人又极像他父亲。爱与男人为伍。可他一旦被套牢，便会套得结结实实；至少也得有一阵子吧——斯派尔这样想着，记起了查尔斯爵士当年匆匆赶回意大利那一桩，其中缘故，并不曾对他披露半分，正如不曾对世人披露半分一样。

斯派尔走到烛台前，把一件极好的外套展开了一半，又重新折好，才放到顺手的一层搁板上。他有一时怀念起自己的青春岁月。他终身未娶——倒不是因为缺了向他抛媚眼的女人，而是出于一种半自觉的审慎。再者，他不知怎的总觉得查尔斯爵士不会赞成这事。也许这样反倒好。如今他无牵无挂，侍奉科斯莫少爷，不必为家里留下了谁而劳神。

斯派尔把一只梳妆盒里的物什在桌上摆开，又斜眼瞟了一下炉边那个身影。很是俊美。有些像查尔斯爵士，却又不像。有一丝异样的、或许是异国的情调；然而任何一个长着两只眼睛的人，都不可能把科斯莫少爷错看成英国绅士之外的任何什么人。

斯派尔随查尔斯爵士游历的那段记忆，已渐渐模糊。可旧日的那种气氛，他还记得不少，足以使他觉察到一种紧张之感、一种浮躁的暗示——这于他而言，无疑是新鲜的。

===Original===
The silence had lasted very long. Cosmo before the fire had not moved. Spire ventured on a remark.

“I notice people are excited about one thing and another hereabouts, sir.”

“Excited. I don’t wonder at it. In what way?”

“Sort of discontented, sir. They don’t like the Austrians, sir. You may have noticed as we came along﻿ ﻿…”

“Did they like us when we held the town?”

“I can hardly say that, sir. I have been sitting for an hour or more in the couriers’ room, with all sorts of people coming in and out, and heard very wild sort of talk.”

“What can you know about its wildness?”

“To look at their faces was enough. It’s a funny place, that room downstairs,” went on Spire, rubbing with a piece of silk a travelling looking-glass mounted cunningly in a silver case which when opened made a stand for it. He placed it exactly in the middle of a little table and turned round to look at his master. Seeing that Cosmo seemed disposed to listen he continued: “It is vaulted like a cellar and has a little door giving into a side street. People come in and put as they like. All sorts of low people, sir, *facchini* and carters and boatmen and suchlike. There was an old fellow came in, a gray-headed man, a cobbler, I suppose, as he brought a bagful of mended shoes for the servants of the house. He emptied the lot on the stone floor, sir, and instead of trying to collect his money from the people that were scrambling for them he made them a speech. He spouted, sir, without drawing a breath. The courier-valet of an English doctor staying here, a Swiss I think he is, says to me in his broken English: ‘He would cut every Austrian throat in this town.’ We were having a glass of wine together and I asked him, ‘And what do you think of that?’ And he says to me, after thinking a bit, ‘I agree with him.﻿ ﻿…’ Very dreadful, sir,” concluded Spire with a perfectly unmoved face.

Cosmo looked at him in silence for a time. “It was very bold talk if that is what the man really said,” he remarked. “Especially as the place is so public as you say it is.”

===Chinese===
这一阵沉默已延续了许久。炉前的科斯莫一动不动。斯派尔大着胆子开了口。

「我瞧这儿一带的人，为这为那都挺激动的，老爷。」

「激动。这倒不奇怪。怎么个激动法？」

「像是满腹牢骚的样子，老爷。他们不喜欢奥地利人，老爷。咱们一路走来您也许留意到了……」

「我们占着这城的时候，他们喜欢我们么？」

「这我可说不上来，老爷。我在信差屋里坐了一个多钟头，形形色色的人进进出出，听见些很放肆的话。」

「你怎么听得出它放肆？」

「瞧他们的脸色就够啦。楼下那间屋子是个古怪地方，」斯派尔接着说，一面用一块绸子擦着一面旅行镜——那镜子精巧地镶在一只银匣子里，打开来便成了一只座架。他把镜子端端正正摆在一张小桌正中，回过头来看主人。见科斯莫似乎有兴致听，便接着说：「那屋子拱顶像个地窖，有一扇小门通一条小街。人随便进，随便坐。尽是些下等人，老爷，*facchini*（脚夫）啦，赶车的啦，船夫啦，诸如此类。有个老头儿走了进来，头发花白，我想是个皮匠，因为他给这宅子的仆役们送来一口袋补好的鞋。他把鞋一股脑儿倒在石板地上，老爷，也不去向那些争抢着拿鞋的人讨钱，反倒对他们演说起来了。他滔滔不绝，老爷，连口气都不喘。住在这儿的一位英国医生——他的信差兼贴身仆，我想是个瑞士人——用他那口蹩脚英语对我说：『他恨不得把这城里奥地利人的喉咙个个割断。』我们正一道喝着一杯酒，我便问他：『那你自己怎么想呢？』他想了想，对我说：『我赞成他。……』真可怕，老爷，」斯派尔面不改色地作了结论。

科斯莫默然看了他一会儿。「要是那人当真是这么说的，那可是非常大胆的话，」他说，「尤其像你所说，那地方又是这般公开的场所。」

===Original===
“Absolutely open to the street, sir; and that same Swiss fellow had told me just before that the town was full of spies and what they call *sbirri* that came from Turin with the King. The King is staying at the Palace, sir. They are expecting the Queen of Sardinia to arrive any day. You didn’t know, sir? They say she will come in an English man-of-war. That old cobbler was very abusive about the King of Piedmont too. Surely talk like that can’t be safe anywhere.”

Spire paused suddenly and Cosmo Latham turned his back to the fire.

“Well, and what happened?” he asked with a smile.

“You could have heard a pin drop,” said Spire in equable tones, “till that Signor Cantelucci﻿—that’s the padrone of this inn, sir﻿ ﻿…”

“The man who lighted me up?” said Cosmo.

“Yes, sir.﻿ ﻿… I didn’t know he was in the room till suddenly he spoke behind my back telling one of the scullions that was there to give the man a glass of wine. And what the old fellow must do but raise it above his head and shout a toast to the Destructor of the Austrians before he tossed it down his throat. I was quite astonished, but Signor Cantelucci never turned a hair. He offered his snuffbox to that doctor’s courier and myself and shrugged his shoulders. ‘It was only Pietro,’ he said, ‘a little mad’﻿—he tapped his forehead, you know, sir. The doctor’s courier sat there grinning. I got suddenly uneasy about you, sir, and went out to the front door to see whether you were coming. It’s very different from what it was thirty years ago. There was no talk in Italy of cutting foreigners’ throats when Sir Charles and I were here. It was quite a startling experience.”

Cosmo nodded. “You seem impressed, Spire. Well, I too had an experience, just as the sun was setting.”

“I am sorry to hear that, sir.”

“What do you mean? Why should you be sorry?”

“I beg your pardon, sir, I thought it was something unpleasant.”

Cosmo had a little laugh. “Unpleasant? No! Not exactly, though I think it was more dangerous than yours, but if there was any madness connected with it, it had a very visible method. It was not all talk either. Yes, Spire, it was exciting.”

===Chinese===
「简直是对着大街敞开的，老爷；那位瑞士人方才还告诉我，城里全是密探，还有他们叫做*sbirri*（暗探）的，是随国王从都灵来的。国王就下榻在王宫里，老爷。他们正盼着撒丁尼亚王后（Queen of Sardinia）哪天就到呢。您还不知道吧，老爷？据说她要搭乘一艘英国军舰来。那老皮匠对皮埃蒙特国王（King of Piedmont）也骂得很凶。这样的话，无论在哪儿恐怕都不能安然无事吧。」

斯派尔忽然顿住，科斯莫·莱瑟姆转过身去，背对着炉火。

「后来呢？」他含笑问。

「那时静得连根针掉在地上都听得见，」斯派尔不紧不慢地说，「直到那位坎泰洛奇先生——也就是这家客栈的东家，老爷……」

「就是领我上楼的那个人？」科斯莫问。

「是的，老爷。……我先前还不知道他在屋里，直到他忽然在我背后开了口，叫一个在场的帮工去给那人一杯酒。那老头儿非得把酒举过头顶，高呼祝『奥地利人的毁灭者』健康，这才一仰脖子灌下去。我着实吃了一惊，可坎泰洛奇先生连眉毛都没动一下。他把鼻烟盒递给我和那位医生的信差各一份，耸了耸肩。『不过是彼得罗（Pietro），』他说，『有点疯癫』——您知道的，老爷，他敲了敲自己的额头。那位医生的信差坐在那儿只管咧着嘴笑。我突然为您不放心起来，老爷，便走到大门口看您来了没有。这儿跟三十年前可大不一样了。当年查尔斯爵士和我在这儿的时候，意大利可没人说什么要割外国人的喉咙。这桩事可真叫我吃惊不小。」

科斯莫点了点头。「你似乎印象很深呢，斯派尔。嗯，我也有过一段遭遇，就在太阳落山那会儿。」

「听您这么说，我真不安，老爷。」

「你这话怎讲？你为何要不安？」

「恕罪，老爷，我还当是什么不愉快的事呢。」

科斯莫轻轻一笑。「不愉快？不！不完全是。不过我想，我那遭遇比你这桩更危险些；可要是其中有什么疯劲儿的话，那疯劲儿里头却有个清清楚楚的章法。而且也并不光是嘴上说说。是的，斯派尔，挺叫人兴奋。」

===Original===
“I don’t know what’s come to them all. Everybody seems excited. There was not excitement in Italy thirty years ago when I was with Sir Charles and took four horses with only one helper from this very town to Florence, sir.”

Cosmo with fixed eyes did not seem to hear Spire’s complaining remark. He exclaimed: “Really it was very extraordinary,” so suddenly that Spire gave a perceptible start. He pulled himself together and asked in a purely business tone:

“Are you going to dine in your room, sir? Time is getting on.”

Cosmo’s mood too seemed to have changed completely.

“I don’t know. I am not hungry. I want you to move one of those screens here near the fire and place a table and chair there. I will do some correspondence tonight. Yes, I will have my dinner here, I think.”

“I will go down and order it, sir,” said Spire. “The cook here is a Frenchman who married a native and﻿ ﻿…”

“Who on earth is swearing like this outside?” exclaimed Cosmo, while Spire’s face also expressed astonishment at the loud burst of voices coming along the corridor, one angry, the other argumentative, in a crescendo of scolding and expostulation which, passing the door at its highest, died away into a confused murmur in the distance of the long corridor.

“That was an English voice,” said Cosmo. “I mean the angry one.”

“I should think it’s that English doctor from Tuscany that has been three or four days here already. He has been put on this floor.”

“From what I have been able to catch,” said Cosmo, “he seems very angry at having a neighbour on it. That must be me. Have you heard his name?”

“It’s Marvel or some such name. He seems to be known here; he orders people about as if he were at home. The other was Cantelucci, sir.”

“Very likely. Look here, Spire. I will dine in the public room downstairs. I want to see that angry gentleman. Did you see him, Spire?”

“Only his back, sir. Very broad, sir. Tall man. In boots and a riding coat. Are you going down now, sir? The dinner must be on already.”

“Yes,” said Cosmo, preparing to go out. “And by the by, Spire, if you ever see in the street or in that room downstairs, where everyone comes in and out as you say, a long fellow wearing a peculiar cap with a tassel, just try to find out something about him; or at any rate let me know when you have seen him.﻿ ﻿… You could perhaps follow him for a bit and try to see where he goes.”

After saying those words Cosmo left the room before Spire could make any answer. Spire’s astonishment expressed itself by a low exclamation, “Well, I never!”

===Chinese===
「我不懂他们都怎么了。个个都像很激动。三十年前我跟着查尔斯爵士在意大利的时候可没有这种激动；那时我只带一个帮手，赶着四匹马，从这座城直去佛罗伦萨呢，老爷。」

科斯莫两眼发直，似乎没听见斯派尔这番抱怨的话。他忽然喊道：「真的，那实在是非同寻常，」喊得那样突然，斯派尔明显地吃了一惊。他定了定神，用一种纯属公事的口吻问：

「您要在房里用晚餐么，老爷？时候不早了。」

科斯莫的心绪似乎也完全变了。

「我不知道。我不饿。我要你把那扇屏风搬一扇到炉火这边来，再在这儿摆一张桌子、一把椅子。我今晚要写些信。嗯，我想我就在房里用晚饭吧。」

「我下去吩咐，老爷，」斯派尔说，「这儿的厨子是个法兰西人，娶了个本地女人……」

「外头到底是谁这般破口大骂？」科斯莫惊呼道，斯派尔脸上也露出惊讶之色——走廊里传来一阵高声喧嚷，一个声音怒气冲冲，另一个则在争辩，责骂与劝解一浪高过一浪，到了门前正当最高潮，随即在长长的走廊远处化作一阵含混的嗡嗡声渐渐消散。

「那是英国口音，」科斯莫说，「我是说那个发怒的声音。」

「我想是从托斯卡纳来的那位英国医生，已经在这儿住了三四天了。他被安置在这一层。」

「从我听到的几句话来琢磨，」科斯莫说，「他似乎是因为这一层多了个邻居而恼怒。那一定是指我了。你打听到他姓甚名谁了么？」

「叫马弗尔（Marvel）还是什么类似的姓。他在这一带似乎颇有人缘；他支使人就像在自己家里一样。另一个声音是坎泰洛奇，老爷。」

「很可能。我说，斯派尔。我要下楼到公共餐厅去用饭。我想看看那位发怒的先生。你见过他么，斯派尔？」

「只见着他的背影，老爷。背很宽，老爷。高个子。穿着靴子和骑装外套。您这就下去么，老爷？晚饭怕是已经摆上了。」

「是的，」科斯莫说着便要出门，「还有，斯派尔，你在街上，或者楼下那间什么人都进出的屋子里，要是瞧见一个瘦高个子，戴一顶缀着流苏的怪模怪样的帽子，就想方设法打听点他的事；至少，你见过他之后要让我知道。……你或许可以跟着他走一程，看看他往哪儿去。」

说罢这话，科斯莫不容斯派尔回答，便已走出了房间。斯派尔的惊讶化作一声低低的感叹：「哎，这可真是！」

## Part II / 第二卷

### I / I

===Original===
Cosmo descended into a hall now empty and with most of its lights extinguished. A loud murmur of voices guided him to the door of the dining room. He discovered it to be a long apartment with flat pilasters dividing its whitewashed walls, and resembling somewhat a convent’s refectory. The resemblance was accentuated by the two narrow tables occupying its middle. One of them had been appropriated by the British naval officers, had lights on it, and bristled with the necks of wine bottles along its whole length. The talk round it was confused and noisy. The other, shorter, table accommodated two rows of people in sombre garments who at first glance struck Cosmo as natives of the town and belonging to a lower station in life. They had less lights, less wine, and almost no animation. Several smaller tables were ranged against the walls at equal intervals, and Cosmo’s eye was caught by one of them because of the candles in the sconce on the wall above it having been lighted. Its cloth was dazzlingly white, and Signor Cantelucci with a napkin in his hand stood respectfully at the elbow of its sole occupant, who was seated with his back to the door.

Cosmo was under the impression that his entrance had been unobserved. But before he had walked half the length of the room Signor Cantelucci, whose eyes had never ceased darting here and there while his body preserved its deferential attitude at the elbow of the exclusive client, advanced to meet him with his serious and attentive air. He bowed. Perhaps the signore would not mind sharing the table of his illustrious countryman.

“Yes, if my countryman doesn’t object,” assented Cosmo readily. He was absolutely certain that this must be the doctor of whom Spire had spoken.

===Chinese===
科斯莫走下楼梯，来到此刻已空空荡荡、大半灯火熄灭的门厅。一阵嘈杂的人声引着他找到了餐厅的门。他发觉这是一间狭长的屋子，粉白的墙壁被扁平的壁柱分隔开来，颇像一座修道院的食堂。居中摆着两张狭长的桌子，更加强了这份相像。其中一张被英国海军军官们占了，桌上点着灯，沿桌一溜儿插满了酒瓶颈，热闹得很。围着它的谈话又乱又闹。另一张短些的桌子，两排坐着身着深色衣裳的人，科斯莫一眼看去，便觉出他们是本城的当地人，出身较低的阶层。他们那儿灯火少些，酒也少些，几乎没什么动静。沿墙还等距摆着几张小桌，其中一张惹了科斯莫的眼，因为墙上壁灯里的蜡烛已点着了。桌布白得晃眼，坎泰洛奇先生（Signor Cantelucci）手里捏着餐巾，恭恭敬敬地立在那张桌唯一的客人肘边——那人背对着门坐着。

科斯莫原以为自己进来时无人留意。可他还没走到屋子的一半，坎泰洛奇先生便迎了上来——此人的眼睛一刻不停地在四处溜转，身子却始终保持着那种对独占主顾的恭顺姿态，神色庄重而殷勤。他一躬身。也许先生不介意与他那位显赫的同乡共坐一桌吧。

「自然，只要我的同乡不反对。」科斯莫爽快地应允了。他满心认定，这必定就是斯帕尔说起过的那位医生了。

===Original===
Cantelucci had no doubt that His Excellency’s company would be most welcome to his illustrious countryman. Then stepping aside, he added under his breath: “He is a person of great distinction. A most valued patron of mine.﻿ ﻿…” The person thus commended, turning his head ensconced in the high collar of his coat, disclosed to Cosmo a round face with a shaved chin, strongly marked eyebrows, round eyes, and thin lips compressed into a slightly peevish droop which, however, was at once corrected by an attempt at a faint smile. Cosmo, too, produced a faint smile. For an appreciable moment they looked at each other without saying a word while Cantelucci, silent too, executed a profound bow.

“Sit down, sir, sit down,” said the elder man (Cosmo judged him to be well over forty), raising his voice above the uproar made by the occupants of the naval table and waving his hand at the empty chair facing his own. It had a high carved back showing some traces of gilding, and the silk which covered it was worn to rags. Cosmo sat down while Cantelucci disappeared and the man across the table positively shouted, “I am glad,” and immediately followed that declaration by an energetic “Oh damn!” He bent over the table: “One can’t hear oneself speak with that noisy lot. All heroes, no doubt, but not a single gentleman.”

He leaned back and waited till the outburst of noisy mirth had died out at the officers’ table. The corners of his mouth drooped again and Cosmo came to the conclusion that face in repose was decidedly peevish.

===Chinese===
坎泰洛奇毫不怀疑，阁下的光临对那位显赫的同乡必定是最受欢迎的。他随即侧身一步，压低声音补了一句：「他是个极有身份的人物。是我最看重的一位主顾。……」受到如此夸赞的那人，把裹在外衣高领里的头转过来，向科斯莫露出一张圆脸——下巴刮得精光，眉毛浓重，一双圆眼，薄嘴唇紧抿着，微微挂下一股不悦之意，然而旋即被一丝勉强浮起的浅笑纠正了。科斯莫也回以一个浅笑。在颇有分量的片刻里，两人默默对视，一言不发，坎泰洛奇也不作声，深深一躬。

「坐下吧，先生，请坐。」年长的那位说（科斯莫估他四十出头），提高了嗓门盖过海军那一桌的喧嚷，朝自己对面那张空椅子一摆手。那椅子高高的雕花椅背上还留着几缕鎏金的痕迹，蒙椅面的绸子已磨得破烂。科斯莫坐下，坎泰洛奇便不见了，而对面那人竟大喊一声：「我很高兴，」紧接着又起劲地迸出一句：「该死！」他探身凑过桌来：「跟这帮闹腾的家伙在一块儿，连自己说的话都听不见。全都是英雄，不用问，可一个君子也挑不出来。」

他往后一靠，直等到军官们那一桌那阵哄笑平息下去。他的嘴角又耷拉下来，科斯莫于是断定，这张脸在静止不动时，分明是带着点愠怒的。

===Original===
“I don’t know what they have got to be so merry about,” the other began, with a slight glance at the naval table and leaning forward again towards Cosmo. “Their occupation is gone. Heroes are a thoughtless lot. Yet just look at that elderly lieutenant at the head of the table. Shabby coat. Old epaulette. He doesn’t laugh. He will die a lieutenant﻿—on half pay. That’s how heroic people end when the heroic times are over.”

“I am glad,” said Cosmo steadily, “that you recognize at least their heroism.”

The other opened his mouth for some time before he laughed, and that gave his face an expression of somewhat hard jollity. But the laugh when it came was by no means loud and had a sort of ingratiating softness.

“No, no. Don’t think I am disparaging our sea service. I had the privilege to know the greatest hero of them all. Yes, I had two talks with Lord Nelson. Well, he was certainly not﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===
「我不懂他们有什么好这样快活的，」对方开了口，朝海军那一桌略瞟了一眼，又朝科斯莫探过身来。「他们的营生已经没了。英雄这帮人，最不长心。可你且瞧瞧桌头上那位年长些的海军上尉。破外套。旧肩章。他可不笑。他到死也还是个上尉——领着半薪。英雄的时代一过，英雄的人物便是这般收场。」

「我倒很高兴，」科斯莫平稳地说，「您至少还认得他们的英雄气。」

对方张了好一会儿嘴才笑出来，这一来，他脸上便带出几分硬邦邦的诙谐相。可那笑一旦出来，倒一点不响，反而有几分讨好的绵软。

「不，不。别以为我在贬低咱们的海上军役。我有幸认识他们里头最了不起的那位英雄。不错，我与纳尔逊勋爵（Lord Nelson）谈过两次话。嗯，他当然不是……」

===Original===
He interrupted himself and raising his eyes saw the perfectly still gaze of Cosmo fastened on his face. Then peevishly:

“What I meant to say was that he at least was indubitably a hero. I remember that I was very careful about what I said to him. I had to be mighty careful then about what I said to anybody. Someone might have put it into his head to hang me at some yardarm or other.”

“I envy your experience all the same,” said Cosmo amiably. “I suppose your conscience was clear?”

“I have always been most careful not to give my conscience any license to trouble me,” retorted the other with a certain curtness of tone which was not offensive; “and I have lived now for some considerable time. I am really much older than I look,” he concluded, giving Cosmo such a keen glance that the young man could not help a smile.

===Chinese===
他顿住不说，抬起眼，正撞见科斯莫那毫不转动的目光定定地落在他脸上。他便带愠道：

「我要说的是，至少他是无可置疑的英雄。我记得，我对他说什么，是极其谨慎的。那个时候，我对谁说什么，都得格外当心。保不齐有人会在他脑子里塞个念头，把我吊死在这个那个桁端上。」

「尽管如此，我仍羡慕您这份经历。」科斯莫和悦地说，「我想，您的良心是清白的吧？」

「我一向极其当心，绝不给我的良心任何放肆作乱的余地，」对方回了一句，语气里带着点不客气的简慢，却并不招人反感，「而且我如今已活了相当些年头了。我实在比看上去要老得多。」他末了补一句，朝科斯莫投来那样锐利的一瞥，叫这年轻人忍不住笑了。

===Original===
The other went on looking at him steadily for a while, then let his eyes wander to a door in a distant part of the long room as if impatient for the coming of the dinner. Then giving it up:

“A man who has lived actively, actively I say, the last twenty years may well feel as old as Methuselah. Lord Nelson was but a circumstance in my life. I wonder, had he lived, how he would have taken all this.”

A slight movement of his hand seemed to carry this allusion outside the confines of the vaulted noisy room, to indicate all the out-of-doors of the world. Cosmo remarked that the hand was muscular, shapely, and extremely well cared for.

“I think there can be no doubt about the nature of his feelings if he were living.” Cosmo’s voice was exactly noncommittal. His interlocutor grunted slightly.

“H’m. He would have done nothing but groan and complain about anything and everything. No, he wouldn’t have taken it laughing. Very poor physique. Very. Frightful hypochondriac.﻿ ﻿… I am a doctor, you know.”

===Chinese===
对方定定地瞧了他一会儿，随即把目光挪向这间长屋远处的一道门，似乎等饭等得不耐烦了。继而放弃了这等待：

「一个人若是在活跃地——我说的是活跃地——过了这二十年，觉得自己老得像玛士撒拉（Methuselah），也是理所当然。纳尔逊勋爵于我一生，不过是桩小事。我倒想知道，他若活着，对这一切会作何感想。」

他的手微微一动，似乎把这一指带到了这拱顶喧嚷的屋子之外，指向了整个室外天地。科斯莫留意到，那只手肌肉结实、形状好看，且照料得极其周到。

「我想，他若是活着，那份感受的性质，当无可置疑。」科斯莫的声气不偏不倚。对方轻轻地哼了一声。

「嗯。他除了对什么都、对一切都呻吟抱怨之外，什么也不会做。不，他不会笑着承受这些。身子骨糟得很。糟得很。可怕的疑病症。……您知道，我是个医生。」

===Original===
Cantelucci was going to attend himself on his two guests. He presented to the doctor a smoking soup tureen enveloped in a napkin. The doctor assumed at once a businesslike air, and at his invitation Cosmo held out his plate. The doctor helped him carefully.

“Don’t forget the wine, my wine, Anzelmo,” he said to Cantelucci, who answered by a profound bow. “I saved his life once,” he continued after the innkeeper had gone away.

The tone was particularly significant. Cosmo, partly repelled and partly amused by the man, enquired whether the worthy host had been very ill. The doctor swallowed the last spoonful of soup.

“Ill,” he said. “He had a gash that long in his side and a set of forty-pound fetters in his legs. I cured both complaints. Not without some risk to myself, as you may imagine. There was an epidemic of hanging and shooting in the South of Italy then.”

===Chinese===
坎泰洛奇要亲自侍候这两位贵客。他端到医生面前一只冒着热气的汤盅，外面裹着餐巾。医生立刻换上一副公事公办的神气，并请科斯莫伸出盘子来。医生仔细地给他舀了汤。

「别忘了酒，我的酒，安泽尔莫，」他对坎泰洛奇说，对方以深深一躬作答。「我救过他一命，」店主走开后，他接着说。

那语气意味深长，分外不同。科斯莫对这人半觉排斥、半觉有趣，便问起这位可敬的老板是否病得很重。医生咽下了最后一匙汤。

「病，」他说。「他肋上一道这么长的口子，腿上一副四十磅重的镣铐。这两样毛病我都给他治好了。您可想而知，我自己也担了不少风险。那个时候，意大利南部正流行着绞刑和枪决。」

===Original===
He noticed Cosmo’s steady stare and raised the corners of his mouth with an effect of geniality on his broad rosy face.

“In ’99, you know. I wonder I didn’t die of it too. I was considerably younger then and my humane instincts, early enthusiasms, and so on had led me into pretty bad company. However, I had also pretty good friends. What with one thing and another I am pretty well known all over Italy. My name is Martel﻿—Doctor Martel. You probably may have heard.﻿ ﻿…”

He threw a searching glance at Cosmo, who bowed noncommittally, and went on without a pause: “I am the man who brought vaccine to Italy, first. Cantelucci was trying to tell me your name but really I couldn’t make it out.”

“Latham is my name,” said Cosmo, “and I only set foot in Italy for the first time in my life two days ago.”

===Chinese===
他察觉到科斯莫那定定的注视，便把嘴角往上一提，在他那张宽阔红润的脸上做出一副和气的模样。

「那是在 '99 年，您知道。我也奇怪自己竟没跟着送命。那时我年轻得多，我那些人道的本能、早年的热情之类，把我带进了一伙很糟糕的伙伴里。不过，我也有相当不错的朋友。左一件事右一件事下来，我在整个意大利也算是颇有几分名头了。我姓马泰尔——马泰尔医生（Doctor Martel）。您或许听人说起过。……」

他向科斯莫投来一道探询的目光，科斯莫含糊地一躬身，他便一刻不停地接着说：「我头一个把牛痘疫苗带到了意大利。坎泰洛奇方才想把你我的姓名互通一下，可我实在没听清。」

「我姓莱瑟姆，」科斯莫说，「我这辈子头一回踏上意大利的土地，不过是两天前的事。」

===Original===
The doctor jerked his head sideways.

“Latham, eh? Yorkshire?”

“Yes,” said Cosmo, smiling.

“To be sure. Sir Charles﻿ ﻿…”

“That’s my father.”

“Yes, yes. Served in the Guards. I used to know the doctor of his regiment. Married in Italy. I don’t remember the lady’s name. Oh, those are old times. Might have been a hundred years ago.”

“You mean that so much history has been made since.”

“Yes, no end of history,” assented the doctor, but checked himself. “And yet, tell me, what does it all amount to?”

===Chinese===
医生把头往旁边一摆。

「莱瑟姆，嗯？约克郡（Yorkshire）的？」

「是的。」科斯莫笑着说。

「这就对了。查尔斯爵士……」

「那是我父亲。」

「是，是。在禁卫军里做过事。我从前认得他那个团的军医。在意大利结的婚。那位夫人的名字我可记不得了。嗐，那都是老话了。简直像一百年前的事。」

「您是说，自那以后已经历了许多历史。」

「不错，历史没完没了。」医生应道，却又顿了顿，「可话又说回来，你倒讲讲，这一切到底算得了什么？」

===Original===
Cosmo made no answer. Cantelucci having brought the wine while they talked, the doctor-filled two glasses, waited a moment as if to hear Cosmo speak, but as the young man remained silent he said:

“Well, let us drink then to Peace.”

He tossed the wine down his throat while Cosmo drank his much more leisurely. As they set down their empty glasses they were startled by a roar of a tremendous voice filling the vaulted room from end to end in order to “let Their Honours know that the boat was at the steps.” The doctor made a faint grimace.

“Do you hear the voice of the British lion, Mr. Latham?” he asked peevishly. “Ah, well, we will have some little peace now here.”

===Chinese===
科斯莫没有作答。两人说话间坎泰洛奇已把酒送了来，医生斟满两只杯子，停了一停，似乎等着科斯莫开口，可那年轻人既不开口，他便道：

「那么，就让我们为和平干一杯吧。」

他把酒一口灌下喉去，科斯莫则从容得多地饮了自己的那杯。两人放下空杯时，却被一声惊天动地的嗓门吓了一跳——那嗓门灌满了这拱顶的屋子，从头响到尾，为的是「让大人们知道，小艇已在台阶边候着」。医生微微做了个怪相。

「您听见英国狮子的吼声了吗，莱瑟姆先生？」他带着愠色问道。「嗐，也好，咱们这儿这就能清静一会儿了。」

===Original===
Those officers at the naval table who had to go on board rose in a body and left the room hastily. Three or four who had a longer leave drew close together and began to talk low with their heads in a bunch. Cosmo glancing down the room seemed to recognize at the door the form of the seaman whom he had met earlier in the evening. He followed the officers out. The other diners, the sombre ones, and a good many of them with powdered heads, were also leaving the room. Cantelucci put another dish on the table, stepped back a pace with a bow, and stood still. A moment of profound silence succeeded the noise.

“First rate,” said Doctor Martel to Cosmo, after tasting the dish, and then gave a nod to Cantelucci, who made another bow and retreated backwards, always with a solemn expression on his face.

===Chinese===
海军那一桌须回船的军官们全体起立，匆匆出了屋。三四个假期长些的凑到一处，脑袋挤作一堆，压低声音谈起来。科斯莫朝屋子的下首一望，似乎在门口认出了他傍晚早些时候遇见过的那个水手的身影。他随军官们走了出去。其余的食客，那些深色衣裳的，其中不少还扑着粉的头，也正陆续离席。坎泰洛奇又端上一道菜放在桌上，退后一步一躬身，便静静立着。一阵极深的寂静接在喧闹之后。

「一流。」马泰尔医生尝过那道菜后对科斯莫说，随即朝坎泰洛奇点点头，坎泰洛奇又鞠了一躬，倒退着退下去，脸上始终是一副郑重的神情。

===Original===
“Italian cooking, of course, but then I am an old Italian myself. Not that I love them, but I have acquired many of their tastes. Before we have done dining you will have tasted the perfection of their cookery, north and south, but I assure you are sharing my dinner. You don’t suppose that the dishes that come to this table are the same the common customers get.”

Cosmo made a slight bow. “I am very sensible of the privilege,” he said.

“The honour and the pleasure are mine, I assure you,” the doctor said in a half-careless tone and looking with distaste towards the small knot of officers with a twenty-four hours’ leave who had finished their confabulation and had risen in a body like men who had agreed on some pleasant course of action. Only the elderly lieutenant lagging a little behind cast a glance at his two countrymen at the little table and followed his comrades with less eager movements.

===Chinese===
「这是意大利菜，自然，不过我自己也算半个老意大利人了。并不是说我喜欢他们，只是我已沾染了他们的不少口味。等我们这顿饭吃完，您就会尝遍他们南北烹调的精妙，可我得向您保证，您此刻与我共进的是我的晚餐。您总不至于以为，端上这张桌子的菜，和寻常主顾吃到的是一样吧。」

科斯莫微微一躬身。「我很体味得到这份优待。」他说。

「这荣幸和这份乐趣都在我这边，我向您保证。」医生半带不经心地说着，嫌恶地朝那一小簇有二十四小时假期的军官望去——他们已结束了密谈，像一群议定了某桩乐事的人那样齐齐起身。唯有那位年长些的上尉稍稍落在后头，朝小桌旁他的两位同胞投来一瞥，这才以没那么急切的步子跟上了同伴。

===Original===
“A quarter of a tough bullock or half a roast sheep are more in their way, and Cantelucci knows it. As to that company that was sitting at the other table, well, I daresay you can tell yourself what they were, small officials or tradesmen of some sort. I should think that emptying all their pockets﻿—and they were how many, say twenty﻿—you couldn’t collect the value of one English pound at any given time. And Cantelucci knows that too. Well, of course. Still he does well here, but it’s a poor place. I wonder, Mr. Latham, what are you doing here?”

“Well,” said Cosmo with a good-humoured smile, “I am just staying here. Just as you yourself are staying here.”

“Ah, but you never saved Cantelucci’s life, whereas I did and that’s the reason why I am staying here: out of mere kindness and to give him an opportunity to show his gratitude.﻿ ﻿… Let me fill your glass. Not bad, this wine.”

“Excellent. What is it?”

“God knows. Let us call it Cantelucci’s gratitude. Generous stuff, this, to wash down those dishes with. Gluttony is an odious vice, but an ambition to dine well is about the only one which can be indulged at no cost to one’s fellow men.”

===Chinese===
「四分之一头老韧的小公牛，或者半只烤羊，才更合他们的脾胃，坎泰洛奇心里有数。至于另一张桌坐着的那一伙人，嗐，我想您自己也分辨得出，无非是些小官吏或买卖人之类。我估摸着，把他们所有人的口袋都掏空——他们有多少人，就算二十个吧——随便什么时候你也凑不齐一个英镑的数目。这一点坎泰洛奇也明白。嗯，自然。不过他在这儿生意还过得去，可这地方终究是穷地方。我倒想知道，莱瑟姆先生，您到这儿来做什么？」

「这个嘛，」科斯莫带几分和善的笑意说，「我不过是住在这里罢了。就同您自己住在这里一样。」

「啊，可您从未救过坎泰洛奇的命，我却救过，这才是我住在这里的缘故：纯粹出于一片好心，好给他一个报答的机会。……让我给您斟上。这酒不赖。」

「极好。是什么酒？」

「天晓得。咱们就把它叫作坎泰洛奇的感恩吧。够劲的货色，拿它顺这些菜下去，再合适不过。贪食是可憎的恶习，可要想吃顿好饭这份志气，大约是唯一一种纵情其中也不会让旁人破费的好毛病。」

===Original===
“It didn’t strike me,” murmured Cosmo absently, for he was just then asking himself why he didn’t like this pleasant companion, and had just come to the conclusion that it was because of his indecisive expression wavering between peevishness and jocularity with something else in addition, as it were, in the background of his handsome, neat, and comfortable person. Something that was not aggressive nor yet exactly impudent. He wondered at his mistrust of the personality which certainly was very communicative but apparently not inquisitive. At that moment he heard himself addressed with a direct inquiry.

“You passed, of course, through Paris?”

“Yes, and Switzerland.”

“Oh, Paris. I wonder what it looks like now. Full of English people, of course. Let’s see, how many years is it since I was last there? Ha, lots of heads rolled off very noble shoulders since. Well, I am trying to make my way there. Curious times. I have found some letters here. Duke of Wellington very much disliked, what? His nod is insupportable, eh?”

===Chinese===
「这我倒没留意。」科斯莫心不在焉地低声说，因为他此时正暗自盘问自己：为什么不喜欢这个和悦的同席者？他刚刚得出的结论是——因为这人脸上那种摇摆不定的神情，在愠怒与诙谐之间游移，之外还另有一点什么，仿佛藏在他那漂亮、整洁、舒泰的仪表的背后。那一点东西既非咄咄逼人，又不全是放肆无礼。他自己也奇怪，何以会对这副性子心存戒备——这性子分明极健谈，却似乎并不爱打听。就在这时，他听见对方直截了当地问了他一句。

「您自然是经过巴黎的喽？」

「是的，还经过了瑞士。」

「嗐，巴黎。我倒想知道它如今是什么光景。自然挤满了英国人。让我想想，我最后一次到那儿，有多少年了？哈，自那以后，不少极尊贵的肩膀上的脑袋都滚落下来啦。嗯，我正设法往那儿去。时世奇特。我在这儿收到几封信。威灵顿公爵（Duke of Wellington）很不得人心，是吧？他点个头也叫人受不了，嗯？」

===Original===
“I have just had a sight of the Duke two or three times,” said Cosmo. “I can assure you that everybody is treating him with the greatest respect.”

“Of course, of course. All the same I bet that all these foreigners are chuckling to themselves at having finished the job without him.”

“They needn’t be so pleased with themselves,” said Cosmo scornfully. “The mere weight of their numbers﻿ ﻿…”

“Yes. It was more like a migration of armed tribes than an army. They will boast of their success all the same. There is no saying what the Duke himself thinks.﻿ ﻿… I wonder if he could have beaten the other in a fair fight. Well, that will never be known now.”

===Chinese===
「我只见过公爵两三面，」科斯莫说，「我可以向您保证，人人都待他极为敬重。」

「自然，自然。话虽如此，我敢打赌，这些外国人一个个都在心里偷着乐呢——这桩差事没他也照样办成了。」

「他们也犯不着这样自鸣得意，」科斯莫带着轻蔑说，「单凭他们人数上的分量就……」

「不错。那与其说是一支军队，不如说是一次武装部族的迁徙。可他们照样会拿这场胜利自吹自擂。公爵本人作何感想，那就无从得知了。……我倒想知道，若是堂堂正正地交手，他能不能胜过另一位。嗐，这如今永远也无从知道了。」

===Original===
Cosmo had a sudden sense of an epical tale with a doubtful conclusion. He made no answer. Cantelucci had come and gone solemnly, self-contained, with the usual two ceremonial bows. As he retreated he put out all the candles on the central table and became lost to view. From the illuminated spot at which he sat, Cosmo’s eyes met only the shadows of the long refectory-like room with its lofty windows closely shuttered so that they looked like a row of niches for statues. Yet the murmur of the piazza full of people stole faintly into his ear. Cosmo had the recollection of the vast expanse of flagstones enclosed by the shadowy and palatial masses gleaming with lights here and there under the night sky thick with stars and perfectly cloudless.

“This is a very quiet inn,” he observed.

“It has that advantage certainly. The walls are fairly thick, as you can see. It’s an unfinished palace, I mean as to its internal decorations, which were going to be very splendid and even more costly than splendid. The owner of it, I mean the man who had it built, died of hunger in that hall out there.”

“Died of hunger?” repeated Cosmo.

“No doubt about it. It was during the siege of Genoa. You know the siege, surely?”

===Chinese===
科斯莫忽地生出一种感觉，仿佛这是一则结局暧昧的史诗故事。他没有作答。坎泰洛奇来过又走了，神情庄重、自持，照例行了两礼两躬。他退下去时，熄灭了当中那张桌上所有的蜡烛，人也随之隐没在暗处。从他所坐的这片光亮之处望去，科斯莫的目光只遇见这间食堂般长屋里的重重阴影——那些高高的窗子紧紧闭着窗板，看上去像是一排安放雕像的神龛。然而，挤满了人的广场上那阵低语，隐隐约约地钻进他耳里。科斯莫记起了那片铺着石板的辽阔空地，四周是被夜色笼罩的宫殿般的巨大建筑群，在密密繁星、万里无云的夜空下，这儿那儿地闪着灯火。

「这是一家很清静的客栈。」他说。

「这好处它自然是有的。墙相当厚，您也看得见。这是一座未完工的宫殿，我是说它内部的装修还没完工——原本要做得极华丽，甚至华贵更甚于华丽。它的主人，我是说那位叫人把它盖起来的人，就活活饿死在外头那间门厅里。」

「饿死？」科斯莫重复道。

「这没什么可怀疑的。那是热那亚被围城的时候。那场围城您总该知道吧？」

===Original===
Cosmo recollected himself. “I was quite a child at the time,” he said.

The venerated client of Cantelucci cracked a walnut and then looked at Cosmo’s face.

“I should think you weren’t seven years old at the time,” he said in a judicial tone. “When I first came into Italy with the vaccine, you know, Sir Charles’s marriage was still being talked about in Florence. I remember it perfectly though it seems as if it had all happened in another world. Yes, indubitably he died of hunger like ten thousand other Genoese. He couldn’t go out to hunt for garbage with the populace or crawl out at night trying to gather nettles in the ditches outside the forts, and nobody would have known that he was dead for a month if one of the bombs out of a bomb-vessel with Admiral Keith’s blockading squadron hadn’t burst the door in. They found him at the foot of the stairs, and, they say, with a lot of gold pieces in his pockets. But nobody cared much for that. If it had been a lot of half-gnawed bones there would have been blood spilt, no doubt. For all I know there were or may be even now secret places full of gold in the thickness of these walls. However, the body was thrown into a corpse-cart and the authorities boarded the doorway. It remained boarded for years because the heirs didn’t care to have anything to do with that shell of a palace. I fancy that the last of them died in the snows of Russia. Cantelucci came along, and owing to a friendship with some sort of scribe in the Municipality he got permission to use the place for his hostelry. He told me that he found several half ducats in the corners of the hall when he took possession. I suppose they paid for the whitewash, for I can’t believe that Cantelucci had much money in his pockets.”

===Chinese===
科斯莫回过神来。「那时候我还是个十足的孩子。」他说。

坎泰洛奇这位受敬重的主顾磕开一只核桃，然后端详起科斯莫的脸来。

「我琢磨着那时您还不到七岁，」他以一种下判词的口吻说，「您知道，当年我带着牛痘疫苗头一遭进入意大利的时候，查尔斯爵士的婚事在佛罗伦萨（Florence）还被人谈起。我记得清清楚楚，尽管这一切仿佛发生在另一个世界似的。不错，他毫无疑问是饿死的，和另外一万个热那亚人一样。他没法跟着平民百姓出去翻捡残羹剩饭，夜里也不能爬出去在堡垒外的壕沟里采荨麻；若不是基思海军上将（Admiral Keith）的封锁舰队里一艘炸弹船上扔下的一颗炸弹把门轰开，只怕他死了整整一个月也没人知道。人们是在楼梯脚下发现他的，据说，他口袋里还揣着不少金币。可没谁把这当回事。倘若发现的是一堆啃了一半的骨头，那必定是要见血的，毫无疑问。据我所知，这些墙的厚处里头，当年可能有、甚至如今也还藏着装满金子的秘窟。话说回来，那尸体被扔上了运尸车，官府把门口钉死了。那一钉就是好些年，因为继承人们都不愿和这副宫殿的空壳打交道。我猜想他们当中最后的一位是死在俄罗斯的大雪里的。后来坎泰洛奇来了，靠着和市政厅某个书办交好的情分，他得了许可，把这地方拿来开客栈。他告诉我，他接手的时候，在门厅的角落里捡到过几个半杜卡特金币。我估摸这些钱是用来付刷墙的那层白灰的，因为我绝不相信坎泰洛奇口袋里能有多少钱。」

===Original===
“Perhaps he found one of those secret hiding-places of which you spoke,” suggested Cosmo.

“What? Cantelucci? He never looked for any gold. He is too much in the clouds; but he has made us dine well in the palace of the starved man, hasn’t he? Sixteen years ago in Naples he was a Jacobin and a friend of the French, a rebel, a traitor to his king if you like﻿—but he has a good memory, there is no denying that.”

“Is he a Neapolitan, then?” asked Cosmo. “I imagined they were of a different type.”

“God only knows. He was there and I didn’t ask him. He was a prisoner of the royalists, of the reactionaries. I was much younger then and perhaps more humane. Flesh and blood couldn’t stand in the sight of the way in which they were being treated, men of position, of attainments, of intelligence. The Neapolitan Jacobins were no populace. They were men of character and ideas, the pick of all classes. They were properly liberals. Still they were called Jacobins and you may be surprised that I, a professional man and an Englishman﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===
「也许他找到了您说的那种秘藏金子的暗处呢。」科斯莫猜道。

「什么？坎泰洛奇？他才不会去找什么金子。他这人成天云里雾里的；不过他确实让我们在这饿死人的宫殿里吃了一顿好饭，不是吗？十六年前在那不勒斯（Naples），他是个雅各宾派（Jacobin），是法国人的朋友，是个叛党，您尽可以说他是国王的叛臣——可他的记性好，这一点无可否认。」

「那么，他是那不勒斯人喽？」科斯莫问，「我还以为他们是另一种做派的人呢。」

「只有天晓得。他当时在那儿，我没问过他。他是保皇党人、是那班反动派手里的囚犯。那会儿我年轻得多，或许也更多几分人情。亲眼看到他们所受的待遇——那可都是有身份、有造诣、有才识的人——血肉之躯是断断受不了的。那不勒斯的雅各宾派并非市井之徒。他们是有品格、有见解的人，是各阶层的翘楚。他们原本是地道的自由派。然而他们却被叫作雅各宾派，您也许会觉得奇怪——我这样一个以专业为业的人，又是个英国人……」

===Original===
Cosmo, looking up at the sudden pause, saw the doctor sitting with the dull eyes and the expression of a man suddenly dissatisfied with himself. Cosmo hastened to say that he himself was no friend of reactionaries and in any case not conceited enough to judge the conduct of men older than himself. Without a sign that he had heard a word of that speech the doctor had a faint and peevish smile. He never moved at all till, after a longish interval, Cosmo spoke again.

“Were you expecting somebody that would want to see you this evening?” he asked.

The doctor started.

“See me? No. Why do you ask?”

“Because within the last five minutes somebody has put his head twice through the door; and as I don’t expect either a visitor or a messenger, I thought he was looking for you. I don’t know a single soul here.”

===Chinese===
科斯莫抬眼看着这突如其来的停顿，只见医生坐在那儿，眼神发黯，一副忽然对自己不满的神气。科斯莫忙接口说，他自己也绝非反动派之友，无论如何也不至于自负到去评判年长于自己之人的行事。医生没有半点迹象表明他听进了这句话，只浮起一个淡淡的、带着愠色的笑。他一动也不动，直到过了颇有一会儿，科斯莫才又开口。

「您今晚是在等什么要见您的人吗？」他问。

医生一怔。

「见我？没有。您为什么这样问？」

「因为过去五分钟之内，有人已经两次把头伸进门来；而我既不盼客人、也不盼信差，便以为他是在找您。我在此地一个人也不认识。」

===Original===
The doctor remained perfectly unmoved. Cosmo, who was looking towards the distant door, saw the head again and this time shouted at it an inquiry. Thereupon the owner of the head entered and had not advanced half the length of the room before Cosmo recognized in him the portly figure of Spire. To his great surprise, however, Spire instead of coming up to the table made a vague gesture and stopped short.

This was strange conduct. The doctor sat completely unconscious, and Cosmo took the course of excusing himself and following Spire, who, directly he had seen his master rise, had retreated rapidly to the door. The doctor did not rouse himself to answer, and Cosmo left him leaning on his elbow in a thoughtful attitude. In the badly lighted hall he found Spire waiting for him between the foot of the stairs and the door which Cosmo presumed was leading to the offices of the hotel. Again Spire made a vague gesture which seemed to convey a warning, and approached his master on tiptoe.

===Chinese===
医生依旧纹丝不动。科斯莫正望着远处那道门，又看见了那颗脑袋，这一回便朝它高声喝问了一句。于是那颗脑袋的主人走了进来，还没走到屋子的一半，科斯莫便认出那是斯帕尔（Spire）圆滚滚的身影。然而令他大为诧异的是，斯帕尔并没有走到桌前来，只含糊地做了个手势，便戛然止步。

这举止未免古怪。医生坐在那里全无觉察，科斯莫便起身告罪，跟着斯帕尔去——斯帕尔一见主人起身，便迅速退回到门口去了。医生并没有振作起来作答，科斯莫便撇下他，让他支着胳膊肘、一副沉思的模样留在那儿。在灯火昏暗的门厅里，他找到了斯帕尔——那人正等在楼梯脚下和一道门之间，科斯莫揣测那道门是通向客栈各处办事房的。斯帕尔又含糊地做了个手势，似乎含着告诫之意，随即踮着脚尖凑近主人。

===Original===
“Well, what is it? What do you mean by flourishing your arm at me like this?” asked Cosmo sharply, and Spire ventured on a warning “Ssh!”

“Why, there is nobody here,” said Cosmo, lowering his voice nevertheless.

“I wanted to tell you, sir, I have seen that fellow.”

“What fellow? Oh yes. The fellow with the cap. Where did you see him?”

“He is here,” said Spire, pointing to the closed door.

“Here? What could a man like that want here? Did you speak to him?”

“No, sir, he has just come in and for all I know he may be already gone away﻿—though I don’t think so.”

“Oh, you don’t think so. Do you know what he has come for?”

Spire made no answer to the question, but after a short silence: “I will go and see, and if you stand where you are, sir, you will be able to look right into the room. He may not be the man.”

Without waiting for an answer he moved towards the closed door and threw it wide open. The room, very much like the dining room but smaller, was lighted gloomily by two smoky oil lamps hanging from the ceiling, over a trestle table having a wooden bench on each side. Bad as the light was Cosmo made out at once the peculiar cap. The wearer, sitting on one of the benches, was leaning with both elbows across the table towards the fair head of a girl half-hidden by a lace scarf. They were engaged in earnest conversation so that they never turned their heads at Spire’s entrance. Cosmo had just time to discern the fine line of the girl’s shoulders, which were half-tinned from him when Spire shut the door.

===Chinese===
「嗯，什么事？你朝我这么比比划划的是什么意思？」科斯莫厉声问，斯帕尔则壮着胆子警告了一声「嘘！」

「怎么，这儿又没有别人。」科斯莫说，却还是压低了嗓门。

「我要禀告您，先生，我瞧见那家伙了。」

「什么家伙？哦，对了。那个戴便帽的家伙。你在哪儿瞧见他的？」

「他就在这儿。」斯帕尔指着那道关着的门说。

「在这儿？那样一个人到这儿来做什么？你跟他说话了没有？」

「没有，先生，他刚进来不久，据我所知，他也许已经走了——不过我不这么想。」

「哦，你不这么想。那你知道他是来做什么的吗？」

斯帕尔没有回答这个问题，沉默了片刻才说：「我去瞧瞧，先生您就站在原地，便能把这屋子里头瞧得一清二楚。他也未必就是那个人。」

他不等回答，便朝那道关着的门走去，把它一把推开。那间屋子很像餐厅，只是小些，被天花板上吊着的两盏冒着烟的油灯照得阴沉沉的，灯下是一张搁板桌，两边各有一条木长凳。灯光虽糟，科斯莫却一眼就辨出了那顶古怪的便帽。戴帽子的人坐在一条长凳上，双肘撑过桌面，朝着那颗被一方蕾丝头巾半遮半掩的金发脑袋凑过去。两人正谈得起劲，因此斯帕尔进去时，他们头也没回。科斯莫只来得及辨清那姑娘肩头美好的线条——那肩头有一半正背着他——斯帕尔便把门关上了。

### II / II

===Original===
Returning to his bedroom, Cosmo found the fire of logs still playing fitfully upon the drawn curtains, upon the dim shape of the canopied bed of state, and perceived that Spire as directed had prepared the writing table and had placed a screen round the inviting-looking armchair.

He did not sit down to write. He felt more than ever that in a moment of amused expansion he had made a rash promise to his sister. The difficulty in keeping it had confronted him for the first time in Paris. Henrietta would have liked to hear of people he met, of the great world indulging in the newfound freedom of travel, the English, the French, the Poles, the Germans. Certainly he had seen quite a lot of people; but the problem was as to what could be said about them to a young girl, ignorant of the world, brought up in the country, and having really no notion of what mankind was like. He admitted to himself with introspective sincerity that even he did not exactly know what mankind was really like. He was too much of a novice, and she, obviously, was too innocent to be told of his suspicions and of what it was like. Even to describe the world outwardly was not an easy task﻿—to Henrietta. The world was certainly amusing. Oh yes, it was amusing; but even as he thought that, he felt within him a certain distaste. Just before he had left Paris he had been at a rout given by a great lady. There was a fellow there who somehow became suspected of picking pockets. He was extremely ugly and therefore attracted notice. The great lady, asked if she had invited him, denied ever having seen him before, but he assured her that he had spoken to her already that evening. Her Ladyship then declared that if he was really the man he gave himself out to be, she was not aware that he was in Paris. She imagined him to be in Ireland. Altogether a peculiar story. Cosmo never knew how it had ended because his friend Hollis led him away to introduce him to Mrs. R., who was most affable and entertained him with a complete inventory of her daughter’s accomplishments, the daughter herself being then in the room, obviously quite lovely and clever, but certainly a little odd; for a little later, on his being introduced, she had discoursed to him for half an hour on things of the heart, charmingly, but in a perfectly cool and detached manner. There was also Lady Jane, very much in evidence, very much run after, with a voice of engaging sweetness, but very free, not to say licentious, in her talk. How could he confide his impressions of her to Henrietta? As a matter of fact his head had been rather full of Lady Jane for some time. She had, so to speak, attended him all the way from Paris up to the morning of his arrival at Cantelucci’s inn. But she had now deserted him. Or was it his mind that had dropped her out of a haunting actuality into that region where the jumble of one’s experiences is allowed to rest? But was it possible that a shabby fellow in tight breeches and bad boots, with a peculiarly shaped cap on his head, could have got between him and Lady Jane about the time of sunset?

Cosmo thought suddenly that one’s personal life was a very bizarre thing. He could write to his sister that before he had been three hours in Genoa he had been involved in passing secret correspondence from Italy to the Island of Elba. Henrietta had solemnly charged him to write everything he could find out, hear, or even guess about Napoleon. He had heard certainly a lot of most extraordinary stories; and if he had not made any guesses he had been associating with persons who actually had been doing nothing else; frightened persons, exulting people, cast-down people, frivolous people, people with airs of mystery or with airs of contempt. But by Jove, now he had been in personal touch and had actually helped a man of the people who was mysteriously corresponding with Elba. He could write something about that but, after all, was it worth while? Finally he concluded he wouldn’t write home at all that evening; pushed the table away, and throwing himself into the armchair extended his legs towards the fire. A moody expression settled on his face. His immobility resembled open-eyed sleep with the red spark of the fire in his unwinking eyes, and a perfect insensibility to outward impressions. But he heard distinctly Spire knocking discreetly at the door. Cosmo’s first impulse was to shout that he wasn’t wanted, but he changed his mind. “Come in.”

===Chinese===
回到卧室，科斯莫发现柴火仍时而舔舐着拉拢的帘子、舔舐着那顶帷幔大床朦胧的轮廓，又察觉斯帕尔（Spire）已遵嘱备好了写字台，在那张看着便诱人的扶手椅四周围起了一架屏风。

他没有坐下写信。他比任何时候都更强烈地感到，自己在一时心情舒畅的当口，对妹妹许下了一个轻率的诺言。这诺言的难处，他是在巴黎头一回领教的。亨丽埃塔（Henrietta）会很愿意听他讲讲所遇之人，讲讲那个上流社会——英国人、法国人、波兰人、德国人——正尽情享受着新近到手的旅行自由。他固然见过不少人；可问题在于，对一个不谙世故、在乡间长大、对世道人心其实毫无概念的少女，能说些什么呢。他以一份自省的坦白对自己承认：就连他自己也不大清楚世道人心究竟是什么样子。他太是个生手，而她呢，显然又太天真，不宜听他那些疑心和所见所闻。便是把那个世界的外表描摹一番，对亨丽埃塔来说也并非易事。那世界自然是好玩的。哦，是的，它好玩；可就在他这般想时，他心里却泛起一丝厌嫌。临离开巴黎之前，他赴过一位贵妇人的盛大晚会。那儿有个家伙不知怎的被人疑心掏人口袋。他生得奇丑，因而格外惹眼。有人问那贵妇人可曾邀过他，她矢口否认曾见过此人，可那人却一口咬定当晚早已与她说过话。夫人阁下于是宣称，他若当真是他所自称的那个人，她可不知他在巴黎；她原以为他在爱尔兰。总归是一桩蹊跷的事。科斯莫始终不知这事结局如何，因为他的朋友霍利斯（Hollis）把他拉走，引他去见 R 太太（Mrs. R.）；这位太太极和蔼，把女儿的一身本事从头到尾给他数了一遍来款待他，而那女儿本人当时就在屋里，模样显然很俏丽，又聪明，却未免有点古怪；因为过不多时，两人一经引见，她竟就谈情说爱之事对他侃侃而谈了半个钟头，颇为迷人，口吻却极其冷静超然。还有那位简小姐（Lady Jane），十分显眼，十分受人簇拥，嗓音甜得迷人，谈吐却极其随便，甚至可以说是放荡。这种种印象，他怎能向亨丽埃塔吐露？事实上，他脑子里装满简小姐已有好些时日了。可以说，从巴黎一路到他抵达坎泰洛奇（Cantelucci）的客栈的那个清晨，她都相伴相随。可如今她已弃他而去。又或者，是他的心把她从一件挥之不去的真事里，丢进了那片任凭各色经历杂沓堆放、暂得安歇的地界？可难道竟有可能，一个紧绷着马裤、蹬着破靴、头上扣一顶模样古怪的帽子的寒碜汉子，竟会在日落前后那段时辰，插到了他与简小姐之间？

科斯莫忽然觉得，一个人的一己生活是件十分古怪的东西。他尽可写信告诉妹妹：他到热那亚（Genoa）不出三个钟头，便已卷入了把秘密信件从意大利送往厄尔巴岛（Elba）的勾当。亨丽埃塔曾郑重地嘱他，把所能打听、听闻、甚至揣测到的关于拿破仑（Napoleon）的一切，都写下来。他确也听了许多极其离奇的故事；纵然他自己不曾作过什么揣测，他却一直在与一些除此之外旁的什么都不做的人交往；惊惶的人，得意的人，颓丧的人，轻浮的人，神神秘秘的人，满脸鄙夷的人。可老天作证，如今他竟已亲身搭上手，实实在在地帮过一个正在与厄尔巴岛暗通款待的平民汉子。关于这个他倒可以写上几句，可说到底，又值得一写吗？末了他断定当晚索性一个字也不写回家；把桌子推开，一屁股跌进扶手椅，把两腿朝火塘伸了出去。一股闷闷的神色落在他脸上。他那一动不动，像是一场睁着眼的睡眠，眼底映着炉火的红光一眨不眨，对外界的一切刺激浑然不觉。可他分明听得斯帕尔在门外恭恭敬敬地敲着。科斯莫的头一个冲动，是想喊一声不用他了，可他又改了主意。「进来。」

===Original===
Spire shut the door carefully, and crossing the room at once put a log on the fire. Then he said:

“Can’t get any hot water this evening, sir. Very sorry, sir. I will see that it won’t happen again.”

At the same time he thought, “Served him right for picking out such an inn to stay at.” Cosmo, still silent, stared at the fire, and when he roused himself at last he perceived Spire in the act of putting down in front of his chair a pair of slippers of shiny leather and red heels.

“Take your boots off, sir?” suggested Spire under his breath.

Cosmo let him do it. “Going to bed now, sir?” asked Spire in the same subdued tone.

“No, but you needn’t wait. I won’t need you any more tonight.”

“Thank you, sir.” Spire lingered, boots in hand. “The two small pistols are on the bedside table, sir. I have looked to the primings. This town is lull of rabble from all parts just now, so I hear. The lock of your door is fairly poor. I shall be sleeping just outside in the corridor, sir. They are going to put me a pallet there.”

“You will be very cold,” protested Cosmo.

“It will be all right, sir. I have got the fur rug out of the carriage. I had everything taken out of the carriage. The yard isn’t safe, sir. Nothing is properly safe in this house, so far as I can see.”

Cosmo nodded absentmindedly. “Oh, wait a moment, Spire. That man, that fellow in the cap, is he still downstairs?”

Spire thought rapidly that he wouldn’t be a party to bringing any of those ragamuffins up to the bedroom. “Gone a long time ago, sir,” he said stolidly.

Cosmo had a vivid recollection of the man’s pose of being settled for an earnest and absorbing conversation to last half the night.

“He doesn’t belong to this house?” he asked.

“No, sir, he only came to talk to a young woman. I left him taking leave of her to come up to you, sir. I suppose he was the man you meant, sir.”

“Yes,” said Cosmo, “I have no doubt about it. He will probably turn up again.”
