侯爵站起身来，开口道：「我想，总而言之，你不把光阴虚掷于此，方为明智。我这里有一封法国驻里窝那（Leghorn）领事的来信，引述了他从厄尔巴岛得到的最新报告。报告说，波拿巴整日整日地把自己关在私室里。所给的缘由是，他惧怕有人从法国和意大利（Italy）派遣密使谋害他的性命。他不见外人。另一份报告则说，近日他对法国与英国巡防舰的动静颇为不安。」

侯爵把一只友善的手搭上科斯莫的肩头。「你无从怨我；我已把最最新的情报给了你。如今让我们去会会小女在接待的客人吧——不论那都是些什么人。我想没几位。」他穿过房间，科斯莫跟在后面，注意到他步态间有一种明显的跛。开门之际，达蒙侯爵说道：

「借你的臂一用，*mon jeune ami*（我年轻的朋友）。今晚我这风湿闹得厉害。」

科斯莫赶忙伸臂相扶，侯爵一手按在门上说：

「我几乎走不动了。但愿我明日还能去赴与撒丁国王的那次觐见。他是个极好的人，可他所有的念头和情感都停滞在了九八年。这使他的一切对答都极为艰难，便是对我亦是如此。他那些大臣倒还讲理些，不过那也只因为他们心怀畏惧罢了。」

一声低沉的呻吟从大使口中逸出。他一手倚着科斯莫的肩，另一手紧紧扣住门把。

「畏惧人民吗？」科斯莫问。

「人民正被各种秘密结社所腐蚀，」侯爵以他那温和的口吻说，「整个意大利都暗流涌动，满是阴谋。可他们最怕的，还是厄尔巴岛上那个人（the Man of Elba）。」

===Original===
Cosmo for an instant wondered at those confidences, but a swift reflection that probably those things were known to everybody who was anybody in Europe made him think that this familiar talk was merely the effect of the Marquis’s kindness to the son of his old friend. “I think I can proceed now,” said the Marquis, pushing the door open. Cosmo recognized one of the rooms which he had passed in the morning. It was the only one of the suite which was fully lighted by a great central glass chandelier, but even in that only two rows of candles were lighted. It was a small reception. The rest of the suite presented but a dim perspective. A semicircle of heavy armchairs was sparsely occupied by less than a dozen ladies. There was only one card table in use. All the faces were turned to the opening door, and Cosmo was struck by the expression of profound surprise on them all. In one or two it resembled thunderstruck imbecility. It didn’t occur to him that the entrance of the French King’s personal representative leaning on the shoulder of a completely unknown young man was enough to cause a sensation. A group of elderly personages, conversing in a remote part of the room, became silent. The Marquis gave a general greeting by an inclination of his head, and Cosmo felt himself impelled towards a console between two windows against which the Marquis leaned, whispering to him, “If I were to sit down it would be such an affair to get up.” The Countess de Montevesso advanced quickly across the room. Cosmo noticed that her dress had a long train. She smiled at Cosmo and said to the Marquis anxiously:

“You are in pain, Papa?”

“A little.… Take him away, my dear, now. He was good enough to lend me his shoulder as far as this.”

“*Venez*, M. Latham,” said Adèle, “I must introduce you at once to Lady William Bentick in order to check wild speculation about the appearance of a mysterious stranger. As it is, all the town will be full of rumours. People will be talking about you this very night.”

===Chinese===
科斯莫一时对这些推心置腹的话暗暗纳罕，可转念一想，这些事大约是欧洲但凡有头有脸的人无不知晓的，便又觉得这番熟不拘礼的闲话，不过是侯爵对老友之子的一片厚意罢了。「我想我现在能走了，」侯爵说着推开了门。科斯莫认出这是他早晨经过的房间之一。这一套房间里，唯独这一间被一盏巨大的中央玻璃吊灯照得通明，可即便是那盏灯，也只点着两排蜡烛。这是一场小型的招待会。其余各间只呈现出一派朦胧的纵深。一圈沉甸甸的扶手椅稀稀落落地坐着不到一打夫人小姐。正在使用的牌桌只有一张。所有的脸都转向那扇推开的门，科斯莫被他们脸上那种深深的惊讶之色震了一下。其中一两位脸上的神情，简直像遭了雷击的呆痴。他并未想到，法国国王的私人代表倚在一个完全无人知晓的年轻人肩头登场，本身就足以引起一场轰动。房间远处一群年长的人物正谈着话，一下子静了下来。侯爵点了点头，作了一个总的招呼，科斯莫只觉自己被引着走向两窗之间的一张小案，侯爵也倚了过去，对他耳语道：「我要是坐下，再要站起来可就麻烦了。」蒙特韦索伯爵夫人（The Countess de Montevesso）快步穿过房间而来。科斯莫注意到她的裙裾拖着长长的后摆。她对科斯莫笑了笑，又急切地对侯爵说：

「您疼吗，爸爸？」

「有一点。……亲爱的，把他带走吧，这会儿就走。他好心肠，借我一条膀子，一直扶我到这里。」

「*Venez*（来），莱瑟姆先生，」阿黛勒（Adèle）说，「我得立刻把您引见给威廉·本蒂克夫人（Lady William Bentick），好煞住那一场对一个神秘来客之出现的胡乱猜疑。就这样，全城也都要谣言四起了。今夜便会有人议论您。」

===Original===
Cosmo followed Adèle across the room. She moved slowly and talked easily with a flattering air of intimacy. She even stopped for a moment under the great chandelier. “Lady William is talking now with Count Bubna,” she explained to Cosmo, who took a rapid survey of a tall, stout man in an Austrian general’s uniform, with his hair tied up in a queue, with black moustaches and something cynical though not ill-natured in his expression. That personage interrupted suddenly his conversation with a lady, no longer very young, who was dressed very simply, and made his way to the ambassador, giving in passing a faintly caustic smile and a keen glance to Cosmo.

“Let me introduce to you Mr. Cosmo Latham,” said Adèle. “He is the son of my father’s very old friend. He and I haven’t met since we were children together in Yorkshire. He has just arrived here.”

Cosmo bowed, and in response to a slight gesture took a seat close to the lady, whose preoccupied air struck him with a sort of wonder. She seemed to have something on her mind. Cosmo could know nothing of the prevalent gossip that it was only the black eyes of Louise Durazzo that were detaining Lord William in Italy. He explained in answer to a careless inquiry as to the latest news from Paris that he had been travelling very leisurely and that he could not possibly have brought any fresh news. Lady William looked at him as if she had not seen him before.

“Oh, I am not very much interested in the news, except in so far that they may make a longer stay here unnecessary for us.”

“I suppose everybody wants to see the shape of the civilized world settled at last,” said Cosmo politely.

“All I want is to go home,” declared Lady William. She was no longer looking at him and had the appearance of a person not anxious to listen to anybody’s conversation. Cosmo glanced about the room. The card game had been resumed. The Austrian general was talking to the Marquis with Madame de Montevesso standing close to them, while other persons kept at a respectful distance. Lady William seemed to be following her own thoughts with a sort of impassive abstraction. Cosmo felt himself at liberty to go on with his observations, and sweeping his glance round noticed, sitting half hidden by the back of the armchair Adèle had vacated, the dark girl with round black eyes, whom he had seen that morning. To his extreme surprise she smiled at him and, not content with that, gave other plain signs of recognition. He thought he could do no less than get up and make her a bow. By the time he sat down again he became aware that he had attracted the notice of all the ladies seated before the fire. One of them put up her eyeglasses to look at him, two others started talking low together with side glances in his direction, and there was not one that did not look interested. This disturbed him much less than the fixed stare of the young creature, which became fastened on him unwinkingly. Even Lady William gave him a short look of curiosity.

===Chinese===
科斯莫随着阿黛勒穿过房间。她步子缓慢，谈吐从容，带着一种令人受宠若惊的亲昵神气。她甚至在那盏大吊灯底下停了一停。「威廉夫人此刻正与布布纳伯爵（Count Bubna）谈话，」她向科斯莫解说，科斯莫趁机飞速打量了一下那个高大的胖子——他穿着一身奥地利将军的军服，头发扎成一条辫子，唇上两撇黑胡子，神色之间带着几分讥讽，却并无恶意。那位大人物忽然打断自己与一位女士——她已不再很年轻，衣着十分素朴——的谈话，朝大使走去，路过时向科斯莫投来一抹淡淡的冷笑和一道锐利的目光。

「让我给您引见科斯莫·莱瑟姆先生（Mr. Cosmo Latham），」阿黛勒说，「他是我父亲一位至交老友的儿子。我和他自孩提时在约克郡（Yorkshire）一道相处之后，就再没见过。他刚刚才到此地。」

科斯莫鞠了一躬，回应对方一个微微的手势，便在那位夫人身边落座，她那一副心事重重的模样令他颇觉诧异。她似乎心里压着什么事。科斯莫自然无从知晓那流传甚广的闲话——说挽留威廉勋爵（Lord William）留在意大利的，无非是路易丝·杜拉佐（Louise Durazzo）那双黑眼睛。他应着她一句随口的、关于巴黎最新消息的探问，答说自己一路走得十分从容，断不可能带来什么新鲜消息。威廉夫人望着他，仿佛先前并未见过他这个人。

「哦，我对那些消息不甚关心，除非它们能使我们在此地多住变得毫无必要。」

「我想，人人都想看看这文明世界最终落得个什么模样吧。」科斯莫客气地说。

「我所想的，不过是回家。」威廉夫人宣称。她已不再看他，一副并不想听任何人对答的神气。科斯莫环视了一下房间。牌局已经恢复。那位奥地利将军正与侯爵谈着话，蒙特韦索夫人挨在他们身边，旁人则都保持着一段恭敬的距离。威廉夫人似乎沉浸在自己的一缕思绪里，一副不动声色的出神之态。科斯莫觉得自己尽可继续他这一番观察，目光一扫，便注意到阿黛勒让出的那张扶手椅椅背后面，半隐半现地坐着早晨他所见过的那个深肤色的圆眼睛姑娘。令他大为吃惊的是，她竟冲他一笑，不仅如此，还明白无误地给了他别种认出他的表示。他想自己少不得起身向她鞠上一躬。等他重新坐下，他才觉察自己已引起了炉前坐着的那所有夫人小姐的注意。其中一位举起长柄眼镜来看他，另外两位开始交头接耳，斜着眼朝他这边瞟，没有一张脸不带几分兴味。可这些都不如那年轻女子定定的凝视叫他不得安宁，那凝视一落在他身上，便一眨不眨。便连威廉夫人也向他投来短短的一道好奇的目光。

===Original===
“I understand that you have just arrived in Genoa.”

“Yes. Yesterday afternoon late. This is my first appearance.”

He meant that it was his first appearance in society and he continued:

“And I don’t know a single person in this room even by name. Of course I know that it is Count Bubna who is talking to the Marquis, but that is all.”

“Ah,” said Lady William with a particular intonation which made Cosmo wonder what he could have said to provoke scepticism. But Lady William was asking herself how it was that this young Englishman seemed to be familiar with the freakish girl who was an object of many surmises in Genoa, and whose company, it was understood, Count Helion of Montevesso had imposed upon his wife. Meantime Cosmo, with the eyes of all the women concentrated upon him with complete frankness, began to feel uncomfortable. Lady William noticed it and out of pure kindness spoke to him again.

“If I understood rightly you have known Madame de Montevesso from childhood.”

“I can’t call myself really a childhood’s friend. I was so much away from home,” explained Cosmo. “But she lived for some years in my parents’ house and everybody loved her there; my mother, my father, my sister—and it seems to me, looking back now, that I too must have loved her at that time; though we very seldom exchanged more than a few words in the course of the day.”

===Chinese===
「我听说您刚刚抵达热那亚。」

「是的。昨日下午很晚。这是我头一回露面。」

他这话的意思是说他头一回在社交场合露面，于是又接着说：

「这房里的人，我连一个也不认得，连名字也叫不上来。我当然知道此刻与侯爵谈话的是布布纳伯爵，可仅此而已。」

「啊，」威廉夫人以一种异样的声调应了一声，叫科斯莫纳罕自己究竟说了什么竟能招来这般怀疑。可威廉夫人心里正在想的是：这年轻的英国人怎么竟像是与那个古怪的姑娘相熟呢——那姑娘是热那亚许多揣测的对象，而众所周知，是蒙特韦索的埃利翁伯爵（Count Helion of Montevesso）硬把这个姑娘塞给妻子作伴的。与此同时，所有那些女子的目光都毫不遮掩地集中在他身上，科斯莫开始觉得不自在了。威廉夫人注意到了，出于一片纯然的好意，又开口对他说话。

「要是我没理解错的话，您自小便认识蒙特韦索夫人。」

「说不上真是童年的玩伴。我那时常常不在家，」科斯莫解释道，「可她在我父母府上住了好几年，那里人人都喜欢她——我母亲、我父亲、我妹妹——如今回头看来，我想那时连我也必定是爱过她的；纵然在一日之中，我们难得交换上比三言两语更多的话。」

===Original===
He spoke with feeling and glanced in the direction of the group near the console where the head of Adèle appeared radiant under the sparkling crystals of the lustre. Lady William, bending sideways a little, leaned her cheek against her hand in a listening attitude. Cosmo felt that he was expected to go on speaking, but it seemed to him that he had nothing more to say. He fell back upon a general remark.

“I think boys are very stupid creatures. However, I wasn’t so stupid as not to feel that Adèle d’Armand was very intelligent and quite different from us all. Her very gentleness set her apart. Moreover, Henrietta and I were younger. To my sister and myself she seemed almost grown up. A couple of years makes a very great difference at that age. Soon after she went away we children heard that she was married. She seemed lost to us then. Presently she went back to France, and once there she was lost indeed. When one looked towards France in those days it seems to me there was nothing to be seen but Napoleon. And then her marriage, too. A Countess de Montevesso didn’t mean anything to us. I came here expecting to see a stranger.”

Cosmo checked himself. It was impossible to say whether Lady William had heard him, or even whether she had been listening at all, but she asked:

“You never met Count Helion?”

“I haven’t the slightest idea of the man. He is not in this room, is he? What is he like?”

Lady William looked amused for a moment at the artless curiosity of the Countess de Montevesso’s young friend; but it was in an indifferent tone that she said:

“Count Helion is a man of immense wealth which he amassed in India somewhere. He is much older than his wife. More than twice her age.” Cosmo showed his surprise, and Lady William continued smoothly: “Of course all the world knows that Adèle has been a model wife.”

Cosmo noted the faintest possible shade of emphasis on those last words and thought to himself: “That means she is not happy and that the world knows it.” But, several men having approached the circle, the conversation became general. He vacated his seat by the side of Lady William and got introduced by Adèle to several people, amongst whom was a delicate young woman splendidly dressed and of a slightly Jewish type who, though she was the wife of General Count Bubna, commander-in-chief of the Austrian troops and the representative of Austria at the Court of Turin, behaved with a strange timidity and appeared almost too shy to speak. A simple Madame Ferrati, or so at least Cosmo heard her name, a lady with white tousled hair, had an aggressive manner. Cosmo remarked in the course of the evening that she seemed rather to be persecuting Lady William, who, however, remained amiably abstracted and did not seem to mind anything. The Marquis, getting away from the console, had seated himself near the little Madame Bubna. This, Cosmo thought, was an unavoidable sort of thing for him to do. A young man with a grave manner and something malicious in his eye, apparently a First Secretary of the Embassy, informed Cosmo shortly after they had been made known to each other that “the wife of the general would not naturally be received in Vienna society,” and that this was the secret of Bubna sticking to his Italian command so long, even now when really all the excitement was over. Of course he was very much in love with his wife. He used to give her balls twice a week at the expense of the Turin Municipality. Old Bubna understood the art of pillaging to perfection, but apart from that he was a *parfait galant homme* and an able soldier. Bonaparte had a very great liking for him. Bubna was the only friend Bonaparte had in this room. He meant sympathy as man for man. Years ago when Bubna was in Paris he got on very well with the Emperor. Bonaparte knew how to flatter a man. It was worth while to sit up half the night to hear Bubna talking about Bonaparte. “I am posting you up like this,” concluded the secretary, “because I see you are in the intimacy of the Marquis and of Madame de Montevesso here.”

===Chinese===
他这话说得动情，朝小案旁那一群人那边瞟了一眼，那儿，阿黛勒的头在吊灯璀璨的水晶之下显得容光焕发。威廉夫人侧过身去，把脸颊倚在手上，一副倾听的模样。科斯莫觉得自己被盼着继续说下去，可他想自己再没什么好说的了。他便落回到一句泛泛之论上。

「我想，男孩子都是些蠢东西。不过，我倒还不至于蠢到感觉不出阿黛勒·达蒙（Adèle d’Armand）极聪明，与我们所有人都大不相同。单是她那份温柔，就使她与众不同。何况亨利埃塔（Henrietta）和我都更小。在我妹妹和我看来，她简直已是大人了。那个年纪上差上两岁，便是天差地别。她走后不久，我们几个孩子便听说她嫁了人。那时她于我们而言便像是失掉了。不久她又回了法国，一旦到了那里，她于我们便当真是失掉了。那些年里，一个人朝法国那边望去，依我看，除了拿破仑（Napoleon），什么也看不见。还有她那桩婚事。一个蒙特韦索伯爵夫人（Countess de Montevesso）对我们而言算不得什么。我到这里来，本以为会见到一个陌生人。」

科斯莫收住了话。威廉夫人究竟听见没有、甚至究竟有没有在听，都无从判断，可她问道：

「您从没见过埃利翁伯爵吗？」

「我对此人全无概念。他不在这房里，是吗？他生得什么模样？」

威廉夫人一时露出好笑的神气，笑这位蒙特韦索伯爵夫人这位年轻朋友的率直好奇；可她接下去用的是一副淡然的口吻：

「埃利翁伯爵是个豪富之人，他的家财是在印度（India）什么地方攒下的。他比妻子年长许多。年龄是她两倍还多。」科斯莫露出惊讶之色，威廉夫人顺顺当当地接下去：「当然，全天下都知道，阿黛勒一直是个模范妻子。」

科斯莫察觉到她最后这几个字上压着最最轻淡不过的一丝重音，心里便想：「那就是说她并不快乐，而天下人也都知道这一点。」可这时已有几位男士走近这个圈子，谈话变得众声喧哗起来。他让出威廉夫人身边的座位，由阿黛勒引见了好几个人，其中有一位娇弱而衣着华美的年轻女子，带着些许犹太人的形貌特征——她虽是布布纳将军伯爵（General Count Bubna）的妻子，而这位将军是奥地利军队的总司令、奥地利驻都灵宫廷的代表，举止却带着一种古怪的怯意，几乎羞怯得开不了口。一位普通的费拉蒂夫人（Madame Ferrati）——至少科斯莫把她的名字听成这样——一位头发花白蓬乱的夫人，举止咄咄逼人。科斯莫在这一晚间留意到，她简直像是在纠缠威廉夫人，而威廉夫人却始终和蔼地出着神，似乎什么事都不放在心上。侯爵已从小案旁走开，在那位娇小的布布纳夫人身边坐下。科斯莫想，他这样做也是无可避免的一桩应酬。一位举止端方、眼里却含着几分促狭之气的年轻人——显然是大使馆的一位首席秘书——在与科斯莫互通姓名之后不久便告诉他，「将军夫人按理是进不了维也纳社交圈的」，而这正是布布纳之所以死死攥着他这意大利军职不放的奥妙，便是在如今这一阵风潮其实都已过去之时。他当然极爱他的妻子。他过去每周两次为她举办舞会，费用由都灵市政厅（Turin Municipality）承担。老布布纳把搜刮的艺术讲究到出神入化，可撇开这一点不谈，他确是个 *parfait galant homme*（十足的君子），也是一员能干的将领。波拿巴对他颇为青眼。布布纳是这房里波拿巴唯一的朋友。这是说人与人之间的那份惺惺相惜。多年前布布纳在巴黎（Paris）时，他与皇帝处得十分融洽。波拿巴懂得如何奉承一个人。为了听布布纳讲波拿巴，熬上半宿也是值得的。「我把这些一一灌输给您，」那秘书最后说，「是因为我看得出来，您与这里的侯爵、与蒙特韦索夫人交情不浅。」

===Original===
He went away then to talk to somebody else, and presently Madame de Montevesso, passing close to Cosmo, whispered to him, “Stay to the last,” and went on without waiting for his answer. Cosmo amongst all the groups engaged in animated conversation felt rather lonely, totally estranged from the ideas those people were expressing to each other. He could not possibly be in sympathy with the fears and the hopes, strictly personal, and with the royalist-legitimist enthusiasms of these advocates of an order of things that had been buried for a quarter of a century and now was paraded like a rouged and powdered corpse putting on a swagger of life and revenge. Then he reflected that in this room, at any rate, it was probably nothing but scandalous gossip and trivial talk of futile intrigues. There was no need for him to be indignant. He was even amused at himself, and looking about him in a kindlier frame of mind he perceived that the person nearest to him was that strange girl with the round eyes. She had kept perfectly still on her uncomfortable stool like a captured savage. Her green flounced skirt was spread on each side of the seat. The bodice of her dress, which was black, was cut low, her bare arms were youthfully red and immature. Her hair was done up smoothly and pulled up from her forehead in the manner of the portraits of the 15th Century.

“Why do they dress her in this bizarre manner?” thought Cosmo. It couldn’t be Adèle’s conception. Perhaps of the Count himself. Yet that didn’t seem likely. Perhaps it was her own atrocious taste. But if so it ought to have been repressed. He reflected that there could be nothing improper in him talking to the niece of the house. He would try his conversational Italian. With the feeling of venturing on a doubtful experiment he approached her from the back, sat down at her elbow, and waited. She could not possibly remain unaware of him being there.

===Chinese===
他随即走开去与旁人谈话，过了一阵，蒙特韦索夫人从科斯莫身边经过，低声对他说了一句：「留到最后再走，」便不等他回答径自走了过去。科斯莫置身于一群群谈得正热闹的人中间，颇觉孤单，与这些人彼此交换的种种见解完全格格不入。他不可能去同情这些人那种种纯属一己的患得患失，也不可能同情这些保皇正统派的热忱——他们所鼓吹的那种秩序，已埋葬了四分之一世纪，如今却被抬出来招摇过市，活像一具涂脂抹粉的尸体，摆出一副复活与复仇的气焰。可转念他又想，在这间屋里，无论如何，多半也不过是些飞短流长的闲话，和那些徒劳阴谋的鸡零狗碎罢了。他也犯不着为之愤慨。他甚至被自己引得发笑，便以一种较为和悦的心境四下打量，发觉离他最近的那个人，便是那个长着一双圆眼睛的怪姑娘。她一直一动不动地坐在那张不舒服的小凳子上，活像一个被俘的野人。她那条缀褶的绿裙子在座位的两边摊开。她那件黑色的上衣领口开得很低，两条赤裸的胳膊带着少年的红润，尚未长成。她的头发梳得光光的，从额前往上拢起，是十五世纪画像里的那种样式。

「他们怎么把她打扮成这般古怪的模样？」科斯莫心想。这不可能是阿黛勒的主意。也许是伯爵本人的。可这似乎又不大像。也许这是她自己骇人的趣味。但若当真如此，也该加以压制才对。他转念又想，他与这家里的侄女攀谈几句，原不会有什么不妥。他要试一试自己那点能应酬的意大利语。怀着一种踏上一次吉凶未卜之尝试的心情，他从她背后走上前去，在她肘边坐下，等着。她不可能不发觉他在那儿。

===Original===
At last she turned her head for a point-blank stare, and once she had her eyes on him she never attempted to take them away. Cosmo uttered carefully a complimentary phrase about her dress, which was received in perfect silence. Her carmine lips remained as still as her round black eyes for quite a long time. Suddenly in a low tone, with an accent which surprised Cosmo but which he supposed to be Piedmontese, and with a sort of spiteful triumph, she said:

“I knew very well it would suit me. You think it does?”

Her whole personality had such an aggressive mien that Cosmo, startled and amused, hastened to say, “Undoubtedly,” lest she should fly at his eyes.

She showed him her teeth in a grin of savage complacency, and the subject seemed exhausted. Cosmo set himself the task to daunt her by a steady gaze. In less than two seconds he regretted his venture. He felt certain that she would not be the one to look away first. There was not the slightest doubt about that. In order to cover his retreat he let his eyes wander vaguely about the room, smiled agreeably, and said: “Your uncle is not here. Shall I have the pleasure of seeing him this evening?”

“No,” she said. “You won’t see him this evening. But he knows you have been here this morning.”

This was, strictly speaking, news to Cosmo, but he said at once and with great indifference:

“Why shouldn’t he? Probably Madame de Montevesso has told him. I used to know your aunt when she was younger than you are, signorina.”

“How do you know how old I am?”

Cosmo asked himself if she would ever wink those black eyes of hers.

“I know that you are not a hundred years old.”

This struck her as humorous, because there was a sound as of a faint giggle which, generally speaking, is a silly kind of sound but in her case had a disturbing quality. It was followed by the hoarse declaration:

“Aunt didn’t. I told Uncle. I looked a lot at you in the morning. Why didn’t you look at me?”

“I was afraid of being indiscreet,” said Cosmo readily, concealing his astonishment.

“What silliness,” she commented scornfully. “And this evening too! I was looking at you all the time and you did nothing but look at all those witches here, one after another.”

“I find all the ladies in the room perfectly charming,” said Cosmo.

“You lie. I suppose you do nothing else from morning till night.”

“I am sorry you have such a bad opinion of me, but it being what it is, hadn’t I better go away?”

“Directly I set eyes on you I knew you were one of that sort.”

“And did you impart your opinion of me to your uncle?” asked Cosmo. He could be no more offended with that girl than if she had been an unmannerly animal. Her peculiar stare remained unchanged but her general expression softened for a moment.

“No. But I took care to tell him that you were a very handsome gentleman.… You are a very handsome gentleman.”

===Chinese===
终于她转过头来，直勾勾盯着他，一旦把眼睛搭在他身上，便再不肯移开。科斯莫字斟句酌地说了一句恭维她衣裳的话，得到的回应是一片彻底的沉默。她那两片殷红的嘴唇，与那双圆圆的黑眼睛一样一动不动，足有相当一阵子。忽然，她以一个低低的声调、一种令科斯莫诧异但他想是皮埃蒙特（Piedmontese）口音的腔调，带着几分恶意的得意说道：

「我早知道它合我身。你也觉得合吗？」

她整个人的做派是那样咄咄逼人，科斯莫又惊又好笑，赶忙说道：「毫无疑问。」唯恐她扑上来抓他的眼睛。

她冲他龇了龇牙，一副野蛮而自得的狞笑，这话题似乎就到此为止了。科斯莫给自己定下一个任务，要用一道不稍瞬息的目光把她压服。不到两秒钟，他便后悔这一尝试了。他确信，先挪开眼睛的绝不会是她。这一点毫无可疑。为了掩饰自己的退却，他任自己的眼睛在房里漫无目的地游移，和悦地一笑，说道：「您伯父不在这里。今晚我能有幸见到他吗？」

「不能，」她说，「您今晚见不着他。可他知道您今早来过。」

严格说来，这对科斯莫是条新闻，可他当即以一种十分淡然的口吻说：

「他怎么会不知道？多半是蒙特韦索夫人告诉他的。我认识您伯母时，她比您现在的年纪还小呢，signorina（小姐）。」

「您怎么知道我多大？」

科斯莫暗自纳闷，她那双黑眼睛到底会不会眨一眨。

「我只知道您没有一百岁。」

这话在她听来是幽默的，因为随之而来的是一声轻笑似的声音，而轻笑这种声音一般说来是愚蠢的，可从她嘴里出来却带着一种扰人心神的特质。紧随其后的，是那声嘶哑的宣告：

「伯母没告诉他。是我告诉伯父的。早上我瞧了您好半天。您怎么不瞧我？」

「我怕自己太冒失，」科斯莫顺口应道，掩饰着自己的惊讶。

「多蠢，」她鄙夷地评说道，「今儿晚上也是！我一直在瞧您，您却一个劲儿地瞧这屋里所有那些妖精，一个接一个。」

「我觉得这屋里所有的小姐夫人都美极了。」科斯莫说。

「您撒谎。我瞧您从早到晚别的不干，就干这个。」

「您对我竟有这般坏看法，我实在抱歉，可既然如此，我还是走开的好，不是吗？」

「我一瞧见您，就知道您是那号人。」

「那您把对我的看法告诉您伯父了吗？」科斯莫问。他对这姑娘生不起气来，就像对一头没规矩的牲口生不起气来一样。她那种独特的盯视仍无改变，可她整个神情一时柔和了一些。

「没有。可我特意告诉他，您是位非常英俊的绅士。……您是位非常英俊的绅士。」

===Original===
What surprised Cosmo was not the downright statement but the thought that flashed through his mind that it was as dreadful as being told that one was good to eat. For a time he stared without any thought of unwinking competition. He was not amused. Distinctly not. He asked:

“Where were you born?”

“How can I tell? In the mountains, I suppose. Somewhere where you will never go. How can it possibly concern you?”

Cosmo offered his apology for his indiscretion, and she received it with a sort of uncomprehending scorn. She said after a pause: “None of those witches, young or old, ever speak to me. And even you didn’t want to speak to me. You only spoke to me… Oh, no! I know why you spoke to me.”

“Why did I speak to you?” asked Cosmo thoughtfully. “Won’t you tell me?”

Upon the firm roundness of that high-coloured face came a subtle change which suggested something in the nature of cunning, and the rough, somewhat veiled voice came from between the red lips which had no more charm or life than the painted lips carved in a piece of wood.

“If I were to tell you would be as wise as myself.”

“Where would be the harm of me being as wise as yourself?” said Cosmo, trying to be playful but somehow missing the tone of playfulness so completely that he was struck by his failure himself.

“If you were as wise as myself you would never come to this house again and I don’t want you to stay away,” was the answer, delivered in a hostile tone.

Cosmo said, “You don’t! Well, at any rate it can’t be because of kindness, so I don’t thank you for it.” He said this with extreme amiability. Becoming aware that people were beginning to leave, he observed, out of the corner of his eye, that nobody went away without glancing in their direction. Then the departure of Lady William caused a general stir and gave Cosmo the occasion to get up and move away. Lady William gave him a gracious nod, and the Marquis, coming up to him, introduced him at the last moment to General Count Bubna just as that distinguished person was making ready to take his wife away. Everybody was standing up and for the first time Cosmo felt himself completely unobserved. Obeying a discreet sign of the Countess Montevesso, he moved unaffectedly in the direction of a closed door, the white and gold door he remembered well from his morning visit. When he had got near to it and within reach of the handle he turned about. He had the view of the guests’ backs as they moved slowly out. Adèle looked over her shoulder for a moment with an affirmative nod. He understood it, hesitated no longer, opened the door, and slipped through without, so far as he could judge, being seen by anybody.

It was as he had thought. He found himself in Madame de Montevesso’s boudoir in which he had been received that morning.

===Chinese===
让科斯莫吃惊的，倒不是这直截了当的一句话，而是脑中倏忽一闪的念头——这话听着，竟如同被告知说一个人「吃起来味道不错」一样可怖。有一阵子，他直盯着她，全无与人比试不眨眼的念头。他并不觉得有趣。分明不觉得有趣。他问：

「您生在哪里？」

「我怎么知道？大概是在山里吧。一个您永远不会去的地方。这关您什么事呢？」

科斯莫为自己的失礼道了歉，而她带着一种莫名其妙的不屑受了这番歉意。她顿了一顿才说：「那些妖精，不论老少，没有一个肯跟我说话。便连您也并不想跟我说话。您之所以跟我说话……哦，不对！我知道您为什么跟我说话。」

「我为什么跟您说话？」科斯莫若有所思地问，「您不肯告诉我吗？」

那张颜色浓重、线条结实的圆脸上，掠过一丝细微的变化，像是某种狡黠的东西，那粗嘎的、略带些喑哑的声音，从那两片红唇之间发出来——那两片红唇，并不比一段木头上雕出来的涂了颜色的嘴唇更有生气或魅力。

「要是我告诉了您，您就和我一样明白了。」

「我和您一样明白，又有什么坏处呢？」科斯莫说，想让自己俏皮些，却不知怎的把俏皮的调子丢失得干干净净，连他自己都为自己这一失败而吃惊。

「您要是和我一样明白，就再也不会到这屋里来了，可我又不想您不来，」她以敌意的口吻答道。

科斯莫说：「您不想！好吧，无论如何，这总不会是出于好意，那我也就不谢您了。」他这话说得极其和蔼。他发觉人们开始告辞，便用眼角余光注意到，没有人离开时不朝他们这边瞟上一眼。随后威廉夫人的告辞引起了一阵总的骚动，给了科斯莫起身走开的机会。威廉夫人向他优雅地颔首为礼，侯爵也走到他跟前，在最后关头把他引见给布布纳将军伯爵，那时这位显赫的人物正准备带妻子离开。人人都站了起来，科斯莫头一次觉得自己彻底无人注目了。他遵从蒙特韦索伯爵夫人一个隐秘的眼色，若无其事地朝一扇关着的门走去——那扇白底描金的门，他早晨来访时记得清清楚楚。等他走到门边、伸手够得着门把的时候，他回过头去。他望见宾客们正缓缓地走出去，留给他一脊背的背影。阿黛勒回过头来望了他一眼，肯定地点了点头。他懂得了这一眼，再不犹豫，推开门，悄无声息地溜了过去——据他所能判断的，没有被任何人看见。

果然如他所想。他发现自己置身于蒙特韦索夫人的小起居室里，正是早晨他被接见的那个地方。

### IV / IV

===Original===
He shut the door behind him gently and remained between it and the screen. He had expected to be followed at once by Adèle. What could be detaining her? But he remembered the remarkable proportions of that suite of reception rooms. He had seen some apartments in Paris, but nothing quite so long as that. The old Marquis would no doubt conduct the little Madame Bubna to the very door of the anteroom. The ambassador of The Most Christian King owed that attention to the representative of His Apostolic Majesty and Commander-in-Chief of the Austrian troops. This was the exact form which his thought took. The Christian King, the Apostolic Majesty﻿—all those submerged heads were bobbing up out of the subsiding flood.

He pictured them to himself in their mental simplicity and with their grand air; the Marquis magnificent and ageing, and the dutiful daughter by his side with her radiant head and her divine form. It was impossible to believe that these two had also been submerged at one time.

All those people were mere playthings, reflected Cosmo without a pang. But who or what was playing with them? he thought further, boldly, and remained for a moment as if amused by the marvellousness of it, in the manner of people watching the changes on the stage. But what could have become of them?

She might next moment be opening the door. Could she have made him stay behind because she wanted to speak with him alone? Why, yes, obviously. Cosmo did not ask himself what she wanted to talk to him about. It was no wonder that he felt, it was a subtle emotion resembling impatience for the arrival of a promised felicity of an indefinite kind. All this was by no means poignant. It was merely delightfully disturbing.

===Chinese===
他随手轻轻把门带上，留在门与屏风之间。他原以为阿黛勒会立刻跟着进来。是什么把她绊住了？可他随即想起那一整套待客厅堂那与众不同的格局。他在巴黎也见过几处宅邸，却没有一处像那样长得没边的。那位老侯爵（Marquis）无疑会把小小的布布纳夫人（Madame Bubna）一直送到前厅门口。至慈至圣的基督君王（The Most Christian King）的大使，理当向使徒陛下（His Apostolic Majesty）兼奥地利全军总司令的代表致此礼数。这正是他这一念头所采取的确切形式。基督君王、使徒陛下——所有那些一度被淹没的人头，此刻都正从退去的洪水里浮上来。

他在心里描摹着他们那种心智上的单纯与那副雍容的气派；那位侯爵气宇轩昂而年华已老，身旁是尽孝的女儿，一头光艳的秀发，一具宛若天赐的身段。要说这两位也曾一度被那洪水淹没过，实在叫人难以置信。

那些人全都不过是玩物罢了，科斯莫这样思忖着，心里没有一丝难过。可又是谁、或者什么东西，在把他们当作玩物呢？他更进一步放胆想下去，怔了片刻，仿佛被这事儿的奇妙所逗乐，就像看客瞧着戏台上的变故那样。可他们究竟怎么了？

她也许下一刻便会推门进来。莫非她让他留下来，是想同他单独说几句话？是啊，显然是如此。科斯莫并没有去问她要同他说些什么。他此刻心里起的，是一种微妙的心绪，像是对一桩许诺过的、却又说不清是什么的福分将至的焦盼，这原是再自然不过的事。这一切都谈不上多么揪心。它只是叫人愉悦地坐立不安罢了。

===Original===
“I shall have a tête-à-tête; that’s clear,” he thought, as he advanced into the room. The air all around him was delightfully warm. Whatever she would have to say would be wonderful because of her voice. He would look her in the face. She did not intimidate him and it was impossible to have too much of that. After all, he thought, immensely amused, it was only Adèle, Ad﻿—

His mental monologue was cut short by the shock of perceiving, seated on the painted sofa, a man who was looking at him in perfect silence and immobility. The fact was that Count Helion, having come into the boudoir sooner than his wife had expected him to do, had directed his eyes to the screen ever since he had heard the opening and the shutting of the door. One of his hands was resting on his thigh, the other hung down holding negligently a number of some gazette which was partly resting on the floor. Though not very big, that piece of paper attracted Cosmo’s eyes; and it was in this way that he became aware of the brown fingers covered with rings, of the gaunt legs encased in silk stockings, and of the crossed feet in dress shoes with gold buckles, almost before he took in the impression of the broad but lean face which seemed to have been stained with walnut juice long enough for the stain to have worn down thin, letting the native pallor come through. The same tint extended to the bald top of the head. But what was really extraordinary was the hair: two patches of black behind each temple, obviously dyed. The man, as to whose identity Cosmo could have no doubt, got up, displaying the full length of his bony frame, in a tense and soldierly stiffness associated with cross-belts and a cowhide knapsack on the back. “A grenadier,” thought Cosmo, startled by this unexpected meeting, which also caused him profound annoyance, as though he had been induced to walk into a trap. What he could not understand was why the man should make that grimace at him. It convulsed his whole physiognomy, involving his lips, his cheeks, and his very eyes in a sort of spasm. The most awful thing was that it stayed there.﻿ ﻿… “Why, it’s a smile,” thought Cosmo, with sudden relief. It was so sudden that it broke into a smile without any particular volition of his own. Thereupon the face of Count Helion recovered its normal aspect and Cosmo heard his voice for the first time. It proceeded from the depths of his chest. It was resonant and blurred and portentous with an effect of stiffness somehow in accord with the man’s bearing. It informed Cosmo that Count Helion had been waiting in the Countess’s boudoir on purpose to make his acquaintance, while in the man’s eyes there was a watchfulness as though he had been uttering a momentous disclosure and was anxious as to its effect. A perfectly horizontal, jet-black moustache underlining the nose of Count Helion, which was broad at the base and thin at the end, suggested comic possibilities in that head, which had too much individuality to be looked upon by Cosmo simply as the head of Adèle’s husband; and Cosmo hardly looked at it in that light. His hold on that fact was slippery. He preserved his equanimity perfectly and said that he himself had wondered whether he would have the pleasure of making the Count’s acquaintance that evening. Both men sat down.

===Chinese===
「我要和她单独谈一谈了；这是明摆着的。」他一边向屋里走去，一边心想。他周身的空气暖得宜人。无论她要说什么，都必是动听的，因为她的声音便是如此。他要直视她的脸。她并不叫他畏惧，而这份坦然，是怎么也嫌不够的。说到底，他想道，觉得有趣极了，那毕竟是阿黛勒，阿黛——

他这番心里的独白，被一个意外的发现骤然打断：那张漆过的沙发上居然坐着一个人，正无声无息、一动不动地望着他。事情的原委是，海利翁伯爵（Count Helion）比他妻子所预料的更早进了这间小客厅，自打听见门开又关上的那一声起，便把目光投向了那道屏风。他一只手搁在大腿上，另一只垂着，随手捏着一卷什么公报之类的刊物，一端半拖在地板上。那片纸虽不大，却把科斯莫的目光吸了过去；他正是这样才留意到那戴满戒指的褐色手指、那裹在丝袜里的瘦长腿，以及那双交叉着、蹬在缀金扣礼鞋里的脚——这一切，几乎都在他形成对那张脸的印象之前便已落进眼里：那张宽阔却瘦削的脸，仿佛曾被核桃汁长久地染过，染的色泽已然磨得稀薄，让本来的苍白透了出来。同样的色调一直延展到那秃了的头顶。可真正出奇的还是那头发：两鬓之后各有一撮黑发，显然是染过的。这个人是谁，科斯莫心里没有半点疑问。那人站起身来，把他那一副骨节分明的身架整个儿竖直了，绷得紧紧的，一副军人的僵硬，叫人联想起十字皮带和背上那只牛皮背囊。「一个掷弹兵。」科斯莫心想，被这场不期而遇吓了一跳，又深感懊恼，仿佛自己是被人诱进了陷阱。他想不明白的是，那人为何要冲他做那样一副怪相。那怪相扭动了他整张脸，连嘴唇、腮帮子，乃至一双眼睛都卷进了一阵痉挛里。最可怖的是，那副怪相居然凝住不散。……「咦，那是一个微笑呀。」科斯莫蓦地松了口气，心想。这松懈来得那样猝然，竟不由自主地在他自己脸上漾开了一个微笑。于是海利翁伯爵的脸恢复了常态，科斯莫也头一次听见他的声音。那声音从他的胸腔深处发出来。洪亮而含混，带着一种威严，又有一种生硬之感，不知怎的恰与这人的气宇相合。这话是告诉科斯莫：海利翁伯爵之所以一直待在伯爵夫人的小客厅里，正是有意要结识他；而那人的眼中却有一种提防之色，仿佛他刚刚吐露了一桩至关重要的密事，正忐忑于它的反响。海利翁伯爵那只鼻子根部宽阔、末端尖细，鼻子下面是一道完全水平的、乌黑的髭，这让那颗脑袋带上了几分滑稽的可能；这脑袋个性太强，科斯莫没法单纯地把它看作阿黛勒的丈夫的那颗脑袋；他几乎不曾那样去看它。他对这一事实的把握是滑不留手的。他神色自若到了极点，应答说自己也正纳闷，今夜是否有幸结识伯爵。两人都坐了下来。

===Original===
“My occupations kept me late tonight,” said the Count. “The courier came in.”

He pointed with his fingers to the gazette lying on the floor, and Cosmo asked if there were any news.

“In the gazette, no. At least nothing interesting. The world is full of vanities and scandals, rumours of conspiracies. Very poor stuff. I don’t know any of those people the papers mention every day. That’s more my wife’s affair. For years now she has spent about ten months of every year in Paris or near Paris. I am a provincial. My interests are in the orphanage I have founded in my native country. I am also building an asylum for﻿ ﻿…”

He got up suddenly, approached the mantelpiece in three strides, and turned round exactly like a soldier in the ranks of a company changing front. He was wearing a blue coat cut away in front and having a long skirt, something recalling the cut of a uniform, though the material was fine and there was a good deal of gold lace about it, as also on his white satin waistcoat. Cosmo recalled the vague story he had heard about Count de Montevesso having served in more than one army before being given the rank of general by the King of Piedmont. The man had been drilled. Cosmo wondered whether he had ever been caned. He was a military adventurer of the commonest type. Some of them have been known to return with a fortune got by pillage and intrigue and possibly even by real talents of a sort in the service of oriental courts full of splendours and crimes, tyrannies and treacheries and dark drama of ambition, or love.

===Chinese===
「我手头的事务让我今晚耽搁到很晚，」伯爵说，「信使刚到。」

他用手指了指摊在地上的那份公报，科斯莫便问可有什么消息。

「公报里倒没有。至少没什么有趣的。这世上满是虚荣与丑闻，还有种种谋反的风闻。都是些很无聊的东西。报上天天点到的那些人，我一个也不认识。那更该是我太太的事。这些年她每年总有十个月是在巴黎或巴黎附近度过的。我是个乡下人。我的心血都在我在家乡创办的那座孤儿院上。我还在建一座收容所，专门收容……」

他猛地站起身，三步走到壁炉台前，转过身来，活像一个连队在变换阵势队列里的士兵。他穿一件蓝色外套，前襟剪开、后摆很长，式样有几分像军服的裁剪，只是料子极为讲究，上头缀着不少金绦，他那件白缎子马甲上也是一样。科斯莫记起自己曾听过一桩语焉不详的传闻，说蒙特韦索伯爵（Count de Montevesso）在受皮埃蒙特国王（King of Piedmont）授予将军衔之前，曾在不止一支军队里效过力。这人是一直被操练过来的。科斯莫暗自纳罕，他可曾挨过军棍。他是最寻常不过的那一类军事冒险家。这一类人里，有几个是带着一笔财富归来的——那财富或是抢来的、谋来的，甚至也许是在那些辉煌与罪行并存、专横与背叛交织、野心或情欲上演着一幕幕阴暗戏剧的东方宫廷里，凭着几分真才实干挣来的。

===Original===
“He is the very thing,” Cosmo exclaimed mentally, gating at the stiff figure leaning against the mantelpiece. Of course he got his fortune in India. What was remarkable about him was that he had managed to get away with his plunder, or at any rate a part of it, considerable enough to enable him to make a figure in the world and marry Adèle d’Armand in England. That was only because of the Revolution. In royal France he would not have had the ghost of a chance; and even as it was, only the odious laxity of London society in accepting rich strangers had given him his opportunity. Cosmo, forcing himself to envisage this dubious person as the husband of Adèle, felt very angry with the light-minded tolerance extended to foreigners characteristic of a certain part of London society. It was perfectly outrageous.

“Where the devil can my wife be?”

Those words made Cosmo start, though they had not been uttered very loudly. Almost mechanically he answered: “I don’t know,” and noticed that Count Helion was staring at him in a curiously unintelligent manner.

===Chinese===
「他正是那样的人。」科斯莫望着那个倚在壁炉台旁、僵直的身影，在心里嚷出来。他这财富当然是在印度挣下的。他身上唯一可称稀奇之处，是他竟能把自己的赃物——至少是其中的一部分——安然带了出来；那一笔已相当可观，足以让他在世上崭露头角，并在英格兰娶了阿黛勒·达蒙（Adèle d’Armand）。这也只是因为发生了革命的缘故。在王政的法兰西，他连一丝一毫的机会都没有；便是如今这光景，也全靠伦敦社交界那种接纳有钱外乡人的可恶的宽纵，才让他钻了空子。科斯莫强使自己把这个来路不明的人物当作阿黛勒的丈夫来端详，心里对伦敦社交界某一类人那种漫不经心地优待外邦人的做派，生出很大的恼怒。这简直是岂有此理。

「我妻子到底跑到哪儿去了？」

这几句话让科斯莫吓了一跳，尽管它们说得不甚响亮。他几乎是机械地答道：「我不知道。」随即注意到海利翁伯爵正用一种说不出的呆愣愣的目光盯着他。

===Original===
“I was really asking myself,” muttered the latter and stirred uneasily, without however taking his elbow off the mantelpiece. “It’s a natural thought since we are, God knows why, kept waiting for her here. I wasn’t aware I had spoken. Living for many years amongst people who didn’t understand any European language﻿—I had hundreds of them in my palace in Sindh﻿—I got into the habit of talking aloud, strange as it may appear to you.”

“Yes,” said Cosmo, with an air of innocence. “I suppose one acquires all sorts of strange habits in those distant countries. We in England have a class of men who return from India enriched. They are called nabobs. Some of them have most objectionable habits. Unluckily their mere wealth﻿ ﻿…”

“There is nothing to compare with wealth,” interrupted the other in a soldierly voice and paused, then continued in the same tone of making a verbal report: “When I was in England I had the privilege to know many people of position. They were very kind to me. They didn’t seem to think lightly of wealth.”

Each phrase came curt, detached, but it was evident that the man did not mean to be offensive. Those statements originated obviously in sincere conviction; and after the Count had uttered them there appeared on his forehead the horizontal wrinkles of unintelligent worry. Cosmo asked himself whether the man before him was not really very stupid. Under the elevated eyebrows his eyes looked worn and empty of all thought.

===Chinese===
「我其实是在问我自己，」后者嘟囔道，不安地动了动身子，却没有把肘从壁炉台上挪开。「这也是人之常情，既然天晓得为了什么，我们竟被晾在这儿等她。我并没觉察自己说出了声。多年来我生活在听不懂任何欧洲语言的人中间——我在信德（Sindh）的那座府第里，这样的人足有几百——我便养成了出声自语的习惯，这话听着怪，却是实情。」

「是了，」科斯莫装出一脸天真的神气说，「我想在那些遥远的国度，人会沾上各种各样古怪的习惯。我们英格兰也有一等人，是从印度发了财回来的。他们被人叫做『纳瓦布』（nabobs）。其中几个的习惯最是招人厌。可惜单单他们那笔钱财……」

「没有什么能和财富相比。」对方以一种军人的口吻打断他，停了一停，又以那种像在口述报告的口吻接着说：「我在英格兰的时候，有幸结识过许多有身份的人。他们待我极好。他们似乎并不把财富看轻。」

每一句都简短、割裂，但很显然，这人并没有冒犯之意。那些话分明是发乎衷心的确信；伯爵说完之后，额上便现出几道横纹，那是一种呆愣愣的焦烦。科斯莫暗自揣度，眼前这个人该不是当真蠢得很吧。在那扬起的眉毛底下，他的眼睛显得疲惫，一片了无思想的空茫。

===Original===
“Lots of money, I mean,” M. de Montevesso began again. “Not your savings and scrapings. Money that one acquires boldly and enough of it to be profuse with.”

“Is he going to treat me to vulgar boasting?” thought Cosmo. He wished that Adèle would come in and interrupt this tête-à-tête which was so very different from the one he had been expecting.

“I daresay money is very useful,” he assented, with airy scorn which he thought might put an end to the subject. But his interlocutor persisted.

“You can’t know anything about it,” he affirmed, then added unexpectedly: “Money will give you even ideas. Lots of ideas. The worst of it is that any one of them may turn out damnable. Well, yes. There is of course danger in money, but what of that?”

“It can scarcely be if it is used for good works, as you seem to use it,” said Cosmo with polite indifference. He meant it to be final, but Count de Montevesso was not to be suppressed.

“It leads one into worries,” he said. “For instance, that orphanage of mine, it is really a very large place. I am trying to be a benefactor to my native province, but I want it to be in my own way. Well, since the Restoration, the priests are trying to get hold of it. They want to turn it to the glory of God and to the service of religion. I have seen enough of all sorts of religions not to know what that means. No sooner had the King entered Paris than the Bishop wrote to me pointing out that there was no chapel and suggesting that I should build one and appoint a chaplain. That Bishop is﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===
「我是说，很多钱，」蒙特韦索先生又开口道，「不是你那点子省吃俭用攒下的钱。是大胆到手的钱，多得可以任意挥霍的钱。」

「他莫非要在我面前粗俗地自吹自擂一番？」科斯莫心想。他巴不得阿黛勒此刻能进来，打断这一场对谈——这场对谈与他方才所盼望的那一场，可实在是天差地远。

「我想钱是很有用处的。」他应道，带着一丝轻飘飘的轻蔑，自忖这或许能把这一桩话头了结掉。可他的对话者偏偏不肯罢休。

「这事你是不会懂的。」他一口咬定，接着又出人意料地补了一句：「钱甚至会给你带来主意。许许多多的主意。糟糕的是，这些主意里头随便哪一个，都可能糟得不可救药。嗯，不错。钱里头自然是藏着危险的，可那又怎样？」

「若是像您那样把它用于善举，想必就未必了。」科斯莫带着客气的淡漠说。他原想让这话收尾，可蒙特韦索伯爵是不肯被压下去的。

「它会惹人烦恼。」他说，「比方说我那座孤儿院吧，那实在是个很大的地方。我原想做我本乡的一个恩人，可我愿意照我自己的法子去做。可自打王政复辟（the Restoration）以来，那些神父就一心想把它攥到手里。他们要把它转向上帝的荣耀，转向宗教的功用。各种各样的宗教我都见得够多了，岂有不知道这意味着什么的。国王前脚进了巴黎，那位主教后脚便写信给我，指出院里没有小教堂，建议我建上一座，再委派一名驻院神父。那位主教啊……」

===Original===
He threw up his head suddenly and Cosmo became aware of the presence of Adèle without having heard even the rustle of her dress. He stood up hastily. There was a short silence.

“I see the acquaintance is made,” said Adèle, looking from one to the other. Her eyes lingered on Cosmo and then turned to her husband. “I didn’t know you would be already here. I had to help my father to his room. I would have come at once here but he detained me.” Again she turned to Cosmo. “You will pardon me.”

“I found Count Helion here. I have not been alone for a minute,” said Cosmo. “You owe me no apologies. I was delighted to make your husband’s acquaintance, even if you were not here to introduce us to each other.”

This was said in English and Count Helion by the mantelpiece waited till Cosmo had finished before he asked, “Where’s Clelia?”

“I have sent her to bed,” said Countess de Montevesso. “Helion, my father would like to see you this evening.”

“I am at the orders of M. le Marquis.”

The grenadier-like figure at the mantelpiece did not stir, and those words were followed only by a slight twitch in the muscles of the face which might have had a sardonic intention. “Tonight, at once,” he repeated. “But with Mr. Latham here?”

===Chinese===
他骤然扬起头来，科斯莫这才觉察到阿黛勒已在屋里——他竟连她衣裙的窸窣声也没听见。他匆匆站起身。有一阵短短的沉默。

「我看你们已经认识了。」阿黛勒说，目光从这一位移到那一位。她的眼睛在科斯莫身上停留了片刻，又转向她的丈夫。「我没料到你已然在这儿了。我得扶我父亲回他房间去。我本想立刻就到这儿来的，可他把我绊住了。」她又转向科斯莫。「你要原谅我。」

「我进来时海利翁伯爵就在这儿了。我连一刻也没独处过。」科斯莫说，「您不必向我告什么歉。我很高兴结识了您的丈夫，哪怕没有您在场替我们引见。」

这番话是用英语说的，壁炉台旁的海利翁伯爵一直等到科斯莫说完，才问：「克莱莉娅（Clelia）呢？」

「我已经打发她去睡了。」蒙特韦索伯爵夫人说，「海利翁，我父亲今晚想见你。」

「我听候侯爵先生（M. le Marquis）吩咐。」

壁炉台旁那个掷弹兵似的人影一动不动，这几句话之后，只有脸部肌肉微微一牵，似乎带着点讥讽之意。「今夜，立刻。」他又重复了一遍，「可还有莱瑟姆先生（Mr. Latham）在场？」

===Original===
“Pray don’t mind me, I am going away directly,” said Cosmo. “It is getting late.”

“In Italy it is never late. I hope to find you here when I return. As the husband of a daughter of the house of D’Armand I know what is due to the name of Latham. Am I really expected at once?”

Adèle moved forward a step or two, speaking rapidly. “There has been some news from Elba, or about Elba, which gives a certain concern to my father. As you have been to the public knowledge in direct touch with people from Elba my father would like to have your opinion.”

Count Helion changed his attitude, and leaning his shoulders against the mantelpiece addressed himself to Cosmo.

“It was the most innocent thing in the world. It was something about the project for the exploitation of the Island of Pianosa. Napoleon sent his treasurer here to get in touch with a banker. I am a man of affairs. The banker consulted me﻿—as a man who knew the spot. It’s true I know the spot, but if you hear it said that it is because of my relations with the Dey of Algiers, pray don’t believe it. I am in no way in touch with the Barbary States.”

===Chinese===
「请不必介意我，我这就告辞了。」科斯莫说，「时候不早了。」

「在意大利，时候从来都不晚。我回来时还望能在这儿见到你。既是达蒙家族（D’Armand）一位女婿的丈夫，我知道莱瑟姆这个姓氏受得起怎样的礼数。当真要我现在就去吗？」

阿黛勒往前走了一两步，语速极快地说：「方才传来了些关于厄尔巴岛（Elba）、或与厄尔巴岛有关的消息，叫我父亲颇为挂心。既然众所周知您曾与厄尔巴岛来的人有过直接接触，我父亲很想听听您的见解。」

海利翁伯爵换了姿态，把双肩靠在壁炉台上，转向科斯莫说话。

「那是世上最稀松平常不过的一件事。是关于皮亚诺萨岛（Island of Pianosa）一处开发计划的事。拿破仑派他的司库到这儿来，与一位银行家接洽。我是个办实务的人。那银行家来向我讨教——当作一个熟悉那地方的人。我确实熟悉那个地方，可若是有人对你说，那是由于我与阿尔及尔的德伊（Dey of Algiers）有所瓜葛，请千万别信。我与巴巴里诸国（the Barbary States）毫无来往。」

===Original===
He made a step forward, and then another, and stood still. “You two had better sit down and talk. Yes, sit down and talk. Renew the acquaintance of your early youth﻿ ﻿… your early youth,” he repeated in a faint voice. “Those youthful friendships﻿ ﻿…” he made a convulsive grimace which Cosmo had discovered to be the effect of a smile. “There is something so charming in those youthful friendships. As to myself I don’t remember ever being youthful.” He stepped out towards the door through which Cosmo had seen Clelia enter that morning. “Let me find you when I return, enjoying yourselves most sentimentally. Most delightful.”

His long stiff back swayed in the doorway and the door came to with a crash.

Cosmo and Adèle looked at each other with a smile. Cosmo, hat in hand, asked just audibly, “I suppose I had better stay?” She made an affirmative sign and, moving away from him, put her foot on the marble fender of the fireplace where nothing was left but hot ashes hiding a reddish glow.

===Chinese===
他往前迈了一步，又迈了一步，便站定了。「你们两个最好坐下来谈谈。对，坐下来谈谈。重温你们少年时代的交情……你们少年时代的交情。」他用一种隐隐约约的声音重复。「那些少年的情谊啊……」他做了一个痉挛般的怪相——科斯莫已经发现，那是他微笑时的神情。「少年的情谊里有一种说不出的迷人。至于我自己，我倒不记得自己曾经年少过。」他朝那扇门走去，那正是今晨科斯莫看见克莱莉娅进来的那扇门。「等我回来时，但愿能见你们正多情地聊得尽兴。再妙不过。」

他瘦长僵直的背影在门口一晃，那门便「砰」地一声合上了。

科斯莫与阿黛勒相视一笑。科斯莫一手捏着帽子，低声勉强听得见地问：「我想我还是留下为好？」她做了个肯定的表示，随即从他身边走开，把脚踏上壁炉前那道大理石挡板——炉中除了盖着一层余烬的滚烫灰烬，已什么都不剩，那灰底下还掩着一抹暗红的光。

### V / V

===Original===
Cosmo, ill at ease, remained looking at her. He was in doubt what the sign she had made meant, a nervous and imperious gesture, which might have been a command for him to go or to stay. In his irresolution he gazed at her, thinking that she was lovely to an incredible degree and that the word “radiant” applied to her extraordinary aptness. Light entered into her composition. And it was not the cold light of marble. “She actually glows,” he said to himself, amazed, “like ripe fruit in the foliage, like a big flower in the shade.”

“Don’t gaze at my blushes,” said Madame de Montevesso in an even tone tinged with a little mockery and a little bitterness. “Would you believe that when I was a girl I was so shy that I used to blush crimson whenever anybody looked at me or spoke to me? It’s a failing which does not meet with much sympathy. And yet my suffering was very real. It would reach such a pitch at times that I was ready to cry.”

“Shall I go away?” asked Cosmo in a deadened voice. He waited for a moment while she seemed to debate in her mind the answer to the question. In his fear of being sent away he went on: “God knows I don’t want to leave you. And after all the Count is coming back and﻿﻿

“Oh, yes, he is coming back. Sit down. Yes. It would be better. Sit down.﻿﻿﻿…” Cosmo sat down where he could see her admirable shoulders, the roundness of her averted head, *coiffée en boucles* and girt with a gold circlet, the shadowy retreating view of her profile. The long drapery of her train flowed to the ground in a dark blue shimmer.﻿﻿﻿… “He is inevitable. He has always been inevitable,” came further from her lips which he couldn’t see, for the mirror above the mantelpiece reflected nothing but her forehead with the gold mist of her hair above.

Cosmo remained silent. For nothing in the world would he have made a sound. He held his breath with expectation; and in the extreme tension of his whole being the lights grew dim around him, while her white shoulders, the thick clustering curls, the arm on which she leaned, and the other bare arm hanging inert by her side, seemed the only source of light in the room.

===Chinese===
科斯莫局促不安地站在那里，仍旧望着她。他拿不准她方才做的那个手势是什么意思——一个紧张而专横的动作，也许是叫他走，也许是叫他留下。他在迟疑不决中凝视着她，心想她美得简直令人难以置信，「光彩照人」这个词用在她是再贴切也没有了。光，是她肌体的一部分；而且那不是大理石般冷冽的光。「她简直是会发光的，」他惊讶地暗自说道，「像枝叶间一枚熟透的果子，像浓荫下一朵硕大的花。」

「别盯着我这脸红看，」蒙特韦索夫人说道，声调平稳，略带几分讥诮，又略带几分苦涩。「你信不信，我做姑娘那阵子害羞得很，别人只要看我一眼、跟我说一句话，我就会涨红了脸？这种毛病向来不大招人同情。可我那时受的罪却是千真万确的。有时竟会涨到一种地步，我简直就要哭出来了。」

「要我走开吗？」科斯莫压低了声音问。他等了片刻，她似乎在心里权衡着这问题的答语。他怕被她打发走，便接着说道：「老天知道，我不想离开你。再说，伯爵就要回来了，而且……」

「哦，是的，他要回来的。坐下吧。对。这样更好。坐下吧。……」科斯莫坐了下来，他坐的位置正好能看见她那令人赞叹的双肩，能看见她侧过去的头那圆润的轮廓——她的发挽成卷（*coiffée en boucles*），箍着一道金圈——还能看见她侧影那朦胧退缩的一瞥。她长长裙裾的下摆如一道深蓝的微光流淌到地上。……「他是不可避免的。他从来就是不可避免的。」这话进一步从她那他看不见的双唇间传出来，因为壁炉架上方的那面镜子，只照出她的额头，以及额上那一片金雾似的头发。

科斯莫默不作声。天底下任凭什么他也不肯弄出一丝声响。他屏住呼吸，满心期待；在他整个身心那种极度的紧绷之中，他周围的灯火都黯淡下去，唯有她那洁白的双肩、那一簇簇浓密的鬈发、她倚着的那只胳膊，还有另一只无力垂在身侧的裸露的胳膊，似乎是这屋里唯一的光源。

===Original===
“You don’t know me at all,” began the Countess de Montevesso. “I don’t charge you with forgetting; but the little you may remember of me cannot be of any use. It is only natural that I should be a stranger to you. But you cannot be a stranger to me. For one thing you were a boy and then you were not a child of outcasts without a country, of refugees with a ruined past and with no future. You were a young Latham, as rooted in your native soil as the old trees of your park. Even then there seemed to me something enviable about you.”

She turned her head a little to glance at him. “You had no idea what it was like after we had gone to London. My ignorance of the world was so profound that I felt ill at ease in it. I hoped I had an attractive face, but I only discovered that I was pretty from the remarks of the people in the street I overheard. I spent my life by the side of my mother’s couch. I never went out except attended by my father or by Aglae. My only amusement was to play a game of chess now and then with an old doctor, also a refugee, who looked after my mother, or listen to the conversation of the people who came to see us. Amongst them there were all the prominent men and women of the old regime. Refugees. They seldom spoke the truth to each other, and yet they were no more stupid than the rest of the world. Nobody could be more good-natured and better company, more frivolous or more inconsiderate. I have seen women of the highest rank work ten hours a day to get bread for their children, but they also slandered one another, told falsehoods about their conduct and their work, and quarrelled among themselves in the style of washerwomen. Morals were even looser than in the times before the Revolution. Manners were forgotten. Every transgression was excused in those who were regarded as good royalists. I don’t mean this to apply to the great body of the refugees. Some of them led irreproachable lives. Round our Princes there were some most absurd intrigues. I didn’t know much of all this, but I remember my poor father’s helpless indignations and my own appalled disgust at the things I could not help hearing and seeing.”

She turned her head to look at Cosmo. “I am telling you all this to give you some idea of the air I had to breathe,” she said in a changed tone. “I don’t think it contaminated me. I felt its odiousness; but all this seemed without remedy. I didn’t even suffer much from it. What I suffered most from was our domestic anxieties; my mother’s fears lest the small resources we had to live on should fail us altogether. Our daily crust of bread seemed to depend on political events in Europe, and they were going against us. Battles, negotiations, everything. A blight seemed to have fallen on the royalist cause. My mother didn’t conceal her distress. What touched me more still was the careworn, silent anxiety of my poor father.”

She paused, looking at Cosmo intently, meeting his eyes fixed on her face. “I was getting on for sixteen,” she continued. “No one ever paid the slightest attention to me. The only genuine passion in my heart was filial love.﻿﻿﻿… But is it any good in going on? And then I can’t tell what you may have heard already.”

“All I have heard,” said Cosmo in a tone of profound respect, “is that Adèle de Montevesso’s life has been irreproachable.”

===Chinese===
「你一点也不了解我，」蒙特韦索伯爵夫人开口道，「我并不怪你忘了我；可你对我可能存有的那一点儿记忆，于事无补。我对你来说是个陌生人，这是再自然不过的事。可你对我来说，却不可能是陌生人。一来，那时你还是个男孩；二来，你并不是那种没有祖国、被放逐之人的孩子，不是过去毁尽、又无未来的流亡者的孩子。你是个年轻的莱瑟姆，像你家园林里的古树一般，深深扎根在故土里。即便在那时，你身上似乎就有一种令我羡慕的东西。」

她把头稍稍转过来瞟了他一眼。「你想象不到我们到了伦敦之后过的是什么样的日子。我对世事无知到了极点，在那世事之中我感到浑身不自在。我巴望自己有一张讨人喜欢的脸，可我究竟漂不漂亮，还是从街上来来往往的人说的话里偷听来的。我整日守在母亲躺椅的旁边。除了由父亲或阿格莱（Aglae）陪着，我从不出门。我唯一的消遣，是偶尔跟一位也是流亡人的老医生——他照看着我的母亲——下一盘棋，要不就是听听来看我们的人谈话。那些人里有旧时代（ancien régime）所有显赫的男女。都是流亡贵族（émigré）。他们彼此之间难得说真话，可他们也并不比这世上其余的人更蠢。再没有比他们更和善、更宜人的同伴了，也没有比他们更轻浮、更不顾前后的。我见过最高贵的夫人一天做十个小时的活计，好给自己的孩子挣面包，可她们彼此造谣中伤，在品行上、活计上互相扯谎，又像洗衣妇似的彼此吵架斗嘴。风化的松弛甚至超过了革命以前。礼数是被抛到九霄云外了。凡是被看作好的保皇党的人，他的一切过失都可以被原谅。我这话并不指那流亡的大多数人。他们当中有些人过着无可指摘的日子。可是在我们的亲王们周围，却有着一些最为荒唐的密谋（intrigue）。这些事我大多并不知道，可我记得我那可怜的父亲那一阵阵无可奈何的愤懑，还有我自己对那些躲不开、听得到、看得见的事所感到的惊骇的厌恶。」

她转过头来看着科斯莫。「我告诉你这一切，是想让你对我不得不呼吸的那空气有个概念，」她换了一种口气说，「我想它并没有玷污我。我嗅到了它的可憎；可这一切仿佛无药可救。我甚至并没有为此受过多少苦。我最受折磨的，是我们家里的种种焦虑；我母亲生怕我们赖以为生的那点微薄进项会一下子断绝。我们每日那块面包皮，仿佛全看欧洲的政局而定，而那政局却处处跟我们作对。战役、谈判，桩桩件件。保皇党的事业仿佛遭了一场天谴。母亲并不掩饰她的愁苦。更叫我心疼的，是我那可怜的父亲那忧心忡忡、默不作声的焦虑。」

她停了停，目不转睛地看着科斯莫，迎上他那定定望着她脸庞的目光。「我快十六岁了，」她接着说，「从来没有谁稍稍把我放在心上。我心里唯一真正的激情，是对父母的孝爱。……可再说下去有什么用呢？再说，我也说不准你已经听过了些什么。」

「我听说过的，」科斯莫以一种极为敬重的口吻说，「只是，阿黛勒·德·蒙特韦索（Adèle de Montevesso）的一生，是无可指摘的。」

===Original===
“I remember the time when all the world was doing its best to make it impossible. Would it shock you very much if I told you that I don’t care at all about its good opinion now? There was a time when it would put the worst construction possible on my distress, on my bewilderment, on my very innocence.”

“Why should the world do that to you?” asked Cosmo.

“Why? But I see you know nothing. I met my husband first at a select concert that was given by the music-master of the late Queen of France. My mother was feeling a little better and insisted on my going out a little. Those were small fashionable affairs. I had a good voice myself, and that evening I sang with Madame Seppio. An English gentleman﻿—his name doesn’t matter﻿—presented M. de Montevesso to me as a friend of his just returned from India and anxious to be introduced to the best society. What with my usual shyness and the unattractive appearance of the man, I don’t think I received his attentions very well. There was really no reason I should notice him particularly. It wasn’t difficult to see that he had not the manners of a man of the world. Where could he have acquired them? He had left his village at seventeen, he enlisted in the Irish Regiment which served in France, then he deserted, perhaps. I only know that some years afterwards he was a captain in the service of Russia. From there he made his way to India. I believe the governor-general used him as a sort of unofficial agent amongst native princes, but he got into some scrape with the company. By what steps he managed to get on to the back of an elephant and command the army of a native prince I really don’t know. And even if I had known then it would not have made him more interesting in my eyes. I was relieved when he made me a deep bow with his hand on his heart and went away. He left a most fugitive impression, but the very next morning he sent his English friend to ask my parents for my hand. That friend was a nobleman, a man of honour, and the offers he was empowered to make were so generous that my parents thought they must tell me of them. I was so astonished that at first I couldn’t speak. I simply went away and shut myself up in my room. They were not people to press me for an answer. The poor worried dears thought that I wouldn’t even consent to contemplate this marriage; while I, shut up in my room﻿—I was afraid, remembering the way they had spoken to me of that offer, that they would reject it without consulting me any further. I sent word by Aglae that I would give my answer next day and that I begged to be left to myself. Then I escaped from the house, followed by Aglae, who was never so frightened in her life, and went to see the wife of that friend of my present husband. I begged her to send at once for General de Montevesso﻿﻿at that time he called himself General. The King of Sardinia had given him this rank in acknowledgment of some service that his great wealth had enabled him to render to the Court of Turin. That lady of course had many scruples about doing something so highly unconventional, but at last, overcome by the exaltation of my feelings, she consented.”

“She did that?” murmured Cosmo. “What an extraordinary thing!”

===Chinese===
「我还记得有一阵子，整个世界都在竭尽全力，要让这种无可指摘变成不可能。要是我告诉你，如今我对它的好评毫不在乎，你会非常震惊吗？曾几何时，它把最恶毒的用心加在我的痛苦上、加在我的惶惑上、甚至加在我那份清白之上。」

「世人为什么要那样对你？」科斯莫问。

「为什么？可见你什么都不知情。我第一次遇见我丈夫，是在已故法兰西王后的那位乐师举办的一场精选音乐会上。母亲那阵子觉得好些了，执意要我出去走走。那是些小小的、时髦的聚会。我自己有一副好嗓子，那天晚上我跟塞皮奥夫人（Madame Seppio）一起唱了歌。一位英国绅士——他的名字无关紧要——把德·蒙特韦索先生作为他的一位刚从印度回来、急于跻身最上流社会的朋友介绍给了我。一则是出于我平日的那份羞怯，二则是由于那人的模样并不讨喜，我想我对他那番殷勤并未怎么领情。我实在没有理由要特别注意他。不难看出他并没有上流社会人物的那份举止。他这份举止又能从哪儿学来呢？他十七岁就离开了村子，报名进了在法国服役的爱尔兰团，后来也许开了小差。我只知道几年之后他成了替俄罗斯效力的一名上尉。从那儿他又辗转去了印度。我相信印度总督把他当作一种非正式的密使，派到土邦王公们中间去办事，可他跟那家公司闹了点纠葛。他究竟是怎么设法爬上了大象的背、又怎么当上了一支土邦军队的统帅，我实在不得而知。便是当年我知道这些，也不会使他在我眼里变得更有意思些。当他一手按着心口，向我深深一鞠躬，转身走开时，我倒松了一口气。他留下的印象极其淡薄，可就在第二天一早，他便派他那位英国朋友来向我父母求亲。那位朋友是位贵族，是个讲体面的人，他受权提出的那种种条件又如此慷慨，我父母觉得非告诉我不可。我惊讶得一时说不出话来。我干脆走开，把自己关进了房间。他们不是那种逼着我作答的人。那两位被烦恼折磨着的可怜的亲人，还以为我连考虑这桩婚事都不肯呢；而我，把自己关在房里——却害怕起来，回想着他们对我提起那门亲事时的口吻，生怕他们不再跟我商量便把它回绝了。我托阿格莱传话，说我第二天给答复，并恳求他们让我独自待着。随后我便溜出了家门，阿格莱跟在后头，她平生从未这样惊慌过。我去见了我如今这位丈夫那位朋友的妻子。我求她立刻派人去把德·蒙特韦索将军找来——那时他还自称为将军。撒丁王国的国王（King of Sardinia）为酬谢他那巨额财富使他得以替都灵宫廷（Court of Turin）出的一份力，授予了他这个军衔。那位夫人面对这样一桩极其不合常规的事，自然顾虑重重，可终于被我那高涨的情绪所打动，应承了下来。」

「她竟这样做了？」科斯莫喃喃道，「真是一件非同寻常的事！」

===Original===
“Yes. She did that, instead of taking me home. People will do extraordinary things to please a man of fabulous wealth. She sent out two or three messengers to look for him all over the town. They were some time in finding him. I waited. I was perfectly calm. I was calmer than I am now, telling you my story. I was possessed by the spirit of self-sacrifice. I had no misgivings. I remember even how cold I was in that small drawing room with a big coal fire. He arrived out of breath. He was splendidly dressed and behaved very ceremoniously. I felt his emotion without sharing it. I, who used to blush violently at the smallest provocation, didn’t feel the slightest embarrassment in addressing that big stiff man so much older than myself. I could not appreciate what a fatal mistake I was committing by telling him that I didn’t care for him in the least and probably never should; but that if he would secure my parents’ future comfort my gratitude would be so great that I could marry him, without reluctance and be his loyal friend and wife for life. He stood there stiff and ominous and told me that he didn’t flatter himself with the possibility of inspiring any deeper feeling.

“We stood there facing each other for a bit. I felt nothing but an inward glow of satisfaction at having, as I thought, acted honourably. As to him I think he was simply made dumb with rage. At last he bowed with his hands on his heart and said that he would not even ask now for the favour of kissing my hand. I appreciated his delicacy at that moment. It would have been an immense trial to my shyness. I think now that he was simply afraid of putting my hand to his lips lest he should lose his self-control and bite it. He told me later, in one of those moments when people don’t care what they say, that at that moment he positively hated me, not the sight of me, you understand, but my aristocratic insolence.”

She paused, and in the youthful sincerity of his sympathy Cosmo uttered a subdued exclamation of distress. Madame de Montevesso looked at him again and then averted her face.

===Chinese===
「是的。她这样做了，而没有把我领回家。人们为了取悦一个富可敌国的人，是什么出格的事都做得出来的。她派出了两三个差人，满城去找他。他们费了些工夫才把他找到。我等着。我极其平静。我那时比我此刻把这故事讲给你听还要平静。我被一种自我献身的精神攫住了。我没有半点疑虑。我甚至记得在那间生着一炉旺旺的煤火的小客厅里，我是怎样地手脚冰凉。他气喘吁吁地赶到了。他衣着华美，举止极尽礼数。我感觉得到他的激动，却不能与之共鸣。我这样一个稍微一撩拨就涨红了脸的人，面对这个比我年长许多、又高又僵硬的汉子，竟没有半分窘迫地开口说话。我对他说，我一点也不喜欢他，多半也永远不会喜欢他；可他若是能保我父母日后安适，我的感激之情将大到使我能够毫无勉强地嫁给他，做他一辈子的忠实朋友和妻子——我那时无从领略，我这是在犯一个多么致命的错误。他僵硬而阴沉地站在那里，对我说，他并不敢奢望能在我心中激起什么更深的情感。

「我们就这样面对面站了一会儿。我心里只觉得一阵内在的、得意的暖意，因为我想，我已经做得体面了。至于他嘛，我想他简直是被愤怒憋得说不出话来了。末了他双手按着心口一鞠躬，说他此刻甚至不敢奢望能获准吻一吻我的手。那一刻我很欣赏他这份体贴心。若真要吻我的手，对我那份羞怯该是一场莫大的煎熬。如今我想，他当时简直是不敢把我的手凑到唇边，生怕自己把持不住，会一口咬下去。后来他在一次那种人们什么都不顾的时分里告诉我，那一刻他是真恨我，并不是恨我这个人，你明白吗，而是恨我那一份贵族气派的傲慢。」

她停了下来；科斯莫出于少年人那份真挚的同情，发出一声压抑的、心疼的惊呼。蒙特韦索夫人又看了他一眼，随即把脸转了过去。

===Original===
“I heard afterwards some gossip to the effect that he had been jilted by a girl to whom he was engaged, the daughter of some captain on half-pay, and that he proposed to me simply to show her that he could find a girl prettier, of higher rank, and in every way more distinguished that would consent to be his wife. I believe that it was this that prevented him from drawing back before my frankness. As to me, I went home, seeing nothing, hearing nothing, caring for nothing, as though I had done with the world, as though I had taken the veil. I can find no other comparison for the peace that was in me. I faced my mother’s reproaches calmly. She was of course very much hurt at my not confiding in her at this crisis of my life. My father, too. But how could I have confided in them in this matter on which their security and welfare depended? How could I have confided in any of the men and women around me who seemed to me as if mad, whose conduct and opinions I despised with youthful severity as foolish and immoral? There was one human being in the world in whom I might perhaps have confided, that perhaps would have understood me. That was your father, Cosmo. But he was three hundred miles away. There was no time. Tell me, did he understand? Has he cast me out of his thoughts forever?”

“My father,” said Cosmo, “has lived like a hermit for years. There was nothing to make him forget you. Yes, he was a man in whom you could have confided. He would have understood you. That doesn’t mean to say that he would have approved. I wish he had been by your side. He would have brought pressure on your parents with the authority of an old and tried friend.”

“And benefactor,” struck in the Countess de Montevesso. “My father, I believe, had an inkling of the truth. He begged me again and again to think well of what I was doing. I told him that I was perfectly satisfied with what I had done. It was perfectly true then. I had satisfied my conscience by telling my suitor that I could never love him. I felt strangely confident that I could fulfil the duties of my new position, and I was absorbed by the happiness of having saved my parents from all anxiety for the future. I was not aware of having made any sacrifice. Probably if I had been twenty or more I would have been less confident; perhaps I wouldn’t have had the courage! But at that age I didn’t know that my whole life was at stake. Three weeks afterwards I was married.

===Chinese===
「我后来听到一些闲话，说他曾被一个与他订了婚的姑娘——某个领半薪的上尉的女儿——甩了，而他向我求婚，纯粹是为了向她证明：他能找到一个比她更漂亮、出身更高、各方面更出众的姑娘肯做他的妻子。我相信，正是因为这个，他才没有在我的坦率面前退缩。至于我，我回了家，视而不见，听而不闻，对什么都不放在心上，仿佛我已经和这世界两清了，仿佛我已经戴上了修女的面纱。我心里的那一份安宁，我再找不到别的比方来形容它。我平静地承受了母亲的责备。在我一生的这个紧要关头，我竟不肯向她吐露半分，她自然是深受伤害的。我父亲也是。可这一桩关系到他们二老安危福祉的事，我怎么能向他们吐露呢？我又怎么能向身边那些在我看来仿佛发了疯的男女——他们的言行主张，我都以一种年轻人的严苛，当作愚蠢与不道德而加以鄙视——吐露半分呢？这世上只有一个人，我也许本可以向他吐露，那个人也许能理解我。那就是你的父亲，科斯莫。可他在三百英里之外。又没有时间。告诉我，他明白吗？他是不是已经永远把我从他的念想中逐出去了？」

「家父，」科斯莫说，「多年来一直过着隐士般的日子。没有什么事能让他忘掉你。是的，他是个你可以向他吐露心事的人。他会理解你。但这并不等于说他会赞成。我真希望他当时就在你身边。他会以一位老而可靠的朋友的威望，向你的父母施加压力。」

「还有恩人，」蒙特韦索伯爵夫人插话道，「我相信我父亲多少察觉了真相。他一遍又一遍地求我，要我三思而行。我告诉他，我对自己所做的事完全满意。那时这完全是真话。我既已对我的求亲者说明我永远不会爱他，便也算对得住自己的良心了。我怀着一种奇怪的信心，觉得自己能够胜任新身份下的种种职责；而能把父母从对未来的种种忧虑里彻底解救出来，这一份幸福把我整个人都浸透了。我并不觉得自己做了什么牺牲。倘若我当时已经二十岁出头，我也许就不会这么有把握了；也许就没有这份勇气！可在那个年纪，我并不知道我押上的是我整个的一生。三个星期之后，我便嫁了人。

===Original===
“As you see, there was no time lost. During that period our intercourse was of the most formal kind only I never even attempted to observe him with any attention. He was very stiff and ceremonious, but he was in a hurry, because I believe he was afraid from his previous experience that I would change my mind. His usual answer to the expression of all my wishes and to most of my speeches was a profound bow﻿﻿and, sometimes, I was amused. In the lightness of my heart a thought would come to me that a lifetime on such terms would be a funny affair. I don’t say he deceived me in anything. He had brought an immense fortune out of India and the world took him at its face value. With no more falsehood than holding his tongue and watching his behaviour he kept me in the dark about his character, his family, his antecedents, his very name. When we first were married he was ostentatious and rather mean at the same time. His long life in India added the force of oriental jealousy to that which would be in a sense natural to a man of his age. Moreover, his character was naturally disagreeable. The only way he could make the power of his great fortune felt was by hurting the feelings of other people, of his servants, of his dependents, of his friends. His wife came in for her share. An older and cleverer woman with a certain power of deception and caring for the material pleasures of life could have done better for herself and for him in the situation in which I was placed, but I, almost a child, with an honest and proud character and caring nothing for what wealth could give, I was perfectly helpless. I was being constantly surprised and shocked by the displays of evil passions and his fits of ridiculous jealousy which were expressed in such a coarse manner that they could only arouse my resentment and contempt.

“Meantime we lived in great style﻿﻿dinner parties, concerts. I had a very good voice. I daresay he was anxious enough to show off his latest acquisition, but at the same time he could not bear me being looked at or even spoken to. A fit of oriental jealousy would come over him, especially when I had been much applauded. He would express his feelings to me in barrack-room language. At last, one evening he made a most scandalous scene before about two hundred guests, and then went out of the house, leaving me to make the best of it before all those people. It caused the greatest possible scandal. The party of course broke up. I spent the rest of the night sitting in my bedroom, too overcome to take off my splendid dress and those jewels with which he always insisted I should bedeck myself. With the first signs of dawn he returned, and coming up into my room found me sitting there. He told me then that living with me was too much of a torture for him and proposed I should go back to my parents for a time.

===Chinese===
「你也看见了，一点儿时间也没有耽搁。在那段时间里，我们之间的来往是最最拘泥礼节的，而且我甚至从未试着稍加留意地观察过他。他很僵硬，很讲究礼数，但他急着办成事，因为我相信他是从先前的经历里得了教训，生怕我又变了卦。他对我一切心愿的表白、对我大半数的话语，通常的答覆都是深深的一鞠躬——有时呢，我觉得好笑。在我那轻松的心境里，会掠过一个念头：一辈子照这样过下去，倒是一桩滑稽的事。我并不是说他在哪件事上骗了我。他从印度带回了一笔巨额的财产，世人便按它表面的价值来接纳他。他不过凭着闭口不言和留心自己的举止——再没有更多的作伪——便把他的为人、他的家世、他的来历，甚至他的真姓名，都瞒得我严严实实。我们刚结婚那阵子，他既爱摆阔，同时又相当吝啬。他在印度度过的漫长岁月，给一种对像他这年纪的人来说本也算是人之常情的妒忌，又加上了东方人那种妒忌的狠劲。何况他生性本就乖戾。他能让他那笔大财的威风被感觉到的唯一办法，便是去伤害别人的感情——伤害他的仆人、他的依附者、他的朋友。他的妻子也有她该当的一份。换了一个年纪大些、更精明些、又多少懂得作伪、并且贪恋尘世种种享乐的女人，处在我这个境地，本可以为自己、也为他做得更妥帖些；可我，几乎还是个孩子，性情又正直又骄傲，对财富所能给予的东西毫不在意，便简直束手无策。他那一阵阵流露出来的恶情劣性，他那荒唐的妒性发作，表达得又那样粗鄙，只能激起我的怨恨与轻蔑，而我却不断地为之惊愕、为之震惊。

「与此同时，我们过着极阔绰的排场——宴客、音乐会。我有一副很好的嗓子。我想他是急于炫耀他这最新到手的战利品的，可与此同时，他又受不了别人看我，甚至受不了别人跟我说话。一阵东方式的妒忌会突然攫住他，尤其是在我博得许多喝彩之后。他会用兵营里的粗话来向我发泄他的情绪。终于，有一天晚上，他在大约两百位客人面前大闹了一场最不堪入目的丑剧，然后径自出门去了，丢下我独自在那一屋子人面前尽力周旋。这件事引起了所能想象的最大一场风波。宴会自然是不欢而散。这一夜的余下时分，我都坐在自己的卧室里，心里难受得连我那身华丽的衣裳、连他总是非要我把自己装点一身不可的那些珠宝，都顾不得脱下来。等到天边透出第一缕晨光，他回来了，上楼进了我的房间，发现我还坐在那里。他便对我说，跟我一同过日子对他来说是一种太过难忍的折磨，并提出要我暂且回父母家住一阵。

===Original===
“We had been married for a little over a year then. For the first time since the wedding I felt really happy. They, poor dears, were delighted. We were all so innocent together that we thought this would be the end of all our troubles, that the man was chivalrous enough to have seen his mistake in the proper light, and to bear the consequences nobly. Hadn’t I told him I could never love him, exactly in so many words?

“I ought to have known that he was incapable of any generosity. As a matter of fact I didn’t think much about it. I, who had overcome my shyness enough to become, young as I was, a perfect hostess in a world which I knew so little﻿﻿because after all that sort of thing was in my tradition﻿﻿I was really too stupid, too unsophisticated for those ten months to have been a lesson to me. I had learned nothing, any more than one learns from a nightmare or from a period of painful illness. I simply breathed freely. I became again the old Adèle. I dismissed M. de Montevesso from my thoughts as though he had never lived. Can you believe this, Cosmo? It is astonishing how facts can fail to impress one; brutalities, abuse, scenes of passion, mad exhibitions of jealousy, as long as they do not attack your conception of your moral personality. All this fell off me like a poisoned robe, leaving hardly a smart behind. I raised my head like a flower after a thunderstorm. Don’t think my character is shallow, Cosmo. There were depths in me that could be reached, but till then I had been only tormented, shocked, surprised, but hardly even frightened. It was he who had suffered. But my turn was to come.”

“I don’t think you were ever a person of shallow feelings.”

“One’s feelings must mature like everything else, and I assure you I had not yet stopped growing. The next six months were to finish my education. For by that time I had lost all my illusions. While I was breathing freely between my father and mother, forgetting the world around us, Montevesso was going about the town with his complaints and his suspicions; regretting he had let me go and enraged that I should have gone from him so easily. And you may be sure he found sympathizers. A rich man, you understand! Who could refuse sympathy to so much wealth? He was obviously a much ill-used man, all the faults of course were on my side; in less than a month I found myself the centre of underhand intrigues and the victim of a hateful persecution. Friends, relatives, mere acquaintances in the world of emigration entered M. de Montevesso’s service. They spied on my conduct and tampered with the servants. There were assemblies in his house where my character was torn to shreds. Some of those good friends offered him their influence in Rome for the annulation of the marriage, for a consideration of course. Others discovered flaws in the marriage contract. They invented atrocious tales. There were even horrid verses made about that scandal; till at last he himself became disgusted with the wretches and closed his house and his purse to them. Years later he showed me a note of their names and the amounts paid for all those manifestations of sympathy. He must have been impressed and disgusted by the retrospect, because it was a big lot of money. As to the names, they were aristocratic enough to flatter his plebeian pride. He showed the list to me just to hurt my feelings.

===Chinese===
「那时我们结婚才一年出头。自婚礼以来，我第一次感到了真正的快乐。他们，那两位可怜的亲人，喜出望外。我们这一家子人都天真得可以，竟以为这一来我们所有的烦恼便都到了头，以为那男人倒颇有几分骑士风度，能够以应有的眼光看清自己的过错，并体面地承担后果。我不是明明白白地告诉过他，我永远也不会爱他吗？

「我早该知道他是毫无慷慨之心的。其实我也没怎么细想这件事。我这样一个年纪轻轻、却已克服了羞怯、足以在我所知甚少的那个世界里做一位无可挑剔的女主人的人——因为说到底，那一套本就在我的家教传统之内——在那十个月里，实在是太过愚蠢、太过不谙世事，竟没有从中学到半点教训。我什么也没有学到，就像一个人不会从一场噩梦或一段痛苦的病痛里学到什么一样。我不过是畅快地喘了口气罢了。我又变回了从前的那个阿黛勒。我把德·蒙特韦索先生从我的念想里赶了出去，仿佛他这个人从来没有存在过。你能相信吗，科斯莫？只要那些事还没有攻击到你对自身道德人格的那份自许，事实竟是这般难以打动一个人：种种暴行、辱骂、激烈的情绪、疯狂的妒性发作，居然都可以不留痕迹。这一切从我身上剥落下来，像一件下了毒的袍子，几乎没有留下半点灼痛。我抬起头来，像一朵花在雷雨过后抬起头来。别以为我的性情浅薄，科斯莫。我的内心也有可以触动的深处，只是在那之前，我所受到的不过是折磨、惊骇、惊愕，几乎连害怕都谈不上。受罪的是他。可这一回，就要轮到我了。」

「我想你从来就不是一个感情浅薄的人。」

「人的感情也像万事万物一样，非要成熟不可，而我向你保证，那时我还没有停止生长。接下来的六个月，才彻底完成了我所受的教养。因为到那时，我已经把一切幻想都丢光了。正当我在父亲与母亲之间畅快地喘息、把周围的世事都忘个干净的时候，蒙特韦索却满城地跑，到处诉苦、到处猜疑；既后悔放我走了，又恼恨我竟这么容易就离开了他。你大可相信，他是找得到同情者的。一个有钱人，你懂吗！这样一大笔财富，谁好意思拒绝给它一点同情呢？他显然是个备受委屈的人，一切的过错当然都在我这一边；不出一个月，我发觉自己已经成了一桩桩暗中密谋的中心，成了一场可憎的迫害的牺牲品。流亡圈里的朋友、亲戚，乃至仅仅面熟的人，都进了德·蒙特韦索先生的麾下。他们窥探我的举止，又勾结我家的仆人。他家常常聚会，在那些聚会上，我的人格被撕成了碎片。有些好朋友向他兜售自己在罗马的门路，说是可以替他办成这桩婚姻的废止，当然，是要报酬的。又有一些人在这纸婚约里挑出了破绽。他们编造出种种骇人的故事。甚至有人就那桩丑闻作了下流恶劣的诗；末了连他自己也对那帮无赖厌恶起来，对他们关上了家门和钱袋。多年之后，他给我看过一张单子，上面记着那些人的名字，以及为所有这些「同情之举」所付的款项。回望那一笔账，他一定是既震动又厌恶，因为那是一大笔钱。至于那些名字，倒是够贵族气派的，足以讨好他那平民出身（plebeian）的虚荣心。他把那张单子拿给我看，无非是为了刺痛我。

===Original===
“Some sinners have been stoned, but I, an innocent girl of seventeen, had been pelted with mud beyond endurance. It was impossible to induce him to come to any sort of arrangement that would leave me in peace. All the world, influenced by his paid friends, was against me. What could I do? Calumnies are hard to bear. Harder than truth. Even my parents weakened. He promised to make amends. Of course I went back to him, as one would crawl out of the mud amongst clean thorns that can but tear one’s flesh. He received me back with apologies that were as nearly public as such things can be. It was a vindication of my character. But directly he had me with him again he gave way to his fits of hatred as before, such hatred as only black jealousy can inspire. It was terrible. For even jealousy has its gradations, coloured by doubts and hopes, and his was the worst, the hopeless kind, since he could never forget my honest declaration.”

The Countess of Montevesso’s voice died out and then Cosmo looked up. She was a little pale, which made her eyes appear darker than ever he had seen them before. Cosmo was too young yet to understand the full meaning of this confession, but his very youth invested the facts with a sort of romantic grandeur, while the woman before him felt crushed by the feelings of their squalid littleness. Without looking at him she said:

“We went travelling for a year and a half, stayed for a time in Paris, where he began to make me scenes again, and then we went on to Italy. The pretext was to make me known to some of his relations. I don’t believe he could remember his mother, and his father, an old dealer in rabbit skins, I believe, had died some time before. As to the rest, I think his heart failed him notwithstanding the brutal pride he used at times to display to me. He took me to see some decayed people living in old ruined houses whom I verily believe he bribed to pass for his more distant connections. It was a pilgrimage amongst the most squalid shams, something that you cannot conceive, yet I didn’t rebel against the horrible humiliation of it. It was part of the bargain. Sometimes I thought that he would kill me in one of those wild places in some lost valley where the people, only a degree removed from peasants in their dress and speech, fawned upon him as the wealthy cousin and benefactor. I am certain that during those wanderings he was half distracted. It was I who went through all this unmoved. But I don’t suppose my life was ever in any danger. At that time none of his moods lasted long enough to let him carry out any definite purpose. And then he is not a man of criminal instincts. After all, he is perhaps a great adventurer. He has commanded armies of a hundred thousand men. He has in a sense faced the power of England in India. The very fact that he had managed to get out of it with so much wealth and with quite a genuine reputation shows that there is something in him. I don’t know whether it’s that that obtained for him a very gracious reception from Bonaparte when he dragged me back to Paris.”

===Chinese===
「有些罪人是被人拿石头砸的，可我，一个十七岁的清白姑娘，却被人拿烂泥一直泼到了忍无可忍的地步。要想劝他达成任何一种能让我过安宁日子的安排，是不可能的。整个世界，受了他那些花钱雇来的朋友的影响，都跟我作对。我能怎么办呢？诽谤是很难忍受的。比真相还难忍受。连我父母也动摇了。他答应要作出补偿。我当然又回到他身边去了，就像一个人从烂泥里爬出来，爬进一丛干净的荆棘里，那荆棘除了撕烂她的皮肉，再无别的本事。他以近乎公开的方式——这类事所能做到的近乎公开的方式——向我道歉，重新接纳了我。这是对我人格的一次洗雪。可一旦他又把我抓在手里，便又像从前一样，听任自己那一阵阵仇恨发作，那是只有漆黑的妒忌才能激起的仇恨。那是可怕的。因为妒忌也有它的等差，染着疑虑与希望的颜色，而他那种是最坏的一种，是最无可救药的一种，因为他永远忘不了我那番坦诚的告白。」

蒙特韦索伯爵夫人的声音渐渐低下去，科斯莫这才抬起头来。她的脸色有些发白，这使她的眼睛显得比他所见过的任何时候都更深暗。科斯莫还太年轻，还不能完全领会这一番自白的全部含义，可正是他的这份年轻，给这些事实披上了一层浪漫的壮丽色彩；而坐在他面前的这个女人，却被这些事实那种委琐渺小的感觉所压倒。她没有看他，开口说道：

「我们出去旅行了一年半，在巴黎住过一阵，他在那里又跟我闹了起来，随后我们便动身去了意大利。借口是要让我见见他的一些亲戚。我想他根本记不得自己的母亲，至于他的父亲——一个老兔皮贩子，我想——早些时候就已经去世了。至于其余的事，我想他到底还是怯了阵，尽管他有时还对我摆出一副粗暴的傲慢。他带我去见了一些破落户，他们住在年久失修的老宅子里，我深信这些人是被他收买了、冒充他远房亲戚的。那是一次穿行于最肮脏的假冒伪劣之间的朝圣，一种你无法想象的东西，可我并没有反抗其中那种可怕的羞辱。这本是那笔交易的一部分。有时我想，他会在那些蛮荒之地、在某个失落的山谷里把我杀了，那里的人，衣着谈吐只比庄稼人高出那么一点点，却谄媚地奉他为阔气的表亲和恩人。我确信，在那一番辗转漂泊之中，他有一半是发了狂的。倒是我，不动声色地经历了这一切。但我并不以为我的性命曾有过什么危险。那时候他的任何一种情绪都持续不了多久，不足以让他贯彻什么明确的目的。况且他又不是个生性犯罪的人。说到底，他也许是个了不起的冒险家。他曾统率过十万人的大军。在印度，他在某种意义上领教过英格兰的力量。他竟能带着那么多财富、又带着相当不错的名声从那里全身而退——光是这一桩，便足以说明他身上有某种东西。我不知道，是不是正因为这某种东西，后来他把我拖回巴黎时，波拿巴（Bonaparte）才给了他一次非常优礼的接见。」

### VI / VI

===Original===
Madame de Montevesso paused, looking at the white ashes in which the sparks had not died out yet. “Yes,” she went on, “I lived near Paris through the whole time of the Empire. I had a charming house in the country. Monsieur de Montevesso had established me in a style which he considered worthy of himself if not of me. He could never forgive me for being what I am. He was tolerated by the returned emigration for my sake, but he grew weary of his own unhappiness and resolved to live by himself in his own province where he could be a great personage. Perhaps he is not altogether a bad man. He consented eagerly to my parents, who had obtained permission to return to France, joining me in the country. I tasted again some happiness in the peace of our semi-retired life and in their affection. Our world was that of old society, the world of returned nobles. They hated and despised the imperial power, but most of them were ready to cringe before it. Yes, even the best were overawed by the real might under the tinsel of that greatness. Our circle was very small and composed of convinced royalists, but I could not share their hatreds and their contempts. I felt myself a Frenchwoman. I had liberal ideas.﻿ ﻿…”

She noticed Cosmo’s eyes fixed on her with eager and friendly curiosity, and paused with a faint smile.

“You understand me, Cosmo?” she asked. The latter gave a little nod without detaching his eyes from the face which seemed to him to glow with the light of generous feelings, but already Madame de Montevesso was going on.

===Chinese===
蒙特韦索夫人停了一停，望着那一炉白灰——灰里的火星尚未熄灭。「是的，」她接着说，「整个帝国（Empire）时期，我都住在巴黎近郊。我在乡间有一所惹人喜爱的宅子。蒙特韦索先生（Monsieur de Montevesso）把我安置在一种排场里，那排场是他认为配得上他自己、纵然不配我也无妨的。他永远也不能原谅我之为我。那些归国的流亡贵族（émigré）看在我的份上才容忍了他，可他对自己那份不快渐渐厌倦了，便决意独自住到他自己的领地去，在那儿他能做个要人。也许他总算不得一个十足的坏人。他倒是很爽快地答应了让我父母——他们已获准回到法兰西——到乡间来与我同住。在他们半隐居的生活的宁静里，在他们的慈爱里，我又尝到了一些幸福的滋味。我们所在的那个圈子，是旧日上流社会的天地，是归国贵族的天地。他们憎恶帝国的权力、藐视它，可他们大多数又都随时准备在它面前卑躬屈膝。是的，便是其中最优秀的人，也被那伟大之物的华彩底下真正的力量所震慑。我们的圈子极小，成员都是些坚定的保皇党（royalist），可我却无法认同他们的种种憎恨与种种蔑视。我自觉是个法兰西女人。我怀抱自由的理念。……」

她察觉科斯莫的眼睛正热切而友善地盯着她，便停了下来，淡淡一笑。

「你懂我的意思吧，科斯莫？」她问。科斯莫微微一点头，目光却没有离开那张脸——在他看来，那脸上正焕发出宽厚情怀的光彩；可蒙特韦索夫人已经又接着说了下去。

===Original===
“I did not want to be patronized by all those returned duchesses who wanted to teach me how to feel and how to behave. Their own behaviour was a mixture of insolence and self-seeking before that government which they feared and despised. I didn’t fear it but neither could I despise it. My heart was heavy during all those years but it was not downcast. All Europe was aflame and the blaze scorched and dazzled and filled one with awe and with forebodings; but then one always heard that fire purifies all which it cannot destroy. The world would perhaps come out better from it.”

“Well, it’s all over,” said Cosmo, “and what has it done? The smoke hangs about yet and I cannot see, but how do you feel?”

Madame de Montevesso, leaning on her elbow on the mantelpiece, with one foot on the fender, looked down at the ashes in which a spark gleamed here and there.

“I feel a little cold,” she said, “and dazed perhaps. One doesn’t know where to look.”

Cosmo got up and made a step forward. His voice, however, was subdued. “Formerly there was a man.”

===Chinese===
「我不想被那些归国的公爵夫人们居高临下地照拂，她们一个个都要教我该怎样感受、该怎样行事。她们自己呢，在那个她们既畏惧又蔑视的政府面前，行径不过是傲慢与钻营的混杂。我并不畏惧它，可我也不能蔑视它。那些年里，我的心头沉重，却并不颓丧。整个欧洲都在燃烧，那烈焰灼人、眩人、叫人心生敬畏与不祥之兆；可人又总听说，火能净化一切它所无法毁掉的东西。这世界也许会从中涅槃出一个更好的样子来。」

「嗯，这都过去了，」科斯莫说，「可它又留下了什么呢？烟雾到今天还萦绕不散，我看不真切——可你觉得怎样？」

蒙特韦索夫人一只手肘撑在壁炉台上，一只脚踩着炉栏，低头望着那炉灰，灰里这儿那儿还闪着一点火星。

「我觉得有点冷，」她说，「也许还有点发懵。人不知该往哪儿看。」

科斯莫站起身，向前迈了一步。然而他的声音是压低了的。「从前有那么一个人。」

===Original===
“A man, yes. One couldn’t help looking towards him. There was something unnatural in that uniqueness, but do you know, Cosmo, the man was nothing. You smile, you think you hear a royalist speaking, a woman full of silly aristocratic prejudice; a woman who sees only a small Corsican squire who hadn’t even the sense to catch the opportunity by the hair as it flew by and be the restorer of the Bourbon dynasty. You imagine all that of me!﻿ ﻿… Of me!”

She kept her pose, desolate, as if looking down at the ashes of a burnt-up world.

“I don’t think you could be stupid if you tried,” he said. “But if the man was nothing, then what has done it?”
