The doctor, a man of special capacity in confronting enigmatical situations, showed himself as perplexed before this one as the most innocent of mortals.

“I don’t know. It seems to me that a man who puts on his hat and cloak before vanishing like this must turn up again. He ought to be given a chance to do so at any rate. He left all his money behind, too. I mean even to the small change.”

===Chinese===
「我想我不必致歉，」他说，带着一种恼怒的动作，像是一个被刺中要害的人。「当然，我本可以径去办我自己的事，那些事对相当多的人都颇为要紧。我对莱瑟姆先生的劝告是离开热那亚，既然他似乎并无留下的目的，似乎又隐约生出去厄尔巴岛（Elba）一游的念头。我建议他把里窝那（Leghorn）作为渡海最便利的港口。」

窗前那个女人究竟有没有在听他说话，实在无从判断。她一动不动，似乎已经忘了他的存在。但那种纹丝不动，也许亦是全神贯注的结果。他打定主意接着说下去。

「他的心思、他的想象，似乎都围着拿破仑（Napoleon）转。在我看来，那是他此行唯一的缘由。」他停了一下。

「我相信，莱瑟姆先生到热那亚来的唯一缘由，就是来看看我们。」蒙特韦索夫人转过身来，朝那张扶手椅走回。她极其苍白。「我是说父亲和我，」她解释道，「前天早上他来看我。我请他来参加我们寻常的傍晚招待。别人都走之后他还留着。是我请他留下的。可父亲需要我，我不得不把莱瑟姆先生留给蒙特韦索先生（Monsieur de Montevesso）陪着。」

医生温和地打断了她。「我知道，夫人。侯爵派人去请您丈夫时，我正和侯爵在那宫里，就在那个房间里。」

「我忘了。」蒙特韦索夫人坦率地承认。「可莱瑟姆先生是安然回到他客栈的呀。」

「是的。第二天他写信一直写到深夜，似乎正写着写着就被凭空摄走了。可像莱瑟姆先生这样的人，不会被人硬从卧室里掳走。我劝那仆人等到四点钟，然后我便径直到这里来了。」

「等到四点钟，」蒙特韦索夫人低声重复。

医生，一个面对扑朔迷离的局面别有专长的人，在这一桩面前，却显得与最天真的凡人一样无所适从。

「我不知道。在我看来，一个人像这样消失之前还戴上帽子、披上斗篷，那他一定会再出现。至少该给他一个再出现的机会。他还把所有的钱都留下了。我是说连零钱都留下了。」

===Original===
The glimpse of helpless concern in that man affected Adèle with a feeling of actual bodily anguish. She got brusquely out of the *bergère* and moved into the middle of the room. The doctor, letting go the back of the chair, turned to face her.

“I am appalled,” she murmured.

This came out as if extracted from her by torture. It moved the doctor more than anything he had heard for years. His voice sank into a soothing murmur.

“I do believe, Madame, that if there had been a murder committed last night anywhere in this town I would have heard something about it this morning. My inn is just the place for such news. I will go back there now. I shall question his servant again. He may give us a gleam of light.”

Her intent, distressed gaze was unbearable, yet held him bound to the spot. It was difficult to abandon a woman in that state! He became aware of the sound of voices outside the door. Some sort of dispute. He hastened to make his bow, and Madame de Montevesso, moving after him, whispered eagerly: “Yes! A gleam of light! Do let me know. I won’t draw a free breath till I hear something.”

Her extended arms dropped by her side a moment before the door flew open and Bernard was heard announcing with calm formality:

“Signorina Clelia.”

===Chinese===
那男人脸上那一闪而无助的关切，让阿黛勒感受到一种近乎肉体的痛楚。她霍地从扶手椅里起身，走到房间中央。医生松开椅背，转过身来面对她。

「我惊骇万分。」她低声道。

这一句话吐出来，仿佛是受了酷刑才逼出的。它之触动医生，胜过他多年来所听到的任何一句话。他的声音低下去，化作一声抚慰的低语。

「夫人，我确确实实相信，昨夜这城里任何角落若发生过一桩命案，今早我总会听到些风声。我住的客栈正是这种消息汇聚之地。我这便回去。我要再盘问他的仆人。他也许能给我们一线光明。」

她那专注而痛苦的目光令人难以承受，却又把他钉在原地不动。要把一个这般境况的女人撇下，实在于心难忍！他察觉到门外有人声。是一场争执。他赶忙告辞鞠躬，蒙特韦索夫人跟着他动了动，急切地低语：「是啊！一线光明！千万要让我知道。在听到什么之前，我连一口自在气都喘不出来。」

她伸出的双臂垂落到身侧，几乎同一刹那，门一下子开了，只听得贝尔纳以平静而正式的口吻通报：

「克莱莉娅小姐（Signorina Clelia）。」

===Original===
The doctor, turning away from Madame de Montevesso, saw “that little wretch” standing just within the room, evidently very much taken aback by the unexpected meeting. He guessed that she had snatched at some opportunity to escape from the old women. It had given her no time to pull on her stockings, a fact made evident by the shortness of the dark petticoat which, with a white jacket, comprised all her costume. She had managed to thrust her bare feet into a pair of old slippers, and her loose hair, tied with a blue ribbon at the back of her head, produced a most incongruous effect of neatness. Her invasion was alarming and inexplicable. The doctor, as he passed out, compressed his lips and stared fiercely with some idea of scaring her into good behaviour. She met this demonstration with a round stupid stare of astonishment. The next moment he found himself outside in the corridor alone with Bernard, who had shut the door quietly and remained with his back to it. The exasperated doctor looked him up and down coolly.

“How long have you been in the habit of hanging about your lady’s door, my friend?” he asked with ominous familiarity.

The simple-minded factotum of the London days, the lovelorn naive swain of the mulatto maid, was a figure of the past now. The doctor was confronted by a calm unmoved servant of much experience, somewhat inclining to stoutness, made respectable by the black well-fitting clothes. He did not flinch at the question, but he took his time. At last he said with the utmost placidity:

“Many years now. Pretty near all my life.”

===Chinese===
医生转过身离开蒙特韦索夫人时，看见「那小丫头」就站在房门内侧，显然被这次意外的相遇弄得十分错愕。他猜她是抓住了什么机会，从那几个老太婆身边溜了出来。这使她连袜子都来不及穿上，那条深色衬裙短了一截，已把这点暴露无遗——衬裙加一件白色短上衣，便是她全身上下的装束。她勉强把一双赤脚塞进一双旧拖鞋里，松散的头发用一根蓝缎带在脑后扎起，反倒造成一种极不相称的整洁之感。她这一闯入，既令人惊慌，又无法解释。医生走过她身边时，紧紧抿住嘴唇，凶狠地瞪着她，指望把她吓唬得规矩些。她以一脸圆睁的、呆头呆脑的惊讶神情回报这一示威。下一刻，他发觉自己已到了外面的走廊里，单独和贝尔纳在一起——后者已悄然带上房门，背靠着门站着。恼怒的医生冷冷地把他从头到脚打量了一番。

「我的朋友，你这种守在你们夫人门口的把戏，惯了有多久了？」他带着不祥的熟稔口气问。

伦敦那段时日里那个心思单纯的杂差，那个苦恋混血女仆的多情天真的傻小子，如今已是过去的人物了。医生面对的，是一个镇定不动声色的、阅历深厚的仆人，身材略显发福，一身合身的黑衣使他显得颇为体面。听了这问题他并不退缩，却不慌不忙。末了他以极度平和的口气说道：

「许多年了。差不多打我记事起就是。」

===Original===
The tone was well calculated to surprise the doctor. Taking advantage of the latter’s silence, Bernard paused before he continued reasonably: “Was I to let her rush in unannounced on Madame la Comtesse while you were there? I tried to send her away but she would think nothing of filling the air with her screams. I kept her back as long as it was prudent﻿ ﻿…” He raised his open hand, palm outwards, warning the doctor to remain silent, while with conscientious gravity he applied his big ear to the door. When he came away he did not apparently intend to take any further notice of the doctor, but stood there with an air of perfect rectitude.

“Is that your constant practice?” asked the astonished doctor, with curiosity rather than indignation. “Suppose I told on you,” he added with a glance at the door. “Suppose somebody else gave you away.﻿ ﻿…”

“You would not be thanked,” was the unexpected answer.

“I wouldn’t be believed﻿—is that it? Well, I confess that I can hardly believe my own eyes.”

“Oh, you would be believed,” was the ready but dispassionate admission. Bernard’s trust in his interlocutor’s acuteness was not deceived.

“Do you mean that you have been found out already?” said the doctor in a changed tone. “Whew! You don’t say! Well, stranger things have happened. Whose old retainer are you?”

“I have always belonged to Madame la Comtesse,” said the old retainer without looking at the doctor, who, after some meditation, accepted the statement at its face value.

“I am staying at Cantelucci’s inn,” he said in an ordinary conversational tone. “And I would be glad to see you there any time you like to call. Especially if you had anything to tell me of Mr. Latham.” Bernard not responding in any way to that invitation, the doctor added, “You know what I mean?”

“Oh, yes, I know what you mean.” That answer came coldly from the lips of the respectable servant, who said nothing more while conscientiously escorting the doctor to the anteroom at the foot of the grand staircase. A little bowed old woman in black clothes clung to the balustrade halfway down the marble steps, in the act of descending, while another, taller and upright, hovered anxiously on the landing above. Bernard’s scandalized, “Go away! Go back!” sounded irresistibly authoritative. The doctor had no doubt that it would send the two crones back to their lair, but he did not stop to see.

===Chinese===
这口吻是精心算计好、要让医生吃一惊的。趁医生沉默之际，贝尔纳停了停，才又合情合理地接下去：「您在里头的时候，难道我能让她连个通报都没有就冲到伯爵夫人面前吗？我想把她打发走，可她才不管那么多，要扯着嗓子尖叫把空气都填满呢。我尽了谨慎所能，把她拦住……」他张开手掌，掌心朝外，警告医生不要出声，一面以一丝不苟的郑重，把他那只大耳朵贴到门上。听完之后他走开，显然不打算再理会医生，只带着一脸十足端正的神气站在那里。

「你一向都是这么做的吗？」吃惊的医生问，与其说是愤慨，不如说是好奇。「假如我去告发你呢？」他朝门瞥了一眼，又补了一句。「假如别人出卖了你呢……」

「您不会受人感谢的。」这是个出乎意料的回答。

「不会有人信我——是这个意思吗？好吧，我承认，我连自己的眼睛都几乎不敢相信。」

「哦，会有人信您的。」这是他爽快却不动声色的承认。贝尔纳对自己这位对话者的敏锐，并未信错。

「你是说，这档子事早已被人识破了？」医生换了一种口气说。「嚯！真有你的！嗯，更稀奇的事也不是没发生过。你是哪家的老仆？」

「我一向都是属于伯爵夫人的。」这老仆说，并不看医生。医生沉吟片刻，便照字面接受了这句话。

「我住在坎泰洛奇的客栈，」他用一种寻常闲谈的口吻说，「您什么时候肯光临，我都乐意恭候。尤其是，您若有什么关于莱瑟姆先生的消息要告诉我。」贝尔纳对这邀请毫无回应，医生便又补一句：「您明白我的意思？」

「哦，明白，我明白您的意思。」这一句冷冷地从那位体面仆人的唇间说出，他再没多话，只是一丝不苟地把医生陪送到大楼梯脚下那间前厅。一个穿黑衣、略有些伛偻的老妇正攀在大理石楼梯中段的栏杆上，作势要下楼；另一个较高的、身板挺直的，则焦急地在上面那层楼梯口徘徊。贝尔纳那义愤填膺的「走开！回去！」听上去有一种不容抗拒的威严。医生毫不怀疑，这会打发那两个丑老婆子回到她们的老巢去，但他并未停下身来看。

===Original===
Bernard went up the staircase slowly to the first landing, where he watched the retreat of the two weird apparitions down a long and dim corridor. They were very much intimidated by this man in black and with a priestly aspect. One of them, however, made a stand, and screamed in an angry cracked voice, “Where’s the child? The child! We are looking for her.”

“Why don’t you take her back to your village and keep her there?” he cried out sternly. “I have seen her. She won’t get lost.”

A distant door slammed. They had vanished as if blown away by his voice; and Bernard with a muttered afterthought, “More’s the pity,” continued up one flight of stairs after another till he reached the wilderness of the garrets that once upon a time had been inhabited by a multitude of servants and retainers. The room he entered was low and sombre, with rough walls and a vast bare floor. His wife Aglae sat on the edge of the bed, with her hands in her lap and downcast eyes which she did not raise at his entrance. He looked at her with a serious and friendly expression before he sat down by her side. And even then she did not move. He took her tragic immobility in silence as a matter of course. His face, which had never been very mobile, had acquired with years a sort of blank dignity. It had been the work of years, of those married years which had crushed the last vestiges of pertness out of the more emotional Aglae. When she whispered to him, “Bernard, this thing kill me a little every day,” he felt moved to put his arm round his wife’s waist and made a mental remark which always occurred to him poignantly on such occasions, that she had grown very thin. In the trials of a life which had not kept its promise of contented bliss, he had been most impressed by the loss of that plumpness which years ago was so much appreciated by him. It seemed to give to that plaint which he had heard before more than once an awful sort of reality, a dreadful precision.﻿ ﻿… A little.﻿ ﻿… Every day.

He took his arm away brusquely and got up.

“I thought I would find you here,” he remarked in an indifferent marital tone. “That man has gone now,” he added.

With a deep sigh the maid of Madame de Montevesso struggled out of the depths of despondency, only to fall a prey to anxiety.

“Oh, Bernard, what did that man want with Miss Adèle?”

Bernard knew enough to have formed a conjecture that English fellow must have either left some papers or a message for the Marquis with Madame de Montevesso.

===Chinese===
贝尔纳缓缓走上楼梯，到了第一个楼梯口，在那里看着那两个怪影似的人影，沿一条长长的、幽暗的走廊退去。她们对这身黑衣、一副教士气派的男人十分忌惮。其中一个却立定了，用一种气愤而干哑的嗓子尖叫：「那孩子呢？孩子！我们在找她。」

「你们怎么不把她带回你们村子去，把她留在那儿？」他厉声喝道，「我见过她了。她丢不了。」

一扇远处门砰地关上。她们消失了，仿佛被他的声音吹散了一般。贝尔纳咕哝着补了一句「只可惜」，又一级接一级地往上登楼梯，直登到那一片阁楼的荒原——从前这里曾住着一大群仆人和家仆。他走进的那间屋子低矮而阴沉，墙粗粝，地面空旷。他的妻子阿格莱（Aglae）坐在床沿，双手放在膝上，眼睛低垂，他进屋时她也不抬眼。他用一种严肃而友善的神情望了她一会儿，才在她身边坐下。即便那时，她也一动不动。她那悲剧性的一动不动，他沉默地视为理所当然，毫不奇怪。他的脸一向不大多表情，这些年来又添了一种呆板的尊严。那是岁月之功，是那些婚后的年月——它们把更具性情的阿格莱身上最后一丝泼辣也碾得干干净净——的功劳。当她对他低语：「贝尔纳，这件事每天都要把我杀死一点点。」他动了恻隐之心，伸手搂住妻子的腰，心里掠过一个念头——每逢这种场合，这念头总要刺他一下——她瘦了许多。在这并未兑现其当初那份安居乐业之诺的一生种种磨难中，最令他印象深刻的，便是她那副丰满——多年以前他曾多么欣赏——的消逝。这仿佛给她那句他不止一次听过的话，赋予了一种可怕的真实感，一种可怖的精确。……一点点。……每一天。

他猛地把胳膊抽开，站了起来。

「我想我会在这儿找到你，」他用一种淡然的、做丈夫的口吻说道，「那个人已经走了。」他又补了一句。

蒙特韦索夫人的女仆长叹一声，从沮丧的深渊里挣出来，却又落入焦虑之手。

「哦，贝尔纳，那个人找阿黛勒小姐有什么事？」

贝尔纳所知道的，已足以让他作出这样的推测：那个英国小伙子，准是给侯爵留了些文件，或是给蒙特韦索夫人留了个口信。

## Part IV / 第四卷

### I / I

===Original===
In what seemed to him a very short time Cosmo found himself under the colonnade separating the town piled upon the hills from the flat ground of the waterside. A profound quietness reigned on the darkly polished surface of the harbour and the long, incurved range of the quays. This quietness that surrounded him on all sides through which, beyond the spars of clustered coasters, he could look at the night-horizon of the open sea, relieved that fantastic feeling of confinement within his own body with its intolerable tremors and shrinkings and imperious suggestions. Mere weaknesses all. His desire, however, to climb to the top of the tower, as if only there complete relief could be found for his captive spirit, was as strong as ever.

The only light on shore he could see issued in a dim streak from the door of the guardhouse which he had passed on his return from the tower on his first evening in Genoa. As he did not wish to pass near the Austrian sentry at the head of the landing-steps, Cosmo, instead of following the quay, kept under the portico at the back of the guardhouse. When he came to its end he had a view of the squat bulk of the tower across a considerable space of flat waste ground extending to the low rocks of the seashore. He made for it with the directness of a man possessed by a fixed idea. When he reached the iron-studded low door within the deep dark archway at the foot of the tower he found it immovable. Locked! How stupid! As if those heavy ship guns up there could be stolen! Disappointed, he leaned his shoulders against the side of the deep arch, lingering as people will before the finality of a closed door or of a situation without issue.

His superstitious mood had left him. An old picture was an old picture; and probably the face of that noble saint copied from an old triptych and of Madame de Montevesso were not at all alike. At most, a suggestion which may have been the doing of the copyist and so without meaning. A copyist is not an inspired person; not a seer of visions. He felt critical, almost ironic, towards the Cosmo of the morning, the Cosmo of the day, the Cosmo rushing away like a scared child from a fanciful resemblance, that probably did not even exist. What was he doing there? He might have asked the way to the public gardens. Lurking within the dark archway, muffled up in his blue cloak, he was a suspect figure like an ambushed *spadassin* waiting for his victim, or a conspirator hiding from the minions of a tyrant. “I am perfectly ridiculous,” he thought. “I had better go back as soon as I can.” This was his sudden conclusion, but he did not move. It struck him that he was not anxious to face his empty room. Was he ready to get into another panic? he asked himself scornfully.﻿ ﻿… At that moment he heard distinctly the sound of whispering as if through the wall, or from above or from the ground. He held his breath. The whispering went on, loquacious. When it stopped, another voice, as low but deeper and more distinct, muttered the words: “The hour is past.”

===Chinese===
仿佛只过了极短的一会儿，科斯莫便发觉自己已置身于那道柱廊之下；那柱廊把堆叠在山坡上的城区与水边平坦的地面分隔开来。一种深沉的寂静笼罩着港湾那幽暗光洁的水面，也笼罩着那一长列向内弯作弧形的码头。这四面包围着他的寂静——透过它，越过簇拥在一起的一艘艘沿海小帆船的桅杆，他可以望见外海夜色里的地平线——使他身体里那作祟的困顿之感得以缓解：那不可忍受的战栗、畏缩，以及专横的种种暗示。这一切不过是懦弱罢了。然而他要攀上塔顶去的欲望，却仍和方才一样强烈，仿佛唯有在那上面，他那被囚禁的灵魂才能找到彻底的解脱。

他在岸上所能看见的唯一一点光亮，是从那间警卫室的门里透出的一缕昏暗的光带——他头一个夜晚在热那亚（Genoa）从塔回来时，就曾经过那间警卫室。因为不想从登岸石阶顶上那个奥地利哨兵的近旁经过，科斯莫没有顺着码头走，而是沿着警卫室背后那道有顶的廊下走去。走到廊的尽头，他望见隔着一大片一直延伸到海边低矮礁石的平坦荒地，那边立着那座塔敦实的身躯。他朝它径直走去，那股劲儿就像一个被某种执念缠住了的人。待他来到塔脚下那条幽深黑暗的拱道里那扇钉满铁钉的矮门前，却发现它纹丝不动。锁上了！多蠢啊！好像上头那些沉重的舰炮能被人偷走似的！他大失所望，把肩膀靠在深拱的壁上，就像人在一扇紧闭的门前、或在一个走投无路的处境面前往往会徘徊流连那样，也徘徊起来。

他那种迷信的心绪已经离他而去。一幅古画不过是一幅古画罢了；那一幅从某座古老三联屏上临摹下来的高贵圣徒的面容，与蒙特韦索夫人（Madame de Montevesso）的面容，多半并不相像。至多不过是临摹者一手造成的某种联想，因而并无意义。临摹者并不是受神灵感召的人，不是能看见异象的先知。他几乎带着一种近乎讥讽的挑剔，看待早晨的科斯莫、白昼里的科斯莫、那个像受惊的孩子一般，从一种多半根本不存在的、凭空的相似里仓皇逃开的科斯莫。他在那儿做什么呢？他本可以去打听去公共花园怎么走。他裹在那件蓝色斗篷里，潜伏在幽暗的拱道中，活像一个伺机等候猎物的伏击刺客（*spadassin*），又像一个躲避暴君爪牙的密谋者。「我简直是可笑极了，」他想，「我还是尽快回去为好。」这是他骤然间得出的结论，可他并没有动。他忽然意识到，自己并不急于面对那间空荡荡的房间。他是不是又准备好要惊惶一场了？他鄙夷地问自己。……就在那一刻，他清清楚楚地听见了有人悄声低语的声音，仿佛是隔着墙壁传来的，又仿佛是从头顶、或从地下传来的。他屏住了呼吸。那低语还在继续，絮絮叨叨。等它停下，另一个声音，同样低却更深沉、更清晰，咕哝出这样几个字：「时辰已过了。」

===Original===
Ha! Whispers in the air, sounds wandering without bodies, as mysterious as though they had come across a hundred miles, for he had heard no footsteps, no rustle, no sound of any sort. Nothing but the two voices. They were so weirdly disembodied and unbelievable that he had to clothe them in attributes: the excitable﻿—the morose. They were quite near. But he did not know on which side of the arch within which he was hiding. For he was frankly hiding now﻿—no doubt about it. He had remembered that he had left his pistols by his bedside. And he was certain he would hear the voices again. The wait seemed long before the fluent-loquacious came back through space and was punctually followed by the deep voice which this time emitted only an unintelligible grunt.

The disagreeable sense of having no means of defence in case of necessity prevented Cosmo from leaving the shelter of the deep arch. Two men, the excitable and the morose, were within a foot of him. Remembering that the tower was accessible on its seaward face, Cosmo surmised that they had just landed from a boat and had crept round barefooted, secret and, no doubt, ready to use their knives. Smugglers probably. That they should ply their trade within three hundred yards of the guardroom with a sentry outside did not surprise him very much. These were Austrian soldiers, ignorant of local conditions and certainly not concerned with the prevention of smuggling. Why didn’t these men go about their business, then? The road was clear. But perhaps they had gone? It seemed to him he had been there glued to that door for an hour. As a matter of fact it was not ten minutes. Cosmo, who had no mind to be stabbed through a mere mistake as to his character, was just thinking of making a dash in the direction of the guardhouse when the morose but cautiously lowered voice began, close to the arch, abruptly: “Where did the beast get to? I thought a moment ago he was coming. Didn’t you think too that there were footsteps﻿—just as we landed?”

===Chinese===
哈！空气里的低语，没有形体的声音在游荡，神秘得像是跨越了一百里地才传来的，因为他没有听见脚步声，没有听见窸窣声，也没有听见任何一种声响。除了那两个声音，什么也没有。它们诡异得无形无体、不可思议，他不得不给它们各自披上些特征：一个易惊多话，一个阴沉寡言。它们就在近旁。可他不知道是在他所藏身的这道拱的哪一侧。因为他此刻确确实实是在藏身——这一点毫无疑问。他想起来，自己把手枪留在了床边。而且他确信自己还会再听见那两个声音。这一回的等待似乎很长，那口齿伶俐、絮絮叨叨的声音才又穿过虚空传回来，紧接着照例是那个低沉的声音，这一回却只发出一声听不清的咕哝。

那种一旦有事便毫无防身之物的别扭之感，使科斯莫不肯离开这道深拱的庇护。两个人，那个易惊多话的和那个阴沉寡言的，离他不过一尺之遥。科斯莫想起这座塔朝海的那一面是可以上下的，便揣测他们刚从一只船上登陆，赤着脚，悄无声息地绕了过来，而且毫无疑问，随时准备动刀子。多半是走私的人。他们竟在离警卫室不过三百码、门外还有哨兵的地方干他们的营生，这在他看来也不算太出奇。那些都是奥地利兵，对本地情形一无所知，自然也无意查禁走私。那么这两个人为什么还不各干各的去呢？路上清清静静。可也许他们已经走了？他觉得自己仿佛已在那儿贴着那扇门站了一个钟头。其实还不到十分钟。科斯莫可不愿意因为别人错认了他的身份而挨上一刀，他正想朝警卫室那个方向冲过去，那阴沉却已小心翼翼压低了嗓门的声音，就在拱边突如其来地开口了：「那畜生跑到哪儿去了？我刚才还以为他要来了。咱们刚上岸那会儿，你不是也听见了脚步声吗——是不是？」

===Original===
Cosmo’s uplifted foot came down to the ground. Of the excitable whisperer’s long rigmarole not a word could be made out. Cosmo imagined him short and thick. The other, whom Cosmo pictured to himself as lean and tall, uttered the word, “Why?” The excitable man hissed fiercely: “To say goodbye.”﻿—“Devil take all these women,” commented the morose voice dispassionately. The whisper, now raised to the pitch of a strangled wheeze, remarked with some feeling: “He may never see her again.”

It was clear they had never even dreamt of any human being besides themselves having anything to do on this part of the shore at this hour of the night. “Won’t they be frightened when I rush out,” thought Cosmo taking off his cloak and throwing it over his left forearm. If it came to an encounter he could always drop it. But he did not seriously think that he would be reduced to using his fists.

He judged it prudent to leave the archway with a bound which would get him well clear of the tower, and on alighting faced about quickly. He heard an exclamation but he saw no one. They had bolted! He would have laughed had he not been startled himself by a shot fired somewhere in the distance behind his back﻿—the most brutally impressive sound that can break the silence of the night. Instantly, as if it had been a signal, a lot of shouting broke on his ear, yells of warning and encouragement, a savage clamour which made him think of a lot of people pursuing a mad dog. He advanced, however, in the direction of the portico, wishing himself out of the way of this odious commotion, when the flash of a musket shot showed him for a moment the tilted head in a shako and the white cross-belts of an Austrian soldier standing erect in the middle of the open ground. Cosmo stopped short, then inclined to the left, moving cautiously and staring into the darkness. The yelling had died out gradually away from the seashore, where he remembered a cluster of the poorer sort of houses nestled under the cliffs. He could not believe that the shot could have been fired at him, till another flash and report of a musket followed by the whizz of the bullet very near his hand persuaded him to the contrary. Thinking of nothing but getting out of the line of fire, he stooped low and ran on blindly till his shoulder came in contact with some obstacle extremely hard and perfectly immovable.

===Chinese===
科斯莫那只抬起了一半的脚落回了地面。那个易惊多话的低语者那一长串颠三倒四的话，他一个字也没听清。科斯莫想象他是个矮胖的人。另一个人，科斯莫在心目中把他描画成又高又瘦，只吐出一个字：「干什么？」那易惊多话的人恶狠狠地嘘道：「去道别。」「这些女人统统见鬼去吧，」那阴沉的声音冷冷地评说道。那低语，此刻已被抬到一种像是被掐住了脖子的喘息的调门，带着几分动情地说：「他也许再也见不着她了。」

显而易见，他们做梦也没有想到，夜里这个时辰在这片海滩上，除了他们自己之外还会有别的什么人有事要做。「等我冲出去，他们还不知要吓成什么样呢，」科斯莫一边想，一边脱下斗篷，搭在左臂上。万一真要动起手来，他随时可以把它丢开。可他并没有当真觉得自己会落到非动拳头不可的地步。

他认为谨慎起见，应当一纵跳出拱道，好让自己远远地离开那座塔，于是一落地便迅疾地回过身来。他听见一声惊呼，却没看见一个人影。他们溜了！他本会笑出声来，要不是他自己也被身后远处某个地方响起的一声枪响吓了一跳——那是能打破夜之寂静的种种声响里，最蛮横、最撼人心魄的一种。霎时间，仿佛这就是个信号，一片喧嚷冲进他的耳朵，那是警告与鼓动的呼喊，一片野蛮的嘈杂，使他想起一大群人追捕一条疯狗。然而他还是朝那道廊下走去，巴不得离这可憎的骚乱远一点；就在这时，一次毛瑟枪射击的火光，让他一瞬间看清了一个歪戴着筒状军帽的头颅，以及一个笔挺地站在空地中央的奥地利士兵身上的白色交叉背带。科斯莫猛地停住，接着朝左偏去，一边小心地挪动，一边凝视着黑暗。那片呼喊渐渐朝着海滩的方向消歇下去——他记得那里有一片较为寒酸的房屋，相依在悬崖底下。他不敢相信那枪是冲他放的，直到又一次火光一闪、一声枪响，紧接着一颗子弹从他手边嗖地飞过，才叫他不得不信。他什么也顾不上想，只想着躲开火力线，便弯下腰，盲目地往前跑，直到肩膀撞上了一样极其坚硬、纹丝不动的东西。

===Original===
He put his hand on it, felt it rough and cold, and discovered it was a stone, an enormous square block such as are used in building breakwaters. Several others were lying about in a cluster like a miniature village on a miniature plain. He crept amongst them, spread his cloak on the ground, and sat down with his back against one of the blocks. He wondered at the marvellous eyesight of that confounded soldier. He was not aware that his dark figure had the starry sky for a background. “He nearly had me,” he thought. His whole being recoiled with disgust from the risk of getting a musket ball through his body. He resolved to remain where he was till all that incomprehensible excitement had quietened down and that brute with wonderful powers of vision had gone away. Then his road would be clear. He would give him plenty of time.

The stillness all around continued, becoming more convincing, as the time passed, in its suggestion of everything being over, convincing enough to shame timidity itself. Why this reluctance to go back to his room? What was a room in an inn, in any house? A small portion of space forced off with bricks or stones in which innumerable individuals had been alone with their good and evil thoughts, temptations, fears, troubles of all sorts, and had gone out without leaving a trace. This train of thought led him to the reflection that no man could leave his troubles behind﻿ ﻿… never﻿ ﻿… never.﻿ ﻿… “It’s no use trying,” he thought with despair. Why should he go to Livorno? What would be the good of going home? Lengthening the distance would be like lengthening a chain. What use would it be to get out of sight?﻿ ﻿… “If I were to be struck blind tomorrow it wouldn’t help me.” He forgot where he was till the convincing silence round him crumbled to pieces before a faint and distant shout which recalled him to the sense of his position. Presently he heard more shouting, still distant but much nearer. This took his mind from himself and started his imagination on another track. The man hunt was not over, then! The fellow had broken cover again and had been headed towards the tower. He depicted the hunted man to himself as long-legged, spare, agile, for no other reason than because he wished him to escape. He wondered whether the soldier with the sharp eyes would give him a shot. But no shot broke the silence which had succeeded the distant shouts. Got away perhaps? At least for a time. Very possibly he had stabbed somebody and﻿ ﻿… by heaven! here he was!

===Chinese===
他把手放上去，摸着它又粗又冷，发觉那是一块石头，一块极大的方块石，是那种用来修建防波堤的石块。附近还零零落落地躺着好几块，聚作一堆，像一片微型平原上的一座微型村落。他爬到它们中间，把斗篷铺在地上，背靠着其中一块坐了下来。他惊叹那个可恶的士兵竟有那样惊人的眼力。他不知道自己那漆黑的人影是以满天星斗为背景的。「他险些儿打中我，」他想。一想到有可能挨上一颗毛瑟枪子弹，他整个的人都带着厌恶缩了回去。他决意留在原地，等到这一场莫名其妙的骚动全都平息下去、那个眼力惊人的畜生走开了再说。到那时他的路就通畅了。他要给他留出充裕的时间。

四周的寂静仍在持续，随着时间一分一秒地过去，那种万事皆休的意味越来越叫人信服，信服到足以叫胆怯本身也自惭形秽。他为什么这般不情愿回到自己的房间去呢？旅店里的一间房，随便哪所房子里的一间房，究竟算得了什么？不过是砖石隔出来的一小块空间，无数的人曾在其中独自面对自己善恶的念头、诱惑、恐惧、种种烦扰，而后走出去，不留一丝痕迹。这一串思绪引他想到：没有任何人能把自己的烦扰抛在身后……永远不能……永远不能。……「挣扎也是徒劳，」他绝望地想。他何必去里窝那（Livorno）呢？回家又有什么好处？把距离拉长，不过是像把一条链子放长罢了。眼不见又有什么用？……「就算我明天瞎了，也无济于事。」他忘了自己身在何处，直到四周那叫人信服的寂静，被一声隐约而遥远的呼喊击得粉碎，才把他唤回到对自己处境的觉察。不久他又听见了更多的呼喊，依旧遥远，却近了许多。这一来他的心思从自己身上挪开了，想象力转上了另一条轨道。这么说，那场追捕还没有结束！那家伙又从藏身处窜了出来，被逼向了塔那边。他在心目中把那个被追的人描画成长腿、精瘦、敏捷，无非是因为他盼望他能逃脱。他不知道那个眼尖的士兵会不会给他一枪。然而远处的呼喊过后继之而起的寂静里，并没有枪声打破它。也许逃脱了？至少暂时是逃脱了。他很可能捅了什么人一刀，而且……天哪！他来了！

===Original===
Cosmo had caught the faint sound of running feet on the hard ground. And even before he had decided that it was no illusion it stopped short and a bulky object fell hurtling from the sky so near to him that Cosmo instinctively drew in his legs with a general start of his body which caused him to knock his hat off against the stone. He became aware of a man’s back almost within reach of his arm. There could be no doubt he had taken a leap over the stone and had landed squatting on his heels. Cosmo expected him to rebound and vanish, but he only extended his arm to seize the hat as it rolled past him and at the same moment pivoted on his toes, preserving his squatting posture.

“If he happens to have a knife in his hand he will plunge it into me,” thought Cosmo. So without moving a limb he hastened to say in a loud whisper: “Run to the tower. Your friends are waiting for you.” It was a sudden inspiration. The man without rising flung himself forward full length and, propped on his arms, brought his face close to Cosmo. His white eyeballs seemed to be starting out of his head. In this position the silence between them lasted for several seconds.

“My friends, but who are you?” muttered the man.

And then the recognition came, instantaneous and mutual. Cosmo simply said, “Hallo!” while the man, letting himself fall to the ground, uttered in a voice faint with emotion, “My Englishman!”

“There were two of them,” said Cosmo.

“Two? Did they see you?”

Cosmo assured him that they had not. The other, still agitated by the unexpectedness of that meeting, asked, incredulous and even a little suspicious: “What am I to think, then? How could you know that they were my friends?”

Cosmo disregarded the question. “You will be caught if you linger here,” he whispered.

The other, as though he had not heard the warning, insisted: “How could they have mentioned my name to you?”

“They mentioned no names.﻿ ﻿… Run.”

“I don’t think they are there now,” said the fugitive.

“Yes. There was noise enough to scare anybody away,” commented Cosmo. “What have you done?”

The other made no answer, and in the pause both men listened intently. The night remained dark. Cosmo thought: “Some smuggling affair,” and the other muttered to himself, “I have misled them.” He sat up by the side of Cosmo and put Cosmo’s hat, which he had been apparently holding all the time, on the ground between them.

“You are a cool hand,” said Cosmo. “The soldiers﻿ ﻿…”

“Who cares for the soldiers? They can’t run.”

“They have muskets, though.”

“Oh, yes. I heard the shots and wondered at whom they were fired.”

“At me. That’s why I have got in here. There is one of them who can see in the dark,” explained Cosmo, who had been very much impressed. His friend of the tower emitted a little chuckle.

“And so you have hidden yourself in here. Soldiers, water, and fire soon make room for themselves. But they did not know who they were after. They got the alarm from that beast.”

He paused suddenly, and Cosmo asked: “Who was after you?”

“One traitor and God knows how many *sbirri*. If they had been only ten minutes later they would have never set eyes on me.”

“I wonder they didn’t manage to cut you off, if they were so many,” said Cosmo.

“They didn’t know. Look! Even now I have deceived them by doubling back. You see I was in a house.” He seemed to hesitate.

“Oh, yes,” said Cosmo. “Saying goodbye.”

The man by his side made a slight movement and preserved a profound silence for a time.

“As I have no demon,” he began slowly, “to keep me informed about other people’s affairs, I must ask you what you were doing here?”

“Why, taking the air like that other evening. But why don’t you try to get away while there is time?”

“Yes, but where?”

“You were going to leave Genoa,” said Cosmo. “Either on a very long or a very deadly journey.”

Again the man by his side made a movement of surprise and remained silent for a while. This was very extraordinary, as though some devil having his own means to obtain knowledge had taken on himself for a disguise the body of an Englishman of the kind that travels and stays in inns. The acquaintance of Cosmo’s almost first hour in Genoa was very much puzzled and a little suspicious, not as before something dangerous but as before something inexplicable, obscure to his mind like the instruments that fate makes use of sometimes in the affairs of men.

===Chinese===
科斯莫听见了坚硬地面上那阵隐约的奔跑声。还没等他断定那不是错觉，那声音便戛然而止，紧接着一个笨重的东西从天而降，落得离他那么近，科斯莫本能地一缩腿，身子猛地一激灵，竟把帽子蹭着石头撞掉了。他觉察到一只几乎伸手可及的男人后背。毫无疑问，那人是跃过石头，落地时蹲在了脚后跟上。科斯莫以为他会反弹起来、一闪而没，可他只是伸出手，抓住了从他身边滚过的帽子，与此同时以脚尖为轴，保持着蹲姿旋转过身来。

「要是他手里正巧有刀，他会把它捅进我身子里来的，」科斯莫想。于是他一动也不敢动，只赶忙用一声响亮的耳语说：「往塔那边跑。你的朋友们在等你。」这是一时灵机一动。那人没有起身，却整个人朝前扑倒，双臂撑地，把脸凑近了科斯莫。他那白眼珠仿佛要从脑袋里迸出来似的。两个人就这样沉默了好几秒。

「我的朋友们，可你是谁？」那人嘟哝道。

紧接着，认出来了，是瞬间的、彼此心照不宣的认出来。科斯莫只说了一声：「嘿！」而那人则让自己瘫倒在地上，用一个激动得几乎发不出声音的嗓音说：「我的英国人！」

「他们一共有两个，」科斯莫说。

「两个？他们看见你了吗？」

科斯莫向他保证他们没有。对方仍因这番不期而遇而激动不已，半信半疑、甚至略带疑心地问：「那我该作何想呢？你怎么知道他们是我的朋友？」

科斯莫没有理会这个问题。「你再磨蹭下去就要被抓住了，」他低声道。

对方仿佛没听见这句警告，依旧追问：「他们怎么会向你提起我的名字？」

「他们没有提起什么名字。……快跑。」

「我想他们这会儿不在那儿了，」那逃亡者说。

「是的。那点动静足够把任何人吓跑了，」科斯莫评说道，「你干了什么？」

对方没有回答，在这一停顿里两人都侧耳细听。夜依旧漆黑。科斯莫想：「又是哪桩走私勾当，」而对方则喃喃自语：「我把他们引开了。」他在科斯莫身边坐直了身子，把科斯莫的帽子——看样子他一直攥着它——放在了两人之间的地上。

「你倒沉得住气，」科斯莫说，「那些士兵……」

「谁在乎那些士兵？他们跑不动。」

「可他们有毛瑟枪。」

「哦，是啊。我听见枪声，正纳闷他们是冲谁放的。」

「冲我。所以我才躲到这儿来。他们里头有一个能在黑暗里看见东西，」科斯莫解释道，他对此印象极深。他这位塔上的朋友发出一声轻笑。

「所以你就藏到这儿来了。士兵、水、火，这三样很快就能给自己腾出地方来。可他们不知道自己追的是谁。他们是听了那畜生的报警。」

他猛地顿住，科斯莫问：「追你的是谁？」

「一个叛徒，还有天知道多少个巡警（*sbirri*）。要是他们晚来十分钟，就压根儿见不着我的影儿了。」

「他们既然那么多，竟没能把你截住，我倒奇怪了，」科斯莫说。

「他们不知道。你瞧！就是这会儿，我还靠杀回马枪骗过了他们。你瞧，我本来是在一户人家里。」他似乎有些迟疑。

「哦，是啊，」科斯莫说，「在道别。」

他身边那人稍稍动了一下，有好一会儿保持着深沉的缄默。

「既然我没有一个魔鬼，」他缓缓地开口道，「替我随时通报别人的闲事，那我只好问你，你在这儿做什么？」

「怎么，跟那天傍晚一样，出来透透气。可你为什么趁着还有工夫不想法子脱身呢？」

「是啊，可往哪儿去？」

「你原是要离开热那亚的，」科斯莫说，「要么是一趟极漫长的旅程，要么是一趟极要命的旅程。」

他身边那人又是一惊，沉默了片刻。这真是万分离奇，仿佛是某个魔鬼，自有它获取消息的门道，借了一具英国人的肉身作伪装——而且是那种四处游历、住旅店的英国人。科斯莫到热那亚几乎头一个钟头便结识的这位相识，大惑不解，也有些疑心，这一次疑的并非像从前那样是某种危险，而是像从前那样是某种无法解释的东西，在他心目中晦暗不明，一如命运有时在人间事务中所借助的那些器具。

===Original===
“So you did see two men a little while ago waiting for me?”

“I did not see them. They seemed to think you were late,” was the surprising answer.

“And how do you know they were waiting for me?”

“I didn’t,” said Cosmo naturally. And the other muttered a remark that he was glad to hear of something that Cosmo did not know. But Cosmo continued: “Of course I didn’t, not till you jumped in here.”

The other made a gesture requesting silence and lent his ear to the unbroken stillness of the surroundings.

“Signore,” he said suddenly in a very quiet and distinct whisper, “it may be true that I was about to leave this town, but I never thought of leaving it by swimming. No doubt the noise was enough to frighten anybody away, but it has been quiet enough now for a long time and I think that I will crawl on as far as the tower to see whether perchance they didn’t think it worth while to bring their boat back to the foot of the tower. I have put my enemies off the track and I fancy they are looking for me in very distant places from here. The treachery, signore, was not in the telling them where I was. Anybody with eyes could have seen me walking about Genoa. No, it was in the telling them who I was.”

He paused again to listen and suddenly changed his position, drawing in his legs.

“Well,” said Cosmo, “I myself wonder who you are.” He noticed the other’s eyes rolling, and the whisper came out of his lips much faster and, as it were, more confidential.

“Attilio, at your service,” the mocking whisper fell into Cosmo’s ear. “I see the signore is not so much of a wizard as I thought.” Then with great rapidity: “Should the signore find something, one never knows, Cantelucci would be the man to give it to.”

And suddenly with a half turn he ran off on all fours, looking for an instant monstrous and vanishing so suddenly that Cosmo remained confounded. He was trying to think what all this might mean, when his ears were invaded by the sound of many footsteps and before he could make a move to get up he found himself surrounded by quite a number of men. As a matter of fact there were only four; but they stood close over him as he sat on the ground, their dark figures blotting all view, with an overpowering effect. Very prudently Cosmo did not attempt to rise; he only picked up his hat, and as he did so it seemed to him that there was something strange about the feel of it. When he put it on his head some object neither very hard nor very heavy fell on the top of his head. He repressed the impulse to have a look at once. “What on earth can it be?” he thought. It felt like a parcel of papers. It was certainly flat. An awestruck voice said, “That’s a foreigner.” Another muttered, “What’s this deviltry?” As Cosmo made an attempt to rise with what dignity he might, the nearest of the band stooped with alacrity and caught hold of his arm above the elbow as if to help him up, with a muttered, “*Permesso, signore.*” And as soon as he regained his feet his other arm was seized from behind by someone else without any ceremony. A slight attempt to shake himself free convinced Cosmo that they meant to stick on.

“Would it be an accomplice?” wondered a voice.

“No. Look at his hat. That’s an Englishman.”

“So much the worse. They are very troublesome. Authority is nothing to them.”

All this time one or another would take a turn to peer closely into Cosmo’s face, in a way which struck him as offensive. Cosmo had not the slightest doubt that he was in the hands of the municipal *sbirri*. That strange Attilio had detected their approach from afar. “He might have given me a warning,” he thought. His annoyance with the fugitive did not last long; but he began to be angry with his captors, of which everyone, he noticed, carried a cudgel.

===Chinese===
「那么，你方才确是看见了两个人在等我？」

「我没看见他们。他们倒像是嫌你迟到了，」这是一个出人意料的回答。

「可你怎么知道他们是在等我？」

「我并不知道，」科斯莫坦然地说。对方嘟哝了一句，说他很高兴总算听见了一桩科斯莫不知道的事。可科斯莫接着说：「我当然不知道，直到你跳进这儿来才知道。」

对方做了个手势请他噤声，侧耳去听四周那一片未受惊扰的寂静。

「先生（signore），」他突如其来地用一种极轻却极清晰的低声说，「我原是要离开此城，这话也许不假，但我可从没想过要泅水离开。那点动静当然是足以把任何人吓跑的，可这会儿已经清静了好一阵子，我想我要朝前爬到塔那边去，看看他们会不会碰巧觉得不值得再把他们的船带到塔脚下来。我已经把我的仇敌引上了歧路，我料想他们这会儿正在离这儿很远很远的地方找我呢。先生，那背叛之罪，并不在于告诉他们我在哪儿。任何长了眼睛的人都瞧得见我在热那亚城里走动。不，那罪过在于告诉他们我是谁。」

他又停下来听了听，忽然变换了姿势，把腿缩了回来。

「好吧，」科斯莫说，「我自己也正纳闷你是谁呢。」他看见对方眼珠骨碌碌地转，那低语从他唇间流出来快了许多，也仿佛更为推心置腹。

「阿蒂利奥（Attilio），听候先生差遣，」那带着嘲弄的低语落进科斯莫的耳朵。「我看先生并不如我想的那样是个巫师。」接着他又极快地说：「万一先生发现了什么东西——这谁说得准呢——要交给人，坎泰洛奇（Cantelucci）便是那个人。」

忽然，他半旋过身子，四肢着地地跑了开去，一瞬间现出一个怪物的模样，又消失得那么突然，留下科斯莫愣在那里。他正琢磨着这一切究竟是什么意思，耳朵里却涌进了一阵杂沓的脚步声，还没等他有所动作站起身来，便发现自己已被好几个汉子团团围住。其实统共只有四个人；可他们紧贴着他，在他席地而坐时俯身笼罩下来，漆黑的人影遮去了一切视野，带着一种压倒一切的气势。科斯莫极为审慎地没有尝试起身；他只是捡起自己的帽子，捡起时却觉得那帽子的手感有些异样。待他把它扣到头上，有一样既不算很硬、也不算很重的东西落到了他的头顶上。他压下了立刻探看一下的冲动。「这到底会是什么呢？」他想。摸着像是一包纸。它确是扁平的。一个满怀敬畏的声音说：「是个外国人。」另一个嘟哝道：「这是什么鬼名堂？」科斯莫尽可能端着架子想起身，离他最近的那一个赶忙弯下腰，一把抓住他肘弯以上的胳膊，像是来搀他起来，嘴里咕哝着：「借光，先生（*Permesso, signore*）。」而他刚一站直，另一只胳膊便被身后的什么人毫不客气地攥住了。他稍稍试着挣了挣，便明白他们是铁了心要钳住他不放了。

「会是个同伙吗？」一个声音疑惑道。

「不是。瞧他的帽子。那是个英国人。」

「那就更糟了。这种人最是麻烦。权威在他们眼里算不得什么。」

这一路，这一个那一个总要轮番凑近了端详科斯莫的脸，那样子让他觉得冒犯。科斯莫毫不怀疑，自己落在了本城那些巡警（*sbirri*）的手里。那个神秘的阿蒂利奥，老远便察觉了他们的来。「他本可以给我一个警报的，」他想。他对那逃亡者的恼火并没持续多久；可他开始对自己的捕获者生出怒气来，他注意到，他们每个人手里都提着一根棍棒。

===Original===
“What authority have you to interfere with me?” he asked haughtily. The wretch who was holding his right arm murmured judicially: “An Inglese, without a doubt.” A stout man in a wide-brimmed hat, who was standing in front of him, grunted: “The authority of four against one,” then addressed his companions to the general effect that he didn’t know what the world was coming to if foreigners were allowed to mix themselves up with conspirators. It looked as if they had been at a loss what to do with their captive. One of them insinuated: “I don’t know. Those foreigners have plenty of money and are impatient of restraint. A poor man may get a chance.”

Cosmo thought that probably each of them was provided with a stiletto. Nothing prevented them from stabbing him in several places, weighting his body with some stones from the seashore, and throwing it into the water. What an unlucky reputation to have! He remembered that he had no money with him. The few coins he used to carry in his pocket were lying on his mantelpiece in the bedroom at the inn. This would have made no difference if those men had been bandits, since they would not be aware of the emptiness of his pockets. “I could have probably bribed them to let me go,” he thought, after he had heard the same man add with a little laugh, “I mean obliging poor men. Those English signori are rich and harmless.”

Cosmo regretted more than ever not being able to make them an offer. It would have been probably successful, as they seemed to be in doubt what to do next. He mentioned he was living at the Casa Graziani. “If one of you will go with me there you shall be recompensed for your trouble.” No answer was made to that proposal except that one of the men coughed slightly. Their chief in a hat with an enormous brim seemed lost in deep thought, and his immobility in front of Cosmo appeared to the latter amusingly mysterious and sinister. A sort of nervous impatience came over Cosmo, an absurd longing to tear himself away and make a dash for liberty, and then an absurd discouragement as though he were a criminal with no hiding-place to make for. The man in the big hat jerked up his head suddenly and disclosed the irritable state of his feelings at the failure of getting hold of that furfante. “As to that Englishman,” he continued in his rasping voice not corresponding to his physical bulk, “let him be taken to the guardroom. He will have to show his papers.”

Cosmo was provoked to say: “Do you expect a gentleman to carry his papers with him when he goes out for a walk?”

He was disconcerted by an outburst of laughter on three sides of him. The leader in the hat did not laugh; he only said bitterly; “We expect papers from a man we find hiding.”

“Well, I have no papers on me,” said Cosmo, and immediately in a sort of mental illumination thought, “Except in my hat.” Of course that object reposing on the top of his head was a bundle of papers, dangerous documents. Attilio was a conspirator. Obviously! The mysterious allusion to something he was to find and hand over to Cantelucci became clear to Cosmo. He felt very indignant with his mysterious acquaintance. “Of course he couldn’t foresee I was going to get into this predicament,” he thought, as if trying to find an excuse for him already.

“Avanti,” commanded the man in front of him.

The grip on his arm of the two others tightened, resistance was no use though he felt sorely tempted again to engage in a struggle. If only he could free himself for a moment, dash off into the darkness, and throw that absurd packet away somewhere before they caught him again. It was a sort of solution; but he discovered in himself an unsuspected and unreasoning loyalty. “No! Somebody would find it and take it to the police,” he thought. “If we come near the quay I may manage to fling it on the water.”

He said with lofty negligence: “You needn’t hold my arms.”

This suggestion was met by a profound silence. Neither of the men holding him relaxed his grasp. Another was treading close on his heels, while the police-hound in the big hat marched a couple of paces in front of him, importantly.

===Chinese===
「你们凭什么来干涉我？」他傲然地问。攥住他右臂的那个家伙煞有介事地低声道：「一个英国人（Inglese），错不了。」一个戴着宽檐帽、站在他面前的壮汉咕哝道：「凭四对一的权威，」接着又对同伴们发表了一通大意如此的话：要是任由外国人和密谋者搅在一处，这世道还成什么话，他真不知道了。看样子他们一时也不知道拿这俘虏怎么办才好。其中一个含蓄地搭腔：「我说不准。那些外国人腰包里钱多，又受不了约束。穷人也许能撞上一个机会。」

科斯莫心想，他们大概每人都随身揣着一把匕首。他们大可在他身上捅出几个窟窿，再用岸边的几块石头坠住尸体，扔进水里。这是何等倒霉的一身名声啊！他想起来自己身上没带钱。他平日揣在口袋里的那几枚硬币，正躺在旅店卧室的壁炉台上。倘若那些人是强盗，这倒也并无分别，因为他们无从知道他的口袋是空的。「我本可以花钱买通他们放我走的，」他心想，这是在他听见同一个人又笑着补了一句之后——「我是说，成全一下穷汉子。那些英国老爷有钱，又不会还手。」

科斯莫比任何时候都更懊恼自己不能向他们开个价。这本是大有希望成功的，因为他们似乎正拿不定主意下一步怎么办。他说自己住在格拉齐亚尼宅（Casa Graziani）。「你们哪一个跟我去那儿，你们这番麻烦自然会有酬报。」这个提议没有得到任何回答，只有其中一人轻轻咳了一声。他们那位戴着大得惊人的帽檐的首领，似乎陷入了深思，他在科斯莫面前那纹丝不动的样子，在后者看来既可疑又神秘，有些好笑。一种神经质的焦躁攫住了科斯莫，一种荒唐的、想挣脱开去、冲向自由的渴望，紧接着又是一种荒唐的灰心丧气，仿佛他是一个无处藏身的罪犯。那戴大帽子的人忽然仰起头来，流露出没能抓住那个无赖（furfante）的恼怒心情。「至于这个英国人，」他那刺耳的、与体格颇不相称的嗓音接着说，「把他带到警卫室去。他得拿出证件来。」

科斯莫被激得回了一句：「难道你们指望一位绅士出门散个步也把证件带在身上吗？」

他身周三面迸发出的一阵大笑弄得他不知所措。那戴帽子的头领没有笑；只冷冷地说：「我们是从一个被发现躲藏着的人身上索取证件的。」

「好吧，我身上没带证件，」科斯莫说，紧接着在一种灵光一闪般的心思里想道：「除了我帽子里。」当然，那个此刻搁在他头顶的东西，便是一包文件，危险的文件。阿蒂利奥是个密谋者。这是明摆着的！那句神秘的、关于某样他要去寻得并交给坎泰洛奇的东西的暗示，在科斯莫心里豁然贯通了。他对这位神秘的相识大为光火。「当然，他也不可能未卜先知我会陷入这般窘境，」他想，仿佛已经在替他开脱了。

「向前走（Avanti），」前面那人喝道。

另两个人攥他胳膊的手攥得更紧了，抵抗已是徒劳，尽管他又一次按捺不住想动手挣扎。只要他能脱身一瞬，冲进黑暗，趁他们再次抓住他之前把这荒唐的一包东西丢掉就好。这倒也是一种解决办法；可他在自己身上发现了一种出乎意料、不讲道理的忠心。「不行！总有人会捡到它，把它送到警察那里去，」他想，「等我们走到码头附近，我可以设法把它扔进水里。」

他带着居高临下的漫不经心说道：「你们不必攥着我的胳膊。」

这一提议换来的是一片深沉的沉默。抓着他的那两个人谁也没松手。另一个人紧跟在他脚后，而那只戴大帽子的警犬则神气十足地在他前面两步开外走着。

===Original===
Before long they approached the guardhouse close enough for Cosmo to see the sentry at the foot of the steps, who challenged them militarily. The sbirro in the hat advanced alone and made himself known in the light streaming through the door. It was too late to attempt anything. As he was impelled by his two captors inside the guardroom, which was lighted by a smoky lamp and also full of tobacco smoke, Cosmo thought, “I am in for it. What a horrible nuisance! I wonder whether they will search me?”

At Cosmo’s entrance with his escort several soldiers reclining on the floor raised their heads. It was a small place which may have been used as a store for sails or cordage. The furniture consisted of one long bench, a rack of muskets, a table, and one chair. A sergeant sitting on that chair rose and talked with the head sbirro for a time in a familiar and interested manner about the incidents of the chase, before he even looked at Cosmo. Cosmo could not hear the words. The sergeant was a fine man with long black moustaches and a great scar on his cheek. He nodded from time to time in an understanding manner to the man in the hat, whom the light of the guardroom disclosed as the possessor of very small eyes, a short thick beard, and a pear-shaped yellow physiognomy which had a pained expression. At the suggestion of the sbirri (they had let him go) Cosmo sat down on a bench running along the wall. Part of it was occupied by a soldier stretched at full length with his head on his knapsack and with his shako hung above him on the wall. He was profoundly asleep. “Perhaps that’s the fellow who took those shots at me,” thought Cosmo. Another of the sbirri approached Cosmo and with a propitiatory smile handed him his cloak. Cosmo had forgotten all about it.

“I carried it behind the signore all the way,” he murmured with an air of secrecy; and Cosmo was moved to say: “You ought to have brought it to me at Cantelucci’s inn,” in a significant tone. The man made a deprecatory gesture and said in a low voice: “The signore may want it tonight.”

He was young. His eyes met Cosmo’s without flinching.

“I see,” whispered Cosmo. “What is going to be done with me?” The man looked away indifferently and said: “I am new at this work; but there is a post of royal gendarmerie on the other side of the harbour.”

He threw himself on the bench by Cosmo’s side, stretched his legs out, folded his arms across his breast, and yawned unconcernedly.

“Can I trust him?” Cosmo asked himself. Nobody seemed to pay any attention to him. The sbirro in the hat bustled out of the guardroom in great haste; the other two remained on guard; the sergeant sitting astride the chair folded his arms on the back of it and stared at the night through the open door. The sbirro by Cosmo’s side muttered, looking up at the ceiling: “I think Barbone is gone to find a boatman.” From this Cosmo understood that he was going to be taken across the harbour and given up to the gendarmes. He thought, “If they insist upon searching me I would have to submit and in any case a hat is not a hiding-place. I may just as well hand the packet over without a struggle.” A bright idea struck him. “If those fellows take me over there in a boat to save themselves the trouble of walking round the harbour I will simply contrive to drop my hat overboard﻿—even if they do hold my arms during the passage.” He was now convinced that Attilio belonged to some secret society. He certainly was no common fellow. He wondered what had happened to him. Was he slinking and dodging about the low parts of the town on his way to some refuge; or had he really found the excitable man and the grumpy man still waiting under the tower with a boat? Most unlikely after such an alarming commotion of yells and shots. He feared that Attilio, unable to get away, could hardly avoid being caught tomorrow, or at the furthest next day. He himself obviously did not expect anything better; or else he would not have been so anxious to get rid of those papers. Cosmo concluded that conspirators were perfectly absurd with their passion for documents, which were invariably found at a critical time and sent them all to the gallows.

He noticed the eyes of the sergeant, a Croat, with pendent black moustaches, fixed on his hat, and at once felt uneasy as if he had belonged to a secret society himself. His hat was the latest thing in men’s round hats which he had bought in Paris. But, almost directly, the sergeant’s eyes wandered off to the doorway and resumed their stare. Cosmo was relieved. He decided, however, to attempt no communication with the young police fellow whose lounging attitude, abandoned and drowsy, and almost touching elbows with him, seemed to Cosmo too suggestive to be trustworthy. And indeed, he reflected, what could he do for him?

His excitement about this adventure was combined in a strange way with a state of inward peace which he had not known for hours. He wondered at his loyalty to the astute Attilio. He would have been justified in regarding the transaction as a scurvy trick; whereas he found that he could not help contemplating it as a matter of trust. He went on exercising his wits upon the problem of those documents (he was sure those were papers of some kind) which he had been asked to give to Cantelucci (how surprised he would be), since apparently the innkeeper was a conspirator too. Yet, he thought, it would be better to destroy them than to let them fall into the hands of the Piedmontese justice, or the Austrian military command. “I must contrive,” he thought, “to get rid of them in the boat. I can always shake my hat overboard accidentally.” But the packet would float and some boatman would be sure to find it during the day. On the other hand, by the time daylight came the handwriting would probably have become illegible. Or perhaps not? Fire, not water, was what he needed. If there had been a fire in that inexpressibly dirty guardroom he would have made use of it at once under the very noses of those wild-looking Croats. But would that have been the proper thing to do in such a hurry?

===Chinese===
不久他们便走得离警卫室足够近，科斯莫看得见台阶脚下的那个哨兵，正以军人的口吻向他们喝问口令。那戴帽子的巡警独自走上前去，在从门里流泻出来的灯光里亮明了身份。现在再想干什么都已太迟。当两个押解者把他推进警卫室——里面点着一盏冒烟的灯，也满是烟草的烟味——科斯莫心想：「这下完了。真是讨厌透顶！他们会不会搜我的身？」

科斯莫带着押解的人一进去，几个躺在地上的士兵便抬起了头。这是个小地方，从前也许是用作存放船帆或缆绳的库房。屋里的陈设只有一条长凳、一个枪架、一张桌子和一把椅子。一个坐在那把椅子上的军士站起身来，与那位巡警头目用一种熟稔而饶有兴致的口吻谈了一阵追捕的经过，才把目光转向科斯莫。科斯莫听不见他们说些什么。那军士是个仪表堂堂的汉子，留着两撇长长的黑八字胡，腮帮子上有一大道疤。他不时心领神会地点着头，对那戴帽子的人——警卫室的灯光显现出他生着一双极小的眼睛、一把又短又厚的胡子和一张梨形的、带着痛苦之色的黄脸。在巡警们的示意下（他们已经松开了他），科斯莫在沿墙的一条长凳上坐了下来。长凳的一部分被一个士兵占着，他直挺挺地躺在那儿，头枕着背囊，筒状军帽挂在他头顶的墙上。他睡得正沉。「也许向我开枪的就是这家伙，」科斯莫想。另一个巡警走到科斯莫跟前，带着讨好的微笑把他的斗篷递给他。科斯莫早把斗篷忘得一干二净了。

「我一路跟在先生后头，替先生拿着它，」他故作神秘地低声道；科斯莫动了动，意味深长地说：「你本该把它送到坎泰洛奇的旅店给我，」那人做了一个歉然的手势，压低声音道：「先生今夜说不定还用得着它呢。」

他很年轻。他的眼睛迎着科斯莫的目光，毫不躲闪。

「我明白了，」科斯莫低声道。「他们打算把我怎么样？」那人漠然地把目光移开，说：「我干这一行还是新手；不过港口那头有一处皇家宪兵的哨所。」

他倒在科斯莫身旁的长凳上，伸直双腿，双臂抱在胸前，毫不在意地打了个哈欠。

「我能信他吗？」科斯莫暗自思忖。似乎谁也不把他放在心上。那戴帽子的巡警匆匆忙忙地奔出警卫室去了；另两个留下来看守；那个跨坐在椅子上的军士把双臂叠在椅背上，隔着敞开的门凝望着夜色。科斯莫身旁的巡警抬头望着天花板嘟哝道：「我想巴博内（Barbone）是去找船夫了。」科斯莫由此明白，自己将被带过港去，移交给宪兵。他想：「他们要是硬要搜我的身，我也只好由他们搜，再说一顶帽子也算不得什么藏匿之处。我倒不如把这包东西交出去，免得一番扭打。」他忽然想出一个妙主意。「要是那几个家伙为了省得绕港走、用船把我送过那边去，我就设法把帽子丢到船外去——哪怕渡船时他们攥着我的胳膊。」这会儿他已确信阿蒂利奥属于某个秘密会社。他当然不是个寻常之辈。他纳罕他后来怎样了。他是在城里下九流的地段里东躲西藏地奔向某个藏身之所呢；还是当真在塔底下找到了那只船，找到了那个仍在那儿等他的、易惊多话的人和那个阴沉的人？经过了那样一场又是呼喊又是开枪的骇人骚动，这多半不大可能。他担心阿蒂利奥既然走不脱，明天、至迟后天就难免落网。阿蒂利奥本人显然也不指望更好的结局；否则他不会那样急于把这包文件打发掉。科斯莫得出结论：密谋者们对文件的那种癖好实在荒唐，那些文件总在要紧关头被搜出来，把他们一齐送上绞架。

他注意到那个军士——一个克罗地亚人，留着下垂的黑八字胡——的目光定在他的帽子上，顿时心里不安起来，仿佛自己也属于某个秘密会社。他那顶帽子是男式圆顶帽里最新式的式样，是他人在巴黎（Paris）买的。可那军士的目光几乎立刻就转到了门口，又恢复了那种凝望。科斯莫松了口气。然而他决定不去试图与那个年轻的警察搭话——那人懒洋洋的、放任而瞌睡的姿态，胳膊肘几乎挨着他的胳膊肘，在科斯莫看来太暧昧了，靠不住。况且，他暗想，他能替自己做什么呢？

他对这桩冒险的兴奋，奇怪地与一种几小时以来未曾有过的内心宁静交织在一起。他对自己对那个精明的阿蒂利奥的忠心感到诧异。他本可名正言顺地把这桩事视作一个卑鄙的把戏；可他发现自己不由自主地把它看作一桩信托。他继续绞尽脑汁盘算着那几份文件——他断定那是某种文件——的难题，这几份文件他受命交给坎泰洛奇（他该多么吃惊啊），因为这位客栈老板显然也是个密谋者。然而他又想，与其让它们落到皮埃蒙特（Piedmontese）的司法当局或奥地利军方手里，还不如把它们毁掉。「我得想法子，」他寻思，「在船上把它们处理掉。我总能装作失手，把帽子抖到船外去。」可那包东西会浮在水上，白天准会有哪个船夫捞着它。另一方面，等天亮时，上面的字迹也许已漫漶不清。也许还不至于？他所需要的是火，不是水。要是那间脏得难以形容的警卫室里生着一盆火，他一定当着那几个相貌凶悍的克罗地亚人的面，立刻就把它用上。可这么匆匆忙忙的，这么做妥当吗？

===Original===
He had not come to any conclusion before Barbone returned, accompanied by a silver-haired, meek old fellow, with a nut-brown face, barefooted and bare-armed, and carrying a pair of sculls over his shoulder, whom Barbone pushed in front of the sergeant. The latter took his short pipe out of his mouth, spat on one side, looked at the old man with a fixed savage stare, and finally nodded. At Cosmo he did not look at all, but to Barbone he handed a key with the words, “Bring it back.” The sbirri closed round Cosmo and Barbone uttered a growl with a gesture towards the door. Why Barbone should require a key to take him out of doors Cosmo could not understand. Unless it were the key of liberty. But it was not likely that the fierce Croat and the gloomy Barbone should have indulged in symbolic actions. The mariner with the sculls on his shoulder followed the group patiently to where, on the very edge of the quay, the Austrian soldier with his musket shouldered paced to and fro across the streak of reddish light from the garrison door. He swung round and stood, very martial, in front of the group, but at the sight of the key exhibited to him by Barbone moved out of the way. The air was calm but chilly. Below the level of the quay there was the clinking of metal and the rattling of small chains, and Cosmo then discovered that the key belonged to a padlock securing the chain to which quite a lot of small rowing boats were moored. The young policeman said from behind into Cosmo’s ear, “The signore is always forgetting his cloak,” and threw it lightly on Cosmo’s shoulders. He explained also that every night all the small boats in the port were collected and secured like this on both sides of the port and the Austrians furnished the sentry to look after them on this side. The object was that there should be no boats moving after ten o’clock, except the galley of the dogana and of course the boat of the English man-of-war.

“Come and see me at noon at Cantelucci’s inn,” whispered Cosmo, to which the other breathed out a “Certainly, Excellency,” feelingly before going up the steps.

Cosmo found himself presently sitting in a boat between two sbirri. The ancient fellow shoved off and shipped his oars. From the quay, high above, Barbone’s voice shouted to him, “The gendarmes will take charge of your boat for the rest of the night.” The old boatman’s only answer was a deep sigh, and in a very few strokes the quay with the sentry receded into the darkness. One of the sbirri remarked in a tone of satisfaction, “Our service will be over after we have given up the signore there.” The other said, “I hope the signore will consider we have been kept late on his account.” Cosmo, who was contemplating with immense distaste the prospect of being delivered up to the gendarmes, emitted a mirthless laugh, and after a while said in a cold tone: “Why waste your time in pulling to the other side of the harbour? Put me on board the nearest vessel. I’ll soon find my way to the quay from one tartane to another, and your service would be over at once.”

The fellow on his left assumed an astonishing seriousness: “Most of those tartanes have a dog on board. We could not expose an illustrious stranger to get bitten by one of these ugly brutes.”

But the other had no mind for grave mockery. In a harsh and overbearing tone he ordered the boatman to pull well into the middle of the harbour away from the moored craft.

===Chinese===
他还没拿定主意，巴博内便回来了，身后跟着一个银发、面色温顺的老人，脸膛是坚果般的褐色，赤着脚，赤着胳膊，肩上扛着一对桨；巴博内把他推到军士跟前。军士从嘴里取下短烟斗，朝一旁啐了一口，用一种死死盯人的凶狠目光打量了老人一番，终于点了点头。对科斯莫，他看也不看一眼，只把一把钥匙递给巴博内，说了一句：「送回来。」巡警们把科斯莫围了起来，巴博内朝门外一摆头，喉间发出一声低吼。巴博内要一把钥匙做什么、把他带到门外去，科斯莫实在想不通。除非那是自由的钥匙。不过，那凶悍的克罗地亚人和阴沉的巴博内，不大像会搞什么象征性的举动。那个肩扛双桨的水手耐心地跟在众人后头，一直走到码头极靠边的所在——那儿有个奥地利士兵扛着毛瑟枪，在驻军门里透出的那道淡红色光带上来回踱着。他一个转身，威风凛凛地挡在众人面前，可一见巴博内亮出的那把钥匙，便让开了道。空气沉静，却冷。在码头平面以下，传来金属的叮当声和小链子的哗啷声，科斯莫这才明白那把钥匙是开一把挂锁用的，那挂锁锁着一根链子，链子上拴着好些只小划艇。年轻的警察从身后凑到科斯莫耳边说：「先生老是忘带斗篷，」便轻轻把它抛到科斯莫肩上。他还解释说，每天夜里港里所有的小船都这样被收拢、锁在港的两侧，奥地利人则派哨兵在这一侧看管它们。为的是十点之后不许再有船只走动，只有海关（dogana）的桨帆船，当然还有英国军舰的小艇，是例外。

「正午到坎泰洛奇的旅店来见我，」科斯莫低声道；对方动情地吐出一句「遵命，大人（Excellency）」，便拾级而上了。

不一会儿，科斯莫发觉自己坐在两个巡警之间的船上。那上了年纪的汉子把船一撑，装上桨。从高高的码头上传来巴博内对他的一声吆喝：「今夜剩下的时辰，你的船就交给宪兵看管了。」老船夫唯一的回答是一声深长的叹息，没划几下，码头连同那个哨兵便退进了黑暗里。一个巡警满意地搭腔：「把先生在那儿一交，我们这趟差事就算完了。」另一个说：「希望先生念着我们是为了他的缘故才熬到这么晚。」科斯莫正满心厌恶地想着被移交给宪兵的前景，发出一声干笑，过了一会儿冷冷地说：「何苦浪费工夫划到港的那一头去？把我送上最近的一条船就成了。我顺着一只只小帆船（tartane）跳过去，很快就能回到码头上，你们的差事也就立刻完了。」

他左边那个家伙装出一副煞有介事的严肃样子：「那些小帆船大多船上都养着狗。我们可不能让一位显贵的外乡人被这些丑陋畜生咬了。」

可另一个人无心玩这种一本正经的嘲弄。他用刺耳而专横的口气，命令船夫把船划到港湾正中央去，离那些停泊的船只远远的。

===Original===
It was like crossing a lake overshadowed by the hills with the breakwaters prolonging the shore to seaward. The old man raised and dipped his oars slowly, without a sound, and the long trails of starlight trembled on the ripples on each side of the boat. When they had progressed far enough to open the harbour entrance Cosmo detected between the end of the jetties far away﻿—he was glancing casually about﻿—a dark speck about the size of a man’s head, which ought not to have been there. The air was perfectly still and the stars thick on the horizon. It struck him at once that it could be nothing than either the English man-of-war’s boat or the boat of the dogana, since no others were allowed to move at night. His thoughts were, however, so busy with speculating as to what he had better do that he paid no more attention to that remarkable speck. He looked absently at the silver-haired boatman pulling an easy stroke and asked himself: Was it or was it not time to lose his hat overboard? How could he contrive to make it look plausible in this absurd calm? Then he reproached himself for reasoning as if those two low fellows (whose proximity had grown extremely irksome to him) had wits of preternatural sharpness. If he were to snatch it and fling it away they would probably conclude that he was trying to make himself troublesome, or simply mad, or anything in the world rather than guess that he had in his hat something which he wanted to destroy. He undid quietly the clasp of his cloak and rested his hands on his knees. His guardians did not think it necessary now to hold his arms. In fact they did not seem to pay much attention to him. Cosmo asked himself for a moment whether he would stand up suddenly and jump into the water. Of course he knew that fully clothed and in his boots they would very soon get hold of him, but the object would have been attained. However, the prospect of being towed behind a boat to the customhouse quay by the collar of his coat and being led into the presence of the gendarmes looking like a drowned rat was so disagreeable that he rejected that plan.

By that time the boat had reached little more than halfway across the harbour. The great body of the shipping was merged with the shore. The nearest vessels were a polacca brig and xebec lying at anchor. Both were shadowy, and the last, with her low spars, a mere low smudge on the dim sheen of the water. From time to time the aged boatman emitted a moan. The boat seemed hardly to move. Everything afloat was silent and dark. The crews of the coasters were ashore or asleep; and if there were any dogs on board any of them they too seemed plunged in the same slumber that lay over all things of the earth, and by contrast with which the stars of heaven looked intensely wakeful. In the midst of his perplexities Cosmo enjoyed the feeling of peace that had come to him directly his trouble had begun.

“We will be all night getting across,” growled suddenly the man on his left. “… I don’t know what Barbone was thinking of to get this antiquity out of his bed.”

“I told him there was hardly any breath in my old body,” declared the boatman’s tranquil voice.

Apparently in order to speak he had to cease rowing, for he rested on his oars while he went on in the grave-like silence. “But he raged like a devil; and rather than let him wake up all the neighbours I came out. I may just as well die in the boat as in bed.”

Both sbirri exclaimed indignantly against Barbone, but neither offered to take the sculls. With a painful groan the old man began to pull again. Cosmo asked: “What’s that dark thing between the heads of the jetties?” One of his captors, turning his head to look, said, “That must be the galley. I wish she would come this way. We would ask her for a tow.” The other man remarked sarcastically, “No fear, they are all snoozing in her except one perhaps to keep a lookout. It’s an easy life.﻿ ﻿… Voga, vecchio, voga.”

Cosmo thought suddenly that if by any chance the man-of-war boat happened to be pulling that way he would hail her without hesitation, and, surely, the officer in charge would not leave him in the hands of those villains without at least listening to his tale. Unluckily their way across the harbour did not take them near the man-of-war. The light at her mizzen peak seemed to Cosmo very far away; so that if it had not burned against the dark background of the land it would have seemed more distant than any star, and not half as brightly vigilant. He took his eyes from it and let them rest idly on the water ahead. The sbirro on his right hand emitted an immense yawn. This provided the other to mutter curses on the tediousness of all this affair. Cosmo had been too perplexed to feel bored. Just then as if in antagonism to those offensive manifestations he felt very alert. Moreover, the moment when something would have to be done was approaching, a tension of all his senses accumulating in a sort of allover impatience. While in that state, staring into the night, he caught sight of the man-of-war’s boat.

But was it?﻿—well, it was something dark on the water, and as there was no other boat about﻿ ﻿… It was small﻿—well, far off and probably end on.﻿ ﻿… He had heard no sound of rowing﻿ ﻿… lying on her oars﻿ ﻿… He could see nothing now﻿ ﻿… well, here goes, on the chance.

Without stirring a limb he took a long breath and let out the shout of “Boat ahoy” with all the force of his lungs. The volume of tone astonished himself. It seemed to fill the whole of the harbour so effectually that he felt he needn’t shout again and he remained as still as a statue. The effect on his neighbours was that both gave a violent start, which set the boat rolling slightly, and in their bewilderment they bent forward to peer into his face with immense eyes. After a time one of them asked in an awestruck murmur, “What’s the matter, signore?” and seized his cloak. The other, Cosmo heard distinctly whisper to himself, “That was a war cry,” while he also grabbed the cloak. The clasp being undone, it slipped off Cosmo’s shoulders and then they clung to his arms. It struck Cosmo as remarkable that the old boatman, had not ceased his feeble rowing for a moment.

The shout had done Cosmo good. It reestablished his self-respect somehow and it sent the blood moving through his veins as if indeed it had been a war cry. He had shaken their nerves. If they had not remained perfectly motionless holding his arms there would have started a scrimmage in that boat which would certainly have ended in the water. But their grip was feeble. They did nothing, but, bending towards each other in front of Cosmo till their heads almost touched, watched his lips from which such an extraordinary shout had come. Cosmo stared stonily ahead as if unconscious of their existence, and again he had that strange illusion of a dark spot ahead of the boat. He thought, “That’s no illusion. What a fool I was. It must be a mooring buoy.” A couple of minutes elapsed before he thought again, “That old fellow will be right into it, presently.”

===Chinese===
这好比横渡一片被群山遮蔽的湖泊，防波堤把海岸朝海的一面延伸出去。那老人无声地缓缓起桨、落桨，长长的星光在水波上拖出的光带，在船的两舷颤动。他们划出去足够远、把港口入口敞开之后，科斯莫瞥见远处两道防波堤尽头之间——他原本不过随眼四下扫扫——有一个约莫人头大小的黑点，而那里本不该有什么东西。空气纹丝不动，地平线上星斗密布。他立时想到，那只能是英国军舰的小艇，或是海关的小艇，因为夜里再没有别的船可以走动。然而他的心思正忙着盘算自己该怎么办，便没再多留意那个显眼的黑点。他心不在焉地望着那位银发船夫轻松地划着桨，问自己：是时候该把帽子丢到水里去了，还是不到时候？在这么个荒唐的无风无浪里，他怎么才能让这事做得不露痕迹呢？接着他又责备自己：他这样盘算，倒好像那两个下等家伙（紧挨着他，已叫他厌烦到了极点）长着什么超乎寻常的机灵脑瓜子似的。他要是猛地抓起帽子把它扔出去，他们多半只会断定他是在故意找麻烦，要不就是疯了，天底下随他们怎么猜都行，唯独猜不到他帽子里藏着什么他想毁掉的东西。他悄悄地解开了斗篷的搭扣，双手搁在膝上。两个看守这会儿觉得不必再攥着他的胳膊了。事实上他们似乎也不大把他放在心上了。科斯莫有一瞬间问自己，要不要猛地站起身、跳进水里。他当然知道，穿着这一身衣服、蹬着靴子，他们很快就会把他抓住，可那目的也就达到了。只是，被人在身后揪着衣领拖在船后、一路拽到海关码头，再像只落汤鸡似的被带到宪兵面前——这幅前景实在太不雅观，他遂打消了这个主意。

到这时，船已划到港湾的中央略过一点。那一大片的船只已与岸融为一片。最近的几艘是抛了锚的一艘桅帆式双桅船和一艘三桅小帆船。两艘都是朦胧的影子，后一艘桅杆低矮，在水面上幽幽的微光里不过是一抹低低的污痕。那上了年纪的船夫不时发出一声呻吟。船似乎一动也不动。水上的一切都沉静而黑暗。那些沿海小船的船员要么上了岸，要么已睡下；就算哪艘船上养着狗，它们也像是同样沉入了笼罩着世间万物的酣睡之中，相形之下，天上的星斗倒显得格外清醒。在种种困惑之中，科斯莫却享受着一种宁静之感，这宁静是打他这桩麻烦开始之时便降临到他身上的。

「我们这样划，得划上一整夜才过得去，」他左边那人突然发牢骚道。「……我不懂巴博内是怎么想的，把这老古董从床上拖起来。」

「我告诉过他，我这一把老骨头里几乎没有气儿了，」船夫平静的声音宣称道。

显然，他要说话就非得停下桨不可，因为他停桨靠桨，在坟墓般的寂静里接着说：「可他像个魔鬼似的发火；我与其让他把左邻右舍都吵醒，不如自己出来了。我死在船上和死在床上，原也差不多。」

两个巡警都愤愤然骂了巴博内几句，可谁也没提出替他划桨。老人痛苦地呻吟一声，又划了起来。科斯莫问：「防波堤两端之间那个黑乎乎的东西是什么？」一个押解者扭头看了一眼，说：「那一定是海关的桨帆船。我巴望它朝这边来。我们就请它拖一拖。」另一个人讥讽道：「想得美。船上的人除了一个望风的，全在打瞌睡呢。那是份自在差事。……划呀，老头儿，划呀（Voga, vecchio, voga）。」

科斯莫忽然想到，万一那军舰的小艇碰巧正朝这边划，他一定毫不犹豫地招呼它；而且，管艇的长官总不至于不听他一句话就把他扔在这些恶棍手里吧。不幸的是，他们横渡港湾的路并不经过那艘军舰。军舰后桅斜桁端的那盏灯，在科斯莫看来远得很；要不是它映在陆地那一片漆黑的背景上，它准会比任何一颗星都更远，而且连一半那么警醒的亮光都没有。他把目光从那盏灯上移开，让它懒懒地落在前方的水面上。他右手的那个巡警打了一个大大的哈欠。这又引得另一个人低声咒骂这桩差事的乏味。科斯莫一直太困惑，顾不上觉得乏味。就在这时，仿佛是要与那些叫人不痛快的举动针锋相对似的，他觉得自己格外清醒。况且，该有所行动的时刻正在逼近，他全部感官的紧张，正积聚成一种弥漫周身的急不可耐。正这般境地，他凝视着夜色，瞥见了军舰的小艇。

可那是吗？——嗯，水面上是有个黑乎乎的东西，而附近又没有别的船……它很小——嗯，离得远，而且多半是船头朝向这边。……他没听见划桨声……是停桨停着……他这会儿什么也看不见了……好吧，碰碰运气。

他一动不动，深深吸了一口气，运足了肺里全部的力气，喊出一声：「前面的船——喂！」那一声呐喊的浑厚把他自己也惊了一跳。它仿佛把整个港湾都填得满满当当，叫他觉得自己不必再喊第二声，便如一尊雕像般纹丝不动。它给邻座两人的影响是：俩人都猛地一惊，惊得小船微微摇晃起来，他们在惶惑里俯身凑近，瞪大了眼睛端详他的脸。过了一会儿，其中一个满怀敬畏地低声问：「怎么了，先生？」一面抓住了他的斗篷。另一个，科斯莫听得清清楚楚地自言自语：「那是一声战吼，」也一把抓住了斗篷。搭扣本是解开的，斗篷便从科斯莫肩上滑了下去，接着他们又改攥住他的胳膊。令科斯莫称奇的是，那老船夫却一刻也没有停下他那虚弱的划桨。

那一声喊对科斯莫大有好处。它不知怎地重建了他的自尊，让血液在血管里奔涌起来，仿佛那当真是一声战吼。他动摇了他们的神经。要不是他们一动不动地攥着他的胳膊，这船上早就会爆发一场混战，而且必以落水收场。可他们抓得有气无力。他们什么也没做，只是在科斯莫面前彼此俯过身去，直到两人的头几乎要碰到一起，盯着他那发出如此非凡一吼的双唇。科斯莫如石像一般木然凝视前方，仿佛不知有他们存在，于是他又生出那种怪异的错觉，觉得船头前方有个黑点。他想：「那不是错觉。我真蠢。那一定是个系船浮标。」过了两分钟，他才又想：「那老头儿再划下去，就要一头撞上它了。」

===Original===
He didn’t consider it his business to utter a warning because the bump he expected happened almost immediately. He had misjudged the distance. Owing to the slow pace the impact was very slight, slighter even than Cosmo expected against such a heavy body as a mooring buoy would be. It was really more like a feeble hollow sound than a shock. Cosmo, who was prepared for it, was really the one that felt it at once, and the ancient boatman looked sharply over his shoulder. He uttered no sound and did not even attempt to rise from the thwart. He simply, as it seemed to Cosmo, let go the oars. The sbirri only became aware of something having happened after the hollow bump was repeated, and Cosmo had become aware that the object on the water was not a buoy but another boat not much bigger than theirs. Then they both exclaimed and in their surprise their grip relaxed. One of them cried in astonishment, “An empty boat.” It was indeed a surprising occurrence. With no particular purpose in his mind Cosmo stood up while one of the sbirri stood up either to catch hold of the boat or push it away, for the two boats were alongside each other by that time. A strange voice in the dark said very loud: “The man in the hat,” and as if by enchantment three figures appeared standing in a row. Cosmo had not even time to feel surprised. The two boats started knocking about considerably, and he felt himself seized by the collar and one arm and dragged away violently from between the two sbirri by the power of irresistible arms which as suddenly let him go as if he were an inanimate object, and he fell heavily in the bottom of the second boat almost before his legs were altogether clear of the other. During this violent translation his hat fell off his head without any scheming on his part.

He was not exactly frightened but he was excusably flustered. One is not kidnapped like this without any preliminaries every day. He was painfully aware of being in the way of his new captors. He was kicked in the ribs and his legs were trodden upon. He heard blows being struck against hard substances which he knew were human skulls because of the abortive yells ending in groans. There was a determination and ferocity in this attack combined with the least possible amount of noise. All he could hear were the heavy blows and the hard breathing of the assailants. Then came a sort of helpless splash. “Somebody will get drowned,” he thought.

He made haste to pull himself forward from under the feet of the combatants. Luckily for his ribs they were bare, which also added to the quietness of that astonishing development. Once in the bows he sat up, and by that time everything was over. Three shadowy forms were standing in a row in the boat, motionless, like labourers who had accomplished a notable task. The boat out of which he had been dragged was floating within a yard or two, apparently empty. The whole affair, which could not have lasted more than a minute, seemed to Cosmo to have been absolutely instantaneous. Not a sound came from the shipping along the quays, not even from the brig and the xebec which were the nearest. A sense of final stillness such as follows, for instance, the explosion of a mine and resembles the annihilation of all one’s perceptive faculties took possession of Cosmo for a moment. Presently he heard a very earnest but low voice cautioning the silent world: “If you dare make a noise I will come back and kill you.” It was perfectly impersonal; it had no direction, no particular destination. Cosmo, who heard the words distinctly, could connect no image of a human being with them. He was roused at last when, dropping his hand on the gunwale, he felt human fingers under it. He snatched his hand away as if burnt and only then looked over. The white hair of the old boatman seemed to rest on the water right against the boat’s side. He was holding on silently, even in this position displaying the meek patience of his venerable age﻿—and Cosmo contemplated him in silence. A voice, not at all impersonal this time, said from the stem sheets, “Get out your oars.”

“There is a man in the water here,” said Cosmo, wondering at his own voice being heard in those fantastic conditions. It produced, however, the desired effect, and almost as soon as he had spoken Cosmo had to help a bearded sailor, who was a complete stranger to him, to haul the old man inside the boat. He was no great weight to get over the gunwale, but they had to handle him as if he had been drowned. He never attempted to help himself. The other men in the boat took an interest in the proceedings.

“Is he dead?” came a subdued inquiry from aft.

“He is very old and feeble,” explained Cosmo in an undertone. Somebody swore long but softly, ending with the remark: “Here’s a complication.”

“That scoundrel Barbone dragged out a dying man,” began Cosmo impulsively.

“Va bene, va bene﻿ ﻿… Bundle him in and come aft, signore.”

Cosmo, obeying this injunction, found himself sitting in the stem sheets by the side of a man whose first act was to put his hand lightly upon his shoulder in a way that conveyed a sort of gentle exultation. The discovery that the man was Attilio was too startling for comment at the first moment. The next it seemed the most natural thing in the world.

“It seems as if nothing could keep us apart,” said that extraordinary man in a low voice. He took his hand off Cosmo’s shoulder and directed the two rowers﻿—who, Cosmo surmised, were the whisperers of the tower﻿—to pull under the bows of the brig. “We must hide from those customhouse fellows,” he said. “I fancy the galley is coming along.”

No other word was uttered till one of the men got hold of the brig’s cable and the boat came to a rest with her side against the stem of that vessel, when Cosmo, who now could himself hear the faint noise of rowing, asked Attilio in a whisper: “Are they after you?”

“If they are after anything,” answered the other coolly, “they are after a very fine voice. What made you give that shout?”

“I had to behave like a frightened mouse before those sbirri, on account of those papers you left with me, and I felt that I must assert myself.” Cosmo gave this psychological explanation grimly. He changed his tone to add that, fancying he had seen the shape of the English man-of-war’s boat, the temptation to hail her had been irresistible.

“Possibly that’s what started them. They know nothing of us. Luck was on our side. We slipped in unseen.” The sound of rowing meantime had grown loud enough to take away from them all desire for further conversation, for the noise of heavy oars working in their rowlocks has a purposeful relentless character on a still night, and the big twelve-oared galley, pulled with a short quick stroke, seemed to hold an unerring way in its hollow thundering progress. For those in the boat concealed under the bows of the brig the strain of having to listen without being able to see was growing intolerable. Cosmo asked himself anxiously whether he was going to be captured once more before this night of surprises was out, but at the last moment the galley swerved and passed under the stem of the polacca as if bent on taking merely a sweep round the harbour. Everybody in the boat drew a long breath. But almost immediately afterwards the sound of rowing stopped short and everyone in the boat seemed turned again into stone.

At last Attilio breathed into Cosmo’s ear, “Per Dio! They have found the other boat.”

Cosmo was almost ashamed at the swift eagerness of his fearfully whispered inquiry:

“Are the men in her dead?”

“All I know is that if either of them is able to talk we are lost,” Attilio whispered back.

“These sbirri were going to deliver me to the gendarmes,” Cosmo began under his breath, when all at once the noise of the oars burst again on their ears abruptly; but soon all apprehension was at an end, because it became clear that the sound was receding towards the east side of the harbour. In fact the customhouse people who had started to row round because of a vague impression that there had been some shouting in the harbour had to their immense surprise come upon a boat which at first seemed empty but which, they soon discovered, contained two human forms huddled up on the bottom boards, apparently dead, but at any rate insensible if they were still breathing. Attilio’s surmise that as the quickest way of dealing with this mystery the customhouse officer had decided to tow the boat at once to the police station on the east side was perfectly right; and also his conviction that now or never was his chance to slip out of that harbour where he and his companions felt themselves in a trap the door of which might snap to at any time. At the best it was a desperate situation, he felt. Cosmo felt it, too, if in a more detached way﻿—like a rather unwilling spectator. Yet his anxiety for the safety of his companions was as great as though he had known them all his life. Though he had in a way lost sight of his personal connection he could not help forming his own view, which he poured into Attilio’s ear while the two rowers put all their strength into their work.

===Chinese===
他没觉得自己有义务发出一声警告，因为他所等候的那一撞几乎立刻就发生了。他判断错了距离。由于船速很慢，那一撞十分轻微，甚至比科斯莫所预料的、撞上像系船浮标那样沉重的物体还要轻微。它其实更像一声有气无力的、空洞的声响，而非一次撞击。科斯莫早有准备，倒是最先感受到它的人，那上了年纪的船夫则机警地回头瞥了一眼。他没发出一点声响，甚至没想从座板上站起来。他只是——在科斯莫看来——松开了双桨。两个巡警却是在那空洞的撞击声又响了一次之后，才意识到出了事；而科斯莫也已明白，水上那个东西并不是浮标，而是另一只不比他们这只大多少的小船。于是两人都惊呼起来，一惊之下松了手。其中一个诧异地喊道：「一只空船。」这确是一桩出人意料的事。科斯莫心里没什么特别的打算，站起身来，而其中一个巡警也站起身，想抓住那只船也好、推开它也好——那时两只船已经并靠在一起了。黑暗中一个陌生的声音很响地说：「戴帽子的那个人，」于是仿佛中了魔法一般，三个人影一字排开地站了出来。科斯莫连吃惊的工夫都没有。两只船开始剧烈地晃荡碰撞，他只觉得自己被一双手揪住了衣领、另一双手攥住了一条胳膊，被一股不可抗拒的力量从两个巡警之间猛烈地拖了出去，那力量又突如其来地松开了他，仿佛他是一件没有生命的东西，他便重重地跌进了第二只船的船底；这时他的两条腿还没完全从那一只船上抽出来。在这一番暴烈的乾坤大挪移中，他的帽子也不知什么时候从头上掉了下去，并非出于他的任何算计。

他倒不算是吓着了，可他一时慌乱，倒是情有可原。一个人并不是每天都能这样连个招呼都不打便被劫了走的。他痛苦地意识到自己碍了新捕获者们的事。他肋骨上挨了一脚，腿上被人踩了一脚。他听见一下下重击落在坚硬的东西上，他知道那是人的脑壳，因为伴随着半途而废的、以呻吟告终的喊叫。这一番袭击又果决又凶猛，同时把动静压到了尽可能小。他所能听见的，只有沉重的击打和袭击者粗重的喘息。接着传来一种无可奈何的扑通声。「有人要淹死了，」他想。

他赶紧从那些格斗者的脚下朝前爬去。他的肋骨算是不幸中的大幸，那些人是赤着脚的，这也让这场惊人的变故更显安静。他一爬到船头便坐起身来，那时一切都已经结束了。三个朦胧的人影在船里一字排开地站着，纹丝不动，像几个刚干完一件了不起的活儿的苦力。他被拖出来的那只船浮在一两码开外，看上去空无一人。这整件事，前后不过一分钟，在科斯莫看来却仿佛是瞬间发生的。沿码头停泊的船只那边没有传来一丝声响，就连最近的那艘双桅船和那艘三桅小帆船也没有。一种终结般的寂静——譬如一颗地雷爆炸之后那种、仿佛把人的一切感知能力都化为乌有的寂静——一时攫住了科斯莫。不久他听见一个极其恳切却低沉的声音，向这沉默的世界发出告诫：「你们谁敢出一点声，我就回来要你们的命。」那声音完全不带个人色彩；它没有方向，也没有特定的对象。科斯莫虽把那几个字听得清清楚楚，却不能把它们与任何一个人的形象联系起来。他终于被唤醒了——他的手落到船舷上，触到了压在下面的几根人的手指。他像被烫了似地缩回手，这才探头去看。只见老船夫的白发紧贴着船舷，仿佛就枕在水面上。他无声地攀附着，便是在这副狼狈相里，仍显出他那令人尊敬的高龄所特有的、温顺的忍耐——科斯莫默默地端详着他。一个声音，这回不再是不带个人色彩的了，从船尾座板处传来：「把桨拿出来。」

「这儿水里还有个人，」科斯莫说，奇怪自己在如此荒唐的处境里自己的声音竟还能被人听见。可这话却收到了预期的效果，几乎他刚一开口，科斯莫就不得不帮着一个胡子拉碴的水手——一个他素不相识的人——把那老人拖进船来。把他弄过船舷倒没多重，可他们不得不像拖一个溺水的人那样侍弄他。他自己压根儿不试着帮一把。船里另几个人对这一番忙碌发生了兴趣。

「他死了吗？」船尾传来一声压低了的问话。

「他又老又弱，」科斯莫低声解释道。有人轻轻地、却骂了很久的脏话，末了添了一句：「这下可麻烦了。」

「那个恶棍巴博内，竟拖了一个快要死的人出来，」科斯莫冲动地开口道。

「行了，行了（Va bene, va bene）……把他塞进来，到船尾来，先生。」

科斯莫依言坐到了船尾座板上，身边那个人头一个动作便是把一只手轻轻搭在他肩上，那姿态透出一种克制的、欣喜的得意。等他认出那人竟是阿蒂利奥，一刹那间惊得连一句话都说不出来。再一刹那，却又觉得这是世上最自然不过的事了。

「仿佛没有什么能把我们分开似的，」这个非凡的人低声说。他从科斯莫肩上移开了手，吩咐两个划手——科斯莫猜他们就是塔上那两个低语者——把船划到那艘双桅船的船头下面去。「我们得躲开那些海关的家伙，」他说，「我瞧那桨帆船像是过来了。」

之后谁也不再开口，直到其中一个人抓住了双桅船的锚链、船靠着那船的船头停了下来，这时已能自己听见远处隐隐划桨声的科斯莫才低声问阿蒂利奥：「他们是冲你来的吗？」

「他们要真是冲什么来的话，」对方冷冷地答道，「那也是冲着一条好嗓子来的。你那一嗓子是怎么回事？」

「为了你留给我的那些文件，我在那两个巡警面前只好像只受了惊的小老鼠似的缩着，我觉得我总得显示显示自己。」科斯莫阴沉地作了这一番心理上的解释。他换了个口气补充道，他仿佛看见了英国军舰小艇的轮廓，那招呼它的诱惑便叫人无法抗拒。

「也许正是那一嗓子把他们引来的。他们对我们一无所知。运气在我们这边。我们是悄悄溜进去的。」这时划桨声已大到足以叫他们再没有攀谈的兴致，因为沉沉静夜里，沉重的桨在桨叉里起落，有一种带着目的、毫不容情的意味，那条十二支大桨、以短促有力的节拍划动的大桨帆船，仿佛在它空洞如雷的行进里把握着一条万无一失的航向。对藏在双桅船船头下那只小船里的人来说，光听得见、却看不见的那种紧张，正变得不可忍受。科斯莫焦急地暗想，自己是不是又要在这充满意外的一夜结束之前再次被俘；可就在最后关头，那条桨帆船一拐弯，从那艘桅帆船的船头下面驶了过去，仿佛只是要绕港湾兜一圈。船上人人都长长地舒了一口气。可几乎紧接着，划桨声戛然而止，船里每个人仿佛又化作了石头。

阿蒂利奥终于对着科斯莫的耳朵吐出一句：「老天（Per Dio）！他们发现了那只船。」

科斯莫几乎为自己那一声惊慌的低问来得如此之快而感到羞愧：

「船上的人死了吗？」

「我只知道，他们俩只要有哪一个还能开口，我们就完了，」阿蒂利奥低声回道。

「这些巡警本是要把我交给宪兵的，」科斯莫压低嗓门刚开口，划桨声突如其来地又冲进他们耳朵里来；可很快一切忧虑都烟消云散了，因为那声音分明正朝港湾东侧渐渐远去。事实上，海关的人当初之所以划船绕行，不过是模模糊糊觉得港里曾有过几声呼喊，却万分意外地撞上了一只船，那船乍看是空的，他们很快便发现船底板上蜷着两具人形，看上去已经死了，至少就算还有一口气，也已人事不省。阿蒂利奥的揣测一点不错：海关的军官为着尽快了结这桩疑案，决定当即把那只船拖到东侧的警察署去；他也坚信，此时不出港，更待何时——在这港湾里，他和他的同伴们觉得自己仿佛陷在一只随时会啪地合上的陷阱里。他感到，这充其量是个孤注一掷的局面。科斯莫也有同感，只是更为超然——像一个颇不情愿的旁观者。然而他对同伴们安危的忧虑，却深得仿佛与他们相识了一辈子。他虽在某种程度上把自己的牵连抛在了脑后，却仍忍不住形成了自己的看法，趁着两名划手使出浑身气力划桨的当儿，他把它倾进了阿蒂利奥的耳朵。

===Original===
Tensely rigid at the tiller, Attilio had listened, keeping his eyes fixed on the gap of dark gleaming water between the black heads of the two breakwaters.

“The signore is right,” he assented. “We could not hope to escape from that galley once she caught sight of us. Our only chance is to slip out of the port before she gets back to her station outside the jetties. This affair will be a great puzzle to them. They will lose some time talking it over with the gendarmes. Unless one or another of those sbirri comes to himself.”

“Yes. Those sbirri﻿ ﻿…” murmured Cosmo.

“What would you have? We did our best with the boat-stretchers, I can assure you.”

Cosmo had no doubt of that. The sound of crashing blows rained on those wretches’ heads had been sickening, but the memory comforted him now. So did the return of the profound stillness after the noise of the galley’s oars had died out in the distance. Cosmo took heart till it came upon him suddenly that there never had been a starry sky that gave so much light, no night so amazingly clear, no harbour of such an enormous extent. He felt he must not lose a minute. He jumped up and began to tear off his coat madly. Attilio exclaimed in dismay, “Stay! Don’t!” It looked as though his Englishman had made up his mind to swim for it. But Cosmo with a muttered, “I must lend a hand,” stepped lightly forward past the rowers, and began to feel under thwarts for a spare oar. Before he found it his hand came in contact with a naked foot. This recalled to him the existence of the ancient boatman. The poor old fellow who had taken no part in the fray had fallen overboard from mere weakness and had had a long soaking in chilly water. He lay curled up in the bows, shivering violently like a dog. For the moment Cosmo was simply vexed at this additional dead weight in the boat. He could think of nothing but of the customhouse galley. He imagined her long, slim, cleaving the glassy water, as if endowed with life, while the clumsy tub in which he sat felt to him a dead thing which had to be tugged along by main force every inch of the way. He set his teeth hard and pulled doggedly as if rowing in a losing race, without turning his head once. Suddenly he became aware of the end of the old Mole gliding past the boat, and that Attilio instead of holding on this way had taken a sweep and was following the outer side of the breakwater towards the shore. Presently, at his word, the oars were taken in, and the boat floated arrested in shallow water amongst the boulders strewn along the base of the Mole. The men panted after their exertions. Not a breath of wind stirred the chilly air. Cosmo returned aft and sat down by Attilio after putting on his coat.

It seemed as though Attilio, while steering with one hand, had managed with the other to go through the pockets of Cosmo’s coat, for his first words murmured in an anxious tone were “Signore, where are those papers?”

Cosmo had forgotten all about them. The shock was severe. “The papers,” he exclaimed faintly. “In my hat.”

“Yes, I put them there. You had it on your head in the boat. I recognized you by it.”

“Of course I had it on. Where is it?”

“God knows,” said Attilio bitterly. “I was asking you for the papers.”

“I only discovered that the packet was in my hat after I put it on,” protested Cosmo. “Four were standing over me already.”

“Is it possible?” exclaimed Attilio, very low.

“Afterwards I was watched all the time.”
