While they were exchanging those words in the extremity of their consternation, the man nearest to them went down suddenly on his knees and began to grope under the thwarts industriously. Having heard the word “hat” he had remembered that while battling with the sbirri he had trodden on some round object which had given way under his foot. He assured the signore that it was a thing that could not be helped while he tendered to him apologetically the rim with one hand and the crown with the other. It was crushed flat like an empty bag, but it was seized with avidity and presently Cosmo’s feelings were relieved by the discovery that it still contained the parcel of papers. Attilio took possession of it with a low nervous laugh. It was an emotional sound which, coming from that man, gave Cosmo food for wonder during the few moments the silence lasted before Attilio announced in a whisper, “Here she is.”

===Chinese===
阿蒂利奥身子笔直、紧绷着坐在舵柄旁，一直听着，眼睛定定地盯在两道防波堤漆黑的堤头之间那一隙幽幽泛光的水面上。

「先生说得对，」他附和道，「那条桨帆船一旦发现了我们，我们就别指望逃脱了。我们唯一的机会，是在它回到堤外它那岗位之前溜出港去。这件事会让他们大惑不解。他们在和宪兵商量这事儿上会耽误一些工夫。除非那几个巡警里头哪一个醒了过来。」

「是啊。那些巡警……」科斯莫喃喃道。

「你又能怎样呢？我们用船座板已尽了力，这我可以向你保证。」

科斯莫对此毫不怀疑。那些重击雨点般落在那几个家伙脑袋上的声音，曾叫人作呕，可此刻回想起来却叫他心安。远处桨帆船的桨声渐渐消歇之后，那深沉寂静的回归，也同样叫他心安。科斯莫壮起了胆，直到他突然意识到：从来没有哪一片星空给过这么多的光，没有哪一个夜晚如此惊人地澄澈，也没有哪一个港湾如此辽阔。他觉得自己一分钟也不能耽搁了。他跳起来，发疯似地扒下外衣。阿蒂利奥慌忙喊道：「别！别这样！」看那样子，仿佛他这位英国人已下定决心要泅水逃走了。可科斯莫咕哝了一句「我得帮一把」，便轻巧地越过两个划手朝前走去，开始隔着座板摸寻一支多余的桨。还没等他摸着，他的手便触到了一只赤裸的脚。这一触叫他记起了那位上了年纪的船夫的存在。这可怜的老人，并未参与那场格斗，单是虚弱便使他跌进了水里，在冰冷的水中浸泡了好一阵。他蜷在船头，像条狗似地剧烈地哆嗦着。此刻科斯莫只是恼火船上又多了这么一个累赘。他满脑子想的只有那条海关的桨帆船。他想见它又长又瘦，劈开那玻璃似的水面，仿佛被赋予了生命；而他坐着的这只笨拙的桶子，在他感觉里却是一件死物，每一寸都得靠蛮力硬拖。他咬紧牙关，闷头划着，仿佛在一场必输的赛船里拼命，一次也没有回头。忽然，他觉察到老防波堤的尽头正从船边掠过，而阿蒂利奥并没有照这样一直下去，却兜了一个弯，沿着防波堤的外侧朝岸边驶去。不久，听他一声令下，桨收了进来，船在防波堤基脚一带散布的乱石之间的浅水里停住了。几个人剧烈地喘着粗气。一丝风也没有，空气冷峭。科斯莫回到船尾，穿上外衣，在阿蒂利奥身边坐下。

看来阿蒂利奥一手掌舵的同时，另一只手竟已摸遍了科斯莫外衣的口袋，因为他头一句话便是焦灼地低声问道：「先生，那些文件呢？」

科斯莫早把它们忘得一干二净了。这一惊非同小可。「文件，」他有气无力地惊呼，「在我帽子里。」

「不错，是我放进去的。你在船上时一直把它戴在头上。我就是凭它认出你来的。」

「我当然戴在头上。它在哪儿？」

「天晓得，」阿蒂利奥苦涩地说，「我正是在向你要那些文件。」

「我是戴上它之后才发现里面有一包东西的，」科斯莫辩解道，「那时候已经有四个人站在我头顶上了。」

「这怎么可能？」阿蒂利奥极低地惊呼。

「打那以后，我就一直被他们盯着。」

正当他们在彼此惊愕之极地交换着这些话的时候，离他们最近的那个人忽然跪了下去，勤快地在座板底下摸索起来。他听见了「帽子」这个词，便记起在和巡警搏斗时，他曾踩在一个圆形的东西上，那东西在他脚底下塌了下去。他一边安慰先生说这是无可奈何的事，一边一手捏着帽檐、一手托着帽顶，赔着小心递了过来。它已被压得像一只空口袋一样扁了，可还是被如获至宝地接了过去，不久科斯莫便喜出望外地发现，那包文件居然还在里面。阿蒂利奥把它收了过去，发出一声低低的、神经质的笑。那是一种动了感情的声音，出自这样一个人之口，在那沉默持续的片刻里，倒给了科斯莫一些琢磨的由头。随后，阿蒂利奥低声宣布：「她来了。」

===Original===
Cosmo, looking seaward, saw on the black and gleaming water, polished like a mirror for the stars, an opaque hummock resembling the head of a rock; and he thought that the race had been won by a very narrow margin. The galley in fact had reached the heads of the jetties a very few minutes only after the boat. On getting back to his station the officer in the galley pulled about fifty yards clear of the end of the old Mole and ordered his men to lay oars in. He had left the solution of the mystery to the police. It was not his concern; and as he knew nothing of the existence of an outside boat, it never occurred to him to investigate along the coast. Attilio’s boat lurking close inshore was invisible from seaward. The distance between the two was great enough to cause the considerable clatter which is made when several oars are laid in together at the word of command to reach Cosmo only as a very faint, almost mysterious, sound. It was the last he was to hear for a very long time. He surrendered to the soft and invincible stillness of air and sea and stars enveloping the active desires and the secret fears of men who have the sombre earth for their stage. At every momentary pause in his long and fantastic adventure it returned with its splendid charm and glorious serenity, resembling the power of a great and unfathomable love whose tenderness like a sacred spell lays to rest all the vividities and all the violences of passionate desire.

Dreamily Cosmo had lost control of the trend of his thoughts, as one does on the verge of sleep. He regained it with a slight start and looked up at the round tower looming up, bulky, at the water’s edge. He was back again, having completed the cycle of his adventures and not knowing what would happen next. Everybody was silent. The two men at the thwarts had folded their arms and had let their chins sink on their breasts; while Attilio, sitting in the stern sheets, held his head up in an immobility to which his open eyes lent an air of extreme vigilance. The waste of waters seemed to extend from the shores of Italy to the very confines of the universe, with nothing on it but the black spot of the galley which moved no more than the head of a rock. “We can’t stay here till daylight,” thought Cosmo.

That same thought was in Attilio’s mind. The race between his boat and the galley had been very close. It was very probable that had it not been for Cosmo volunteering to pull the third oar it would have resulted in a dead heat, which of course would have meant capture. As it was, Attilio had just escaped being seen by pulling short round the jetty instead of holding on into the open sea. It was a risky thing to do, but then, since he had jeopardized the success of his escape through his desire to get hold of Cosmo again, there was nothing before him but a choice of risks.

===Chinese===
科斯莫朝海那面望去，看见在漆黑而泛光、亮如星镜的水面上，有一个不透明的鼓包，像是一块礁石的顶部；他想，这一场赛跑赢得真是险之又险。桨帆船其实只比小船晚那么几分钟到达防波堤的堤头。那军官回到岗位后，把船划到离老防波堤尽头约莫五十码开外，便下令收桨。他把这桩疑案的了结交给了警察。那不干他的事；而且他既不知道外头还有一只船，也就压根儿没想到要沿海岸去搜查。阿蒂利奥的船紧贴着岸边藏着，从海上是看不见的。两只船之间的距离，足以让一声令下、数桨齐收时那相当可观的哗啦声，传到科斯莫耳朵里时已只是一缕极细微、近乎神秘的声响。这是他在很长很长一段时间里听到的最后一声人间的响动了。他把自己交给了那柔润而不可战胜的空气与海洋与星斗的寂静——这寂静笼罩着那些以阴郁的大地为舞台的人们的勃勃欲念与隐秘恐惧。在他这场漫长而荒诞的奇遇的每一处片刻的停歇里，它总要带着它那辉煌的魅力和壮丽的安详重返，像是一种伟大而深不可测的爱的力量，它的柔情有如一道神圣的符咒，使一切炽烈欲望的鲜活与暴烈都归于安息。

科斯莫恍惚间已失去了对自己思路流向的把握，一如人在将睡未睡之际那样。他一个轻微的激灵又把它抓了回来，抬头望见那座圆塔的庞然身躯，矗立在水边。他又回来了，走完了一遭冒险的轮回，却不知接下来还会发生什么。人人都不作声。座板上的两个人都抱起了双臂，下巴垂到了胸前；而阿蒂利奥坐在船尾座板上，昂着头，纹丝不动，那双睁着的眼睛给他这副姿态添上一股极端警觉的神气。那片汪洋仿佛从意大利的海岸一直延伸到宇宙的尽头，水面上唯有那桨帆船的一个黑点，像一块礁石的顶部似地一动不动。「我们不能在这儿待到天亮，」科斯莫想。

同样的念头也在阿蒂利奥心里。他的船与那条桨帆船之间的赛跑实在凶险。要不是科斯莫自告奋勇划第三支桨，多半会是个不分先后的平局，那自然就等于被抓住了。就这样，阿蒂利奥也只是在堤外短促地一拐弯、没有一直驶向外海，才勉强没被瞧见。这做法未免冒险，可既然他为了重新得到科斯莫已使自己的逃亡功败垂成，那么摆在他面前的，便只是在种种风险之间作一抉择了。

===Original===
Attilio was a native of a tiny white townlet on the eastern shore of the Gulf of Genoa. His people were all small cultivators and fishermen. Their name was Pieschi, from whose blood came the well-known conspirator against the power of the Dorias and in the days of the Republic. Of this fact Attilio had heard only lately (Cantelucci had told him) with a certain satisfaction. In his early youth, spent on the coast of the South American continent, he had heard much talk of a subversive kind and had become familiar with the idea of revolt looked upon as an assertion of manly dignity and the spiritual aim of life. He had come back to his country about six months before and, beholding the aged faces of some of his people in the unchanged surroundings, it seemed to him that it was his own life that had been very long, though he was only about thirty. Being a relation of Cantelucci he found himself very soon in touch with the humbler members of secret societies, survivals of the revolutionary epoch, stirred up by the downfall of the Empire and inspired by grandiose ideas, by the hatred of the Austrian invaders bringing back with them the old tyrannical superstitions of religion and the oppression of privileged classes. Like the polite innkeeper he believed in the absolute equality of all men. He respected all religions but despised the priests who preached submission and perceived nothing extravagant in the formation of an Italian empire (of which he had the first hint from the irritable old cobbler, the uncle of Cecchina) since there was a great man﻿—a great Emperor﻿—to put at its head, very close at hand. The great thing was to keep him safe from the attempts of all these kings and princes now engaged in plotting against his life in Vienna﻿—till the hour of action came. No small task, for the world outside the ranks of the people was full of his enemies.

Attilio, still and silent by Cosmo’s side, was not reproaching himself for having gone in the evening to say goodbye to Cecchina. The girl herself had been surprised to see him, for they had said goodbye already in the afternoon. But this love affair was not quite two months old and he could not have been satisfied with a hurried wordless goodbye, snatched behind a half-closed door, with several people drinking at the long table in Cantelucci’s kitchen on one side and a crabbed old woman rummaging noisily in a storeroom at the end of the dark passage. Cecchina had, of course, reproached him for coming, but not very much. Neither of them dreamed of there being any danger in it. Then, straight out of her arms, as it were, he had stepped into that ambush! His presence of mind and his agility proved too much for the party of the stupid Barbone. It was only after he had given them the slip in the maze of small garden plots at the back of the houses that he had time, while lying behind a low wall, to think over this unexpected trouble. He knew that the fellows who were after him belonged to the police, because they had called on the soldiers for assistance; but he concluded that he owed this surprise to some jealous admirer of Cecchina. It was easy enough for any base scoundrel to set the police after a man in these troubled times. It may even have been one of Cantelucci’s affiliated friends. His suspicions rested on the small employees who took their meals at the inn, and especially on a lanky scribe with a pointed nose like a rat who had the habit of going in and out through the courier’s room, only, Attilio believed, in order to make eyes at Cecchina. That the ambush had been laid on the evening fixed for his departure was a mere coincidence.

The real danger of the position was in having the papers on him, but, anxious that his friends at the tower should not give him up, he came out of his hiding-place too soon. The soldiers had gone away, but the sbirri were still half-heartedly poking about in dark corners and caught sight of him. Another rush saved him for the moment. The position he felt was growing desperate. He dared not throw away the papers. The discovery of Cosmo sitting amongst the stones was an event so extraordinary in itself that it revolutionized his rational view of life as a whole in the way a miracle might have done. He felt suddenly an awed and confiding love for that marvellous person fate had thrown in his way. The pursuit was close. There was no time to explain. There was no need.

===Chinese===
阿蒂利奥是热那亚湾东岸一座小小的白色镇子上的本地人。他那一族都是些小自耕农和渔夫。他们姓皮耶斯基（Pieschi），共和时代那位赫赫有名的、密谋反抗多里亚家族（Dorias）权力的叛逆，便是从这一族的血脉里出来的。这件事阿蒂利奥近来才听说（是坎泰洛奇告诉他的），颇感几分欣慰。他早年是在南美洲大陆的海岸上度过的，在那里听了许多带有颠覆意味的言论，已把造反的观念看作一种对男子汉尊严的伸张、一种人生的精神目标，习以为常。他大约半年前回到本国，看见自家一些人在那原封不动的环境里苍老了许多的脸，便觉得倒像是自己的这一生前前后后过得太长了，其实他不过三十上下。因为与坎泰洛奇是亲戚，他很快就和那些秘密会社里较卑微的成员们搭上了线——那些会社是大革命（the Revolution）时代的遗存，因帝国（the Empire）的倾覆而重新骚动，又被种种宏大的念头所鼓舞，怀着对奥地利入侵者的仇恨——这些入侵者把宗教的旧式专横迷信和特权阶级的压迫又一并带了回来。和那位客客气气的客栈老板一样，他相信人人绝对平等。他尊重一切宗教，却蔑视那些劝人顺从的神父；他也不觉得建立一个意大利帝国有什么荒唐（他头一回听到这个念头，是从塞基娜那个易怒的老鞋匠舅舅嘴里）——因为近在咫尺便有一位伟人、一位伟大的皇帝（Emperor），可以请来坐上它的宝座。头等大事，是要护着他，使他免遭那些此刻正在维也纳（Vienna）密谋害他性命的国王和王公们的毒手——只等行动的时辰到来。这不是一桩轻省的差事，因为人民队伍之外的世界，到处都是他的仇敌。

阿蒂利奥静静地、默默地坐在科斯莫身边，并不因自己傍晚跑去向塞基娜（Cecchina）道别而自责。那姑娘自己看见他也吃惊，因为他们下午已经道过别了。可这段恋情还不到两个月，他怎能满足于在半掩的门后匆匆忙忙、一言不发地偷着道个别——一边是坎泰洛奇厨房那张长桌上有好几个人在喝酒，另一边是幽暗过道尽头一个脾气乖戾的老太婆在杂物间里翻箱倒柜，弄得叮呤咣啷。塞基娜当然怪他不该来，却也没有多说什么。他们俩谁也没想到这会有什么危险。谁知，才从她怀抱里出来，可说是前脚才出门，他便一头撞进了那场埋伏！他那临危不乱的镇定和身手，对付巴博内那一伙蠢货绰绰有余。直到他在屋后那一片蛛网似的小菜园里甩掉了他们，他才有工夫趴在一堵矮墙后面，琢磨这桩突如其来的麻烦。他知道追他的那些人是官府的，因为他们请了士兵来帮忙；可他断定，这场惊扰要归功于塞基娜某个吃醋的爱慕者。在这兵荒马乱的年头，随便哪个下流的坏蛋要让官府去盯一个人，再容易不过了。甚至也可能是坎泰洛奇某个有结社之交的朋友干的。他的怀疑落在那些在旅店里搭伙的小职员身上，尤其是落在了一个长着老鼠一般尖鼻子的瘦高个子文书身上——这人有事没事总爱从信使的屋里进进出出，阿蒂利奥深信，他这无非是为了向塞基娜挤眉弄眼。这场埋伏偏偏设在他定下要启程的那个晚上，不过是个巧合罢了。

处境真正的危险在于他身上带着文件，可他唯恐塔上的朋友等得不耐烦、丢下他不管，便过早地从藏身之处钻了出来。士兵们已经走了，可巡警们还在那里不死不活地朝黑暗的角落里东戳西戳，一眼瞥见了他。又是一次飞奔，暂时救了他。他觉得处境正变得走投无路。他不敢把文件扔掉。发现科斯莫坐在石堆之中，这件事本身就离奇到了极点，竟像一场奇迹那样，把他整个儿对人生的理性看法彻底翻了个个儿。他忽然对这位命运抛到他面前的神奇人物生出一种既敬畏又信赖的倾慕。追兵就在身后。没有工夫解释。也没有解释的必要。

===Original===
But directly he found himself safe in the boat Attilio began to regret having parted with the papers. It was not much use proceeding on his mission without these documents entrusted to him by Cantelucci, acting on behalf of superior powers.

He asked himself what could have happened to Cosmo? Did the fellows arrest him on suspicion? That was not very likely, and at worst it would not mean more than a short detention. They would not dare to search him, surely. But even if they found the packet Cosmo would declare it his own property and object to its being opened. He had a complete confidence in Cosmo’s loyalty and, what was more, in that young Englishman’s power to have his own way. He had the manner for that and the face for that. The face and bearing of a man with whom it was lucky to be associated in anything.

The galley being just then at the other end of her beat, Attilio saw his way clear to slip into the harbour. The state of perfect quietness over the whole extent of the harbour encouraged his native audacity. He began by pulling to the east side where the gendarmerie office was near the quay. Everything was quiet there. He made his men lay their oars amongst the shadows of the anchored shipping and waited. Sleep, breathless sleep, reigned on shore and afloat. Attilio began to think that Cosmo could not have been discovered. If so, then he must be nearing Cantelucci’s inn by this time. He resolved then to board one of the empty coasters moored to the quay, wait for the morning there, and then go himself to the inn, where he could remain concealed till another departure could be arranged. He told his men to pull gently to the darkest part of the quay. And then he heard Cosmo’s mighty shout. He was nearly as confounded by it as the sbirri in the boat. That voice bursting out on the profound stillness seemed loud enough to wake up every sleeper in the town, to bring the stones rolling down the hillsides. And almost at once he thought, “What luck!” The luck of the Englishman’s amazing impudence; for what other man would have thought of doing that thing? He told his rowers to lay their oars in quietly and get hold of the boat-stretchers. The extremely, feeble pulling of the old boatman gave the time for these preparations. He whispered his instructions: “We’ve got to get a foreign signore out of that boat. The others in her will be sbirri. Hit them hard.” Just before the boats came into contact he recognized Cosmo’s form standing up. It was then that he pronounced the words, “the man in the hat,” which were heard by Cosmo. Attilio ascribed it all to luck that attended those who had anything to do with that Englishman. Even the very escape unseen from the harbour he ascribed, not to Cosmo’s extra oar, but to Cosmo’s peculiar personality.

Without departing from his immobility he broke silence by a “signore,” pronounced in a distinct but restrained voice. Cosmo was glad to learn the story before the moment came for them to part. But the theory of luck which Attilio tacked on to the facts did not seem to him convincing. He remarked that if Attilio had not come for him at all he would have been far on the way in his mysterious affairs, whereas now he was only in another trap.

For all answer the other murmured, “Si, but I wonder if it would have been the same. Signore, isn’t it strange that we should have been drawn together from the first moment you put foot in Genoa?”

“It is,” said Cosmo, with an emphasis that encouraged the other to continue, but with a less assured voice.

“Some people of old believed that stars have something to do with meetings and partings by their disposition and that some if not all men have each a star allotted to them.”

“Perhaps,” said Cosmo in the same subdued voice. “But I believe there is a man greater than you or I who believes he has a star of his own.”

“Napoleon, perhaps.”

“So I have heard,” said Cosmo, and thought, “Here he is, whenever two men meet he is a third, one can’t get rid of him.”

“I wonder where it is,” said Attilio, as if to himself, looking up at the sky. “Or yours, or mine,” he added in a still lower tone. “They must be pretty close together.”

Cosmo humoured the superstitious strain absently, for he felt a secret sympathy for that man. “Yes, it looks as if yours and mine had been fated to draw together.”

“No, I mean all three together.”

“Do you? Then you must know more than I do. Though indeed as a matter of fact he is not very far from us where we sit. But don’t you think, my friend, that there are men and women, too, whose stars mark them for loneliness no man can approach?”

“You mean because they are great.”

“Because they are incomparable,” said Cosmo after a short pause, in which Attilio seemed to ponder.

“I like that what you said,” Attilio was heard at last. “Their stars may be lonely. Look how still they are. But men are more like ships that come suddenly upon each other without a warning. And yet they, too, are guided by the stars. I can’t get over the wonder of our meeting tonight.”

“If you hadn’t been so long in saying goodbye we wouldn’t have met,” said Cosmo, looking at the two men dozing on the thwarts, the whisperers of the tower. They were not at all like what he had imagined them to be.

Attilio gazed at his Englishman for a time closely. He seemed to see a smile on Cosmo’s lips. Wonder at his omniscience prevented him from making a reply. He preferred not to ask, and yet he was incapable of forming a guess, for there are certain kinds of obviousness that escape speculation.

“You may be right,” he said. “It’s the first time in my life that I found it hard to say goodbye. I begin to believe,” he went on murmuring, “that there are people it would be better for one not to know. There are women﻿ ﻿…”

“Yes,” said Cosmo, very low and as if unconscious of what he was saying. “I have seen your faces very close together.”

The other made a slight movement away from Cosmo and then bent towards him. “You have seen,” he said slowly and stopped short. He was thinking of something that had happened only two hours before. “Oh well,” he said with composure, “you know everything, you see everything that happens. Do you know what will happen to us two?”

“It’s very likely that when we part we will never see each other again,” Cosmo said, resting his elbows on his knees and taking his head between his hands. He did not look like a man preparing to go ashore.

===Chinese===
可是一俟他在船上安顿下来、转危为安，阿蒂利奥便开始后悔把文件脱了手。没有坎泰洛奇——他是奉上面势力之命行事——托付给他的这些文件，他这一趟差使几乎是白跑了。

他暗自揣度，科斯莫后来怎样了呢？那些家伙是起疑才抓他的吗？这倒不大可能，而且往坏里说，也不过是短期扣押。他们总不至于敢搜他的身吧。可即便他们发现了那包东西，科斯莫也会声明那是他自己的财物，反对拆开它。他对科斯莫的忠诚深信不疑，而且更相信这位年轻英国人那份能把事情办成、能遂己愿的本事。他有那份气度，也有那张面孔。那是一张脸、一种仪态，凡是与他共事的人，都算走运。

那桨帆船这会儿正在它巡弋路线的另一头，阿蒂利奥看清了自己的路，可以溜进港湾去了。整个港湾一片绝对的寂静，助长了他天生的胆大妄为。他先把船划到东侧，宪兵办事处就在码头附近。那里一切悄无声息。他命人收了桨，停歇在抛锚船只的阴影里，等候着。岸上、水上，都笼罩着睡眠，那种屏息的睡眠。阿蒂利奥开始相信科斯莫并未被发现。若真如此，那么这会儿他该已快到坎泰洛奇的旅店了。他于是决定登上码头边某条空着的沿海小船，在那儿等到天明，再亲自到旅店去——在那里他可以藏身，直到另一次启程安排妥当。他命手下人轻轻地把船划到码头最暗的那一段。就在那时，他听见了科斯莫那一声震天的呐喊。他几乎和船里的两个巡警一样被吓蒙了。那声音在深沉的寂静里迸发出来，响亮得足以唤醒城里每一个酣睡的人，足以把山坡上的石头都震得滚下来。紧接着他几乎立刻想道：「多好的运气！」这是那个英国人那惊人胆大妄为所招来的运气；换作别的什么人，谁会想到干这种事？他叫划手们悄悄收了桨，去摸船座板。老船夫那虚弱之极的划桨，恰好给了他们准备的时间。他低声吩咐：「我们要从那只船里救出一个外国的先生。船里其余的人是巡警。下狠手打。」就在两条船即将相撞之前，他认出了站起身来的科斯莫的身形。于是他念出了「戴帽子的那个人」这几个字——科斯莫听见了它。阿蒂利奥把这一切都归之于那位英国人身上附着的运气。甚至连这一次神不知鬼不觉地逃出港湾，他也不归功于科斯莫多划的那一支桨，而归功于科斯莫那奇特的禀赋。

他没有改变那副纹丝不动的姿态，只吐出一句「先生」，声音清晰而克制。科斯莫正乐得在他们分手之前听听事情的原委。可阿蒂利奥把这些事归之于运气的说法，他并不信服。他说，要是阿蒂利奥压根儿没来找他，这会儿他早已在自己那桩神秘的勾当里走出去好远了，而现在他不过是从一个陷阱落进了另一个陷阱罢了。

对方只低声答了一句：「是（Si），可我怀疑事情是否还会一样。先生，我们从你踏进热那亚的头一刻起便被牵引到一起，这岂不奇怪吗？」

「是奇怪，」科斯莫说，那强调的口气鼓励了对方说下去，只是声音已不那么笃定。

「古人相信，星辰与人间的聚散离合有某种干系，他们还相信，有些人——如果不是所有的人——各有一颗分派给他的星。」

「也许吧，」科斯莫以同样低沉的嗓音说，「不过我相信，有那么一个人，他比你我都伟大，他相信他自己有一颗星。」

「拿破仑（Napoleon），也许。」

「我听说正是，」科斯莫说，心里想：「他又来了，每当两个人相遇，他总是那第三个，甩都甩不掉。」

「我不知道它在哪儿，」阿蒂利奥说，像是对自己说的，仰望天空。「你的，或是我的，」他又以更低的嗓音添了一句，「它们一定挨得很近。」

科斯莫心不在焉地顺着这迷信的调子，因为他暗中觉得与这个人有几分亲近。「是啊，看来你的星和我的星是命中注定要凑到一起的。」

「不，我是说我们三个都凑到一起。」

「是吗？那你一定知道得比我多了。不过说实在的，事实上我们坐着这儿，他离我们也不算远。可你不觉得吗，我的朋友，世上有那么一些男人，也有女人，他们的星注定他们孤独，无人能够靠近？」

「你的意思是，因为他们伟大。」

「因为他们无可比拟，」科斯莫停了一停才说，在这一停里，阿蒂利奥似乎在沉思。

「我喜欢你说的这话，」终于又听见了阿蒂利奥的声音，「他们的星也许是孤独的。瞧它们多么静。可人更像船只，彼此事先毫无预警地突然相遇。然而它们，也是由星辰指引的。我至今仍不能从我们今夜相遇的惊奇里回过神来。」

「要是你没有那么磨蹭着道别，我们本不会相遇，」科斯莫说，望着座板上打盹的那两个人，塔上的两个低语者。他们和他想象的样子截然不同。

阿蒂利奥紧紧地盯着他的英国人看了一阵。他仿佛看见科斯莫唇边浮着一丝笑。对他那份无所不知的本领的惊叹，叫他答不上话来。他宁可不去问，可他也猜不出个所以然，因为世上有那么一种显而易见的事，是猜测所不及的。

「也许你说得对，」他说，「这还是我平生头一次觉得道别竟是这般难舍。我开始相信，」他喃喃地接着说，「世上有那么一些人，不相识反倒更好。有一些女人……」

「是啊，」科斯莫说，声音极低，仿佛并不自觉自己在说什么，「我看见你们两张脸贴得极近。」

对方微微地朝旁边挪了挪，又俯过身来凑近他。「你看见了，」他缓缓地说，戛然止住。他在想着两个小时之前发生的一件事。「哦，也罢，」他从容地说，「你什么都知道，发生的什么事你都看得见。你可知道我们俩将来会怎样？」

「很可能，我们这一别，便再也不会相见了，」科斯莫说，双肘支在膝上，双手捧住了头。他看起来不像一个准备上岸的人。

===Original===
There were no material difficulties absolutely to prevent him from landing. The foot of the tower with the narrow strip of ground which a boat could approach was not sixty yards off, and all this was in the shadow of its own reflection, the high side of the breakwater, the bulk of the tower, making the glassy water dark in that corner of the shore. And besides, the water in which the boat floated was so shallow that Cosmo could have got to land by wading from where the boat lay without wetting himself much above the knees, should Attilio refuse to come out from under the shelter of the rock. But probably Attilio would not have objected. The difficulty was not there.

Attilio must have been thinking on the same subject, as became evident when he asked Cosmo whether those sbirri knew where he lived. After some reflection Cosmo said that he was quite certain they knew nothing about it. The sbirri had put no questions to him. They had not, he said, displayed any particular curiosity about what he was. “But why do you ask?”

“Don’t you know?” said Attilio, with only half-affected surprise. “There might have been a dozen of them waiting for you in the neighbourhood on the chance of your returning, and you have no other place to go to.”

“No, I haven’t,” said Cosmo in a tone as though he regretted that circumstance. He thought, however, that there might have been some of them out between the port and the town, and he knew only one way and that not very well, he added.

As a matter of fact that danger was altogether imaginary, because Barbone, who certainly was in the pay of the police for work of that sort, was not imaginative enough to do things without orders, and after sending his prisoner off left the rest of the gendarmes and went home to bed, while his young acolyte went about his own affairs. The other two sbirri were being medically attended to, one of them especially being very nearly half killed by an unlucky blow on the temple. All the other sbirro could say in a feeble voice was that there were four in the boat, that they were attacked by an inexplicable murderous gang, and that he imagined that the other two, the prisoner and the boatman, were now dead and very likely at the bottom of the harbour. The brigadier of the gendarmerie could not get any more out of him, and, knowing absolutely nothing of the affair, thought it would be time to make his report to the superior authorities in the morning. All he did was to go round to the places where the boats were chained, which were under his particular charge, and count the boats. Not one was wanting. His responsibility was not engaged.

Thus there was nothing between Cosmo and Cantelucci’s inn except his own distaste. There was a strange tameness in that proceeding, a lack of finality, something almost degrading. He imagined himself slinking like a criminal at the back of the beastly guardhouse, starting at shadows, creeping under the colonnade, getting lost in those dreadful deep lanes between palaces, with the constant dread of having suddenly the paws of those vile fellows laid on him and being dragged to some police post with an absurd tale on his lips and without a hat on his head and what for? Simply to get back to that abominable bedroom. However, he would have to go through it.

“Pity you don’t know the town,” Attilio’s cautious voice was heard again, “or else I could tell you of a place where you could spend the remainder of the night and send word to your servant tomorrow. But you could not find it by yourself. And that’s a pity. I assure Your Excellency that she is a real good woman. To have a secret place is not such a bad thing. One never knows what one may need, and she is a creature to be trusted. She has an Italian heart and she is a giardiniera too. What more could I tell you?”

Cosmo thought to himself vaguely that the girl he had seen in Cantelucci’s kitchen did not look like a woman gardener, though of course if Attilio had a love affair it would be naturally amongst people of that sort. But it occurred to him that perhaps it was some other woman Attilio was talking about. He made no movement. Attilio’s murmurs took on a tone of resignation. “Your luck, signore, will depart with you, and perhaps ours will follow after.” Cosmo protested against that unreasonable assumption, which was of course an absurdity but nevertheless touched him in one of those sensitive spots which are like a défaut d’armure in the battle-harness of various conceits which one wears against one’s kind. He considered luck less in a sudden overwhelming conviction of it, in the manner of a man who had crossed the path of a radiating influence, or who had awakened a sleeping and destructive power which would now pursue him to the end of his life. He was young, farouche, mistrustful and austere, not like a stoic, but in the more human way like a man who has been born fastidious. In a sense altogether unworldly. Attilio emitted an audible sigh.

“You won’t call it your luck,” he pursued. “Well, let us leave it without a name. It is something in you. Your carelessness in following your fantasy, signore, as when you forced your presence on me only two days ago,” he insisted, as if carelessness and fantasy were the compelling instruments of success. His voice was at its lowest as he added: “Your genius makes you true to your will.”

No human being could have been insensible to such words uttered unexpectedly in a tone of secret earnestness. But Cosmo’s inward response was a feeling of profound despondency. He was crushed by their appalling unfitness. For the last twenty-four hours he had been asking himself whether he had a will of his own, and it had seemed to him that he had lost the notion of the real nature of courage. At that very moment while listening to the mysteriously low pitch of Attilio’s voice the thought flashed through his mind that there was something within him that made of him a predestined victim of remorse.

“You can’t possibly know anything about me, Attilio,” he said, “and whatever you like to imagine about me, you will have to put me on shore presently. I can’t stay here till the morning, and neither can you,” he added. “What are you thinking of doing? What can you do?”

“Is it possible that it is of any interest to the signore? Only the other evening I could not induce you to leave me to myself, and now you are impatient to leave me to my fate. What can I do? I can always take a desperate chance,” he paused, and added through his clenched teeth, “and when I think what little I need to make it almost safe﻿ ﻿…” The piously uttered exclamation, “Ah, Dio!” was accompanied by a shake of a clenched fist apparently addressed to the universe, but made as it were discreetly, in keeping with the low and forcible tones.

“And what is that?” asked Cosmo, raising his head.

===Chinese===
要说上岸，倒也并没有什么绝对的实实在在的障碍。塔脚下那一窄条可以泊船的陆地，离此不过六十码，而且这一切都笼罩在它自身投下的阴影里——防波堤高耸的一面、塔敦实的身躯，把那一隅岸边的玻璃般水面映得幽暗。况且船所浮着的水浅得很，万一阿蒂利奥不肯从那块礁石的庇护下出来，科斯莫从船这儿涉水过去上岸，湿也不会湿过膝盖。可阿蒂利奥多半也不会反对。难处并不在此。

阿蒂利奥想必也在想着同一桩事，这从他的问话里便看得出来——他问科斯莫，那些巡警知不知道他住在哪儿。科斯莫想了想，说他十分肯定他们一无所知。那些巡警一句也没盘问过他。他们对他是什么人，他说，并未流露出什么特别的好奇心。「可你问这个做什么？」

「你不知道吗？」阿蒂利奥说，只做出一半惊讶的样子，「说不定有十几个人正守在你住处附近，碰运气等你回去呢，再说你也没有别的地方可去。」

「是的，我没有，」科斯莫说，那口气倒好像是为这事感到惋惜。不过他又说，港口和城区之间也许会布着他们一些人，而他只认得一条路，而且认得也不太熟。

事实上，那份危险完全是想象出来的，因为巴博内——他干那种事当然是受警察的雇——还没那份想象力，能不奉命行事；他把囚犯一打发走，便撇下其余的宪兵，回家睡觉去了，他那个年轻的小徒弟也去忙自己的事。另外两个巡警正在接受医治，其中一个太阳穴上挨了不幸的一击，几乎半个送了命。另一个巡警只能用微弱的声音说，船上原是四个人，他们遭到了一伙莫名其妙的杀人凶徒的袭击，他猜想另外那两个，即囚犯和船夫，如今已死，多半已沉在港湾底下了。宪兵队长再也无法从他嘴里问出更多的话来，而对这件事又一无所知，便觉得还是等早上再向上司报告为好。他所做的，不过是到他专司看管的那些锁船之处走了一圈，点了点船。一只也不少。他并无失职之责。

如此一来，横亘在科斯莫和坎泰洛奇的旅店之间的，便只有他自己的厌恶了。这一举动里有一种奇怪的驯顺，一种缺乏了结之感，一种近乎屈辱的东西。他想象自己像罪犯一般在那间可憎的警卫室背后溜过去，对影子一惊一乍，在柱廊下蹑手蹑脚，在那一条条宫殿之间的可怕深巷里迷了路，时刻提心吊胆，怕那些下流家伙的爪子忽然搭上自己的肩头，把自己拖到某个警察署去，嘴里还编着一段荒唐的借口，头上连顶帽子也没有——为的什么呢？无非是回到那间可憎的卧室去。然而，他非得走这一遭不可。

「可惜你不熟这城，」又听见了阿蒂利奥那谨慎的声音，「不然我可以告诉你一个地方，你可以在那儿度过这一夜剩下的工夫，明天再派人给你的仆人送信。可你一个人是找不着的。这就可惜了。我向大人保证，她是个真正的好女人。有这么一个隐秘的去处，倒也不算坏事。谁知什么时候用得着什么，她是一个靠得住的人。她有一颗意大利人的心，又是个女菜农（giardiniera）。我还能告诉你什么呢？」

科斯莫模模糊糊地想，他在坎泰洛奇厨房里见过的那个姑娘，可不像一个女菜农，当然，阿蒂利奥要是有恋情，自然是与那一类人家里的。可他转念又想，阿蒂利奥说的也许是另一个女人。他一动不动。阿蒂利奥的低语带上了一种无可奈何的口气。「先生的运气，将随先生一同离去，说不定我们的运气也会跟着一起走了呢。」科斯莫反对这个不讲道理的假设，这当然是荒唐的，却偏偏触到了他某一处敏感的所在——那种敏感之处，好比一个人用来对付同类的那一身种种自负的铠甲上的一个缺口（défaut d’armure）。他对运气的估量，更多是出于一种突如其来的、压倒性的确信，那情形，仿佛一个曾穿过某种辐射影响力的人，又仿佛一个惊醒了一种沉睡的、毁灭性的力量、而这力量此后将追逐他到生命尽头的人。他年轻、孤傲（farouche）、多疑、严峻，不像一个斯多葛派，而是一种更人间的方式——像一个生来便挑剔的人。在一种全然不染尘俗的意义上。阿蒂利奥发出了一声听得见的叹息。

「你不肯把它叫作你的运气，」他接着说，「好吧，我们就让它无名无姓。那是你身上的一种东西。你追随着自己的奇想的那种漫不经心，先生，正如两天前你硬闯到我面前来那样，」他坚持道，仿佛漫不经心和奇想正是成功的利器。他以最低的嗓音添了一句：「你的天赋使你忠于自己的意志。」

任谁听了这般突如其来、以秘密而恳切的口吻吐出的话，都不能无动于衷。可科斯莫内心的反应，却是一种深深的沮丧。他被这些话惊人的不得体压垮了。过去二十四个钟头里，他一直在问自己是否还有自己的意志，他似乎已失去了对勇气真正本质的概念。便在此刻，一边听着阿蒂利奥那种秘而压低的嗓音，一个念头闪过他心头：他身上有一种东西，使他成为一个预定了的、悔恨的牺牲品。

「你对我根本不可能知道什么，阿蒂利奥，」他说，「随你怎么想象我，你总得马上把我送上岸去。我不能在这儿待到天亮，你也不能，」他又说，「你打算怎么办？你能怎么办？」

「先生竟会对此感到兴趣，这怎么可能呢？就在几天前的傍晚，我还劝不动先生让我独处，现在先生却又急着要把我撇给我的命运了。我能怎么办？我总还可以孤注一掷，」他停了停，又从牙缝里挤出一句，「而且我一想，只需多么微不足道的一点儿东西，就能叫它几乎稳操胜券……」那一句虔敬地吐出的感叹「啊，天主（Ah, Dio）！」，伴随着一记挥拳，那拳头仿佛是朝着整个宇宙挥去的，却又挥得颇为收敛，与那低沉而有力的语调相称。

「那是什么？」科斯莫抬起头来问。

===Original===
“Two pairs of stout arms, nothing more. With four oars and this boat and using a little judgment in getting away I would defy that fellow there.” He jerked his head towards the galley which in this tideless sea had not shifted her position a yard. “Yes,” he went on, “I could even hope to remain unseen on account of a quick dash.”

And he explained to Cosmo further that in an hour or so a little nearer the break of day, when men get heavy and sleepy, the watchfulness of those customhouse people would be relaxed and give him a better chance. But if he was seen then he could still hope to out-row them, though he would have preferred it the other way because with a boat making for the open sea they would very soon guess that there must be some vessel waiting for her, and by telling the tale on shore, that government xebec lying in the harbour would soon be out in chase. She was fast, and in twenty-four hours she would soon manage to overhaul all the craft she would sight between this and the place he was going to.

“And where is that?” asked Cosmo, letting his head rest on his hands again.

“In the direction of Livorno,” said the other, and checked himself. “But perhaps I had better not tell you, for should you happen to be interrogated by all those magistrates, or perhaps by the Austrians, you would of course want to speak the truth as becomes a gentleman﻿—a nobilissimo signore﻿—unless you manage to forget what I have already told you or perchance elect to come with us.”

“Come with you,” repeated Cosmo, before something peculiar in the tone made him sit up and face Attilio. “I believe you are capable of carrying me off.”

“Dio ne voglio,” was Attilio’s answer, “God forbid. The noise you would make would bring no end of trouble. But for that perhaps it would have been better for me,” he added reflectively. “Whereas I have made up my mind that there should be nothing but good from our association. Yet, signore, you very nearly went away with us without any question at all, for our head pointed to seaward and you could have had no idea that I was coming in here. Confess, signore, you didn’t think of return then. I had only to hold the tiller straight another five minutes and I would have had you in my power.”

“You were afraid of the dogana galley, my friend,” said Cosmo as if arguing a point.

“Signore, this minute,” said Attilio with the utmost seriousness. “Wake up there,” he said in a raised undertone to his two men. “Take an oar, Pietro, and pull the boat to the foot of the tower.”

“There is also that old boatman,” said Cosmo.

“Hold,” said Attilio. “Him I will not land. They will be at his place in the morning, and then he tells his tale﻿ ﻿… unless he is dead. See forward there.”

A very subdued murmur arose in the bows and Attilio muttered, “Pietro would not talk to a dead man.”

“He is extremely feeble,” said Cosmo.

It appeared on Attilio’s enquiry that this encumbrance as he called him was just strong enough to be helped over the thwarts. Presently, sustained under the elbows, he joined Cosmo in the stem sheets, where they made him sit between them. He let his big hands lie in his lap. From time to time he shivered patiently.

“That wretch Barbone knows no pity,” observed Cosmo.

“I suppose he was the nearest he could get. What tyranny! The helpless are at the mercy of those fellows. He saved himself the trouble of going three doors farther.”

They both looked at the ancient frame that age had not shrivelled.

“A fine man once,” said Attilio in a low voice. “Can you hear me, vecchio?”

“Si, and see you too, but I don’t know your voice,” was the answer in a voice stronger than either of them expected, but betraying no sort of interest.

“They will certainly throw him into prison.” And to Cosmo’s indignant exclamation Attilio pointed out that the old man would be the only person they would be able to get hold of and he would have to pay for all the rest.

Cosmo expressed the opinion that he would not stay there long.

“Better for him to die under the open sky than in prison,” murmured Attilio in a gloomy voice. “Listen, old man, could you keep the boat straight at a star if I were to point you one?”

“I was at home in a boat before I could speak plainly,” was the answer, while the boatman raised his arm and let it rest on the tiller as if to prove that he had strength enough for that at least.

“I have my boat’s crew, signore. Let him do something for all Italy if it is with his last breath, that old Genoese. And now if you were only to take that bow oar you have been using so well only a few moments ago, I will pull stroke and we will make this boat fly.”

===Chinese===
「两对结实的胳膊，仅此而已。有四支桨、这只船，再加一点动身时机上的判断，我就敢和那边那个家伙较量。」他把头朝那条桨帆船一摆——在这片无潮无汐的海上，它一码也没有挪动过位置。「是的，」他接着说，「我甚至可以指望，靠一个迅猛的冲刺而不被它瞧见。」

他进一步向科斯莫解释，再过约莫一个钟头、临近天色将亮未亮之时，人便会变得迟钝嗜睡，那些海关人员的警觉会松懈下来，他的机会也就更好。可万一那时被他们瞧见，他也仍可指望在桨上赛过他们，只是他宁可走前一条路，因为若是一只船朝外海驶去，他们很快就会猜到必然有某只船在等着接应它，而一旦这事在岸上传开，泊在港里的那艘政府的三桅小帆船很快就会出港追赶。它航速很快，二十四小时之内，足以把从这里到他去的那个地方之间所能望见的每一只船都追上一遍。

「那是什么地方？」科斯莫问，又把头支在双手上。

「朝里窝那那个方向，」对方说，又咽了下去。「不过我也许还是不告诉你的好，因为万一你被那些法官、说不定被奥地利人盘问，你当然会想像个绅士那样——一位极其高贵的先生（nobilissimo signore）——说真话，除非你能设法忘掉我已告诉你的那些话，再不然就是，你索性选了跟我们一道走。」

「跟你一道走，」科斯莫重复了一遍，接着，对方语气里某种异样的东西使他坐直了身子，面对阿蒂利奥。「我相信你说得出就做得到，能把我一并拐走。」

「天主不容（Dio ne voglio），」阿蒂利奥答道，「老天不容。你闹将起来，麻烦可没个完。要不为这个，也许对我倒更好，」他若有所思地补了一句。「可我已下定决心，咱们的交往只能带来好处。然而，先生，方才你险些儿连一句问都没有便跟着我们一起走了，因为我们的船头是朝着海那面的，你根本无从想到我是要驶进来。坦白吧，先生，那时你可没想过回头。我只要再把舵把直直地再握五分钟，你便在我掌中了。」

「你当时是怕海关的桨帆船呢，我的朋友，」科斯莫说，像是在辩一个论点。

「先生，就是这一刻，」阿蒂利奥以最郑重的口吻说。「醒醒，」他提高了压低的嗓门，对两个手下说，「拿起一支桨，彼得罗（Pietro），把船划到塔脚下去。」

「还有那个老船夫呢，」科斯莫说。

「慢着，」阿蒂利奥说，「他我可不上岸。他们明早会上他家去，那时他便说出了他的故事……除非他死了。朝船头那边瞧。」

船头传来一阵压得极低的嘟哝，阿蒂利奥嘀咕道：「彼得罗可不愿对一个死人说话。」

「他虚弱极了，」科斯莫说。

阿蒂利奥一问之下，才知这个被他称作累赘的人，气力倒还够让人架着越过座板。不久，由人托着胳膊肘，他到了船尾座板上，与科斯莫并坐，他们让他坐在两人中间。他任凭自己那双大手摊在膝头。他不时耐心地打着寒噤。

「那个恶棍巴博内毫无怜悯之心，」科斯莫评说道。

「我想他是就近能抓到的就抓了。何等的暴政！无力的人任凭这帮家伙摆布。他省下了多走三个门的力气。」

两人都端详着那一副岁月未能使之枯槁的老迈身躯。

「当年也是一条好汉，」阿蒂利奥低声说。「听得见我说话吗，老头儿（vecchio）？」

「听得见（Si），也看得见你，可我不认得你的声音，」回答的声音比他俩谁预料的都更响亮，却流露出不带一丝兴致。

「他们准会把他投进牢里。」科斯莫愤慨地一声惊呼，阿蒂利奥便指出，这老人将是他所能抓到的唯一一个人，他得替其余所有人顶罪。

科斯莫表示，他看老人在那儿待不长。

「他宁可在露天下死去，也比死在牢里强，」阿蒂利奥阴郁地低声道。「听着，老人家，我要是指给你一颗星，你能照着它把船把直吗？」

「我还在口齿不清的时候，就已经在船上像在自己家里一样了，」他这样回答，一面抬起胳膊，把它搁在舵柄上，仿佛要证明自己至少还有这点力气。

「我已经有了我的船员，先生。让这位热那亚老人，即便是用他最后一口气，也为整个意大利做一点事吧。现在，只要先生肯拿起方才划得那么顺手的那支头桨，我来划领桨，我们就能让这只船飞起来。」

===Original===
Cosmo felt the subdued vibration of this appeal without having paid any attention to the words. They required no answer. Attilio pressed him as though he had been arguing against objections. Surely he was no friend of tyranny or of Austrian oppressors and he wouldn’t refuse to serve a man whom some hidden power had thrown in his way. He, Attilio, had not sought him. He would have been content never to have seen him. He surely had nothing that could call him back on shore this very night, since he had not been more than three days in Genoa. No time for him to have affairs. The words poured out of his lips into Cosmo’s ear while the white-headed boatman sat still above the torrent of whispered speech, appearing to listen like a venerable judge. What could stand in the way of him lending his luck and the strength of his arm? Surely it couldn’t be love, since he was travelling alone.

“Enough,” said Cosmo, as if the word had been extorted from him by pain, but Attilio felt that his cause had been gained, though he hastened to apologize for the impropriety of the argument, and assure the milord Inglese that nothing would be easier than to put him ashore in the course of the next day.

“What do you think. Excellency, there is my own native village not very far from Genoa on the Riviera di Ponente, and you will be amongst friends to carry out such orders as you may give, or pass you from one to another back to Genoa as fast as mules can climb or horses trot. And it would be the same from any point in Italy. They would get you into Genoa in disguise, or without disguise, and into the very home of Cantelucci, so that you could appear there without a soul knowing how you entered or how you came back.”

Cosmo, feeling a sudden relief, wondered that he should have found it in the mere resolution to go off secretly with only the clothes he stood up in, absolutely without money or anything of value on him, not even a watch, and without a hat, at the mere bidding of a man bound on some secret work, God knows where and for what object, and who had volunteered to him no statement except that he had cousins in every spot in Italy and a love affair with an ortolana. The enormous absurdity of it made him impatient to be doing, and upon his expressed desire to make a start Attilio, with the words, “You command here, signore,” told his men it was time to be moving.

In less than half an hour the boat, with all her crew crouching at the bottom and using the oars for poling in the shallow water along the coast with infinite precaution to avoid knocks and bangs as though the boat, the oars, and everything in her were made of glass, had been moved far enough from the tower to have her nose put to the open sea. After the first few strokes Cosmo felt himself draw back again to the receding shore. But it was too late. He seemed to feel profoundly that he was not﻿—perhaps no man was﻿—a free agent. He felt a sort of fear, a faltering of all his limbs, as he swung back to his oar. Then his eyes caught the galley, indeed everybody’s eyes in the boat were turned that way except the eyes of the ancient steersman, the white-headed figure in an unexpectedly erect attitude who, with hardly any breath left in his body and a mere helpless victim of other men’s will, had a strange appearance of the man in command.

In less than ten minutes the galley became invisible, and even the long shadows of the jetties had sunk to the level of the sea. There was a moment when one of the men observed without excitement, “She’s after us,” but this remark provoked no answer and turned out to be mistaken, and for an hour longer Attilio, pulling stroke, watched the faint phosphorescent wake, the evanescent fire under the black smoothness of the sea, elusive like the tail of a comet amongst the dim reflections of the stars. Its straightness was the only proof of the silent helmsman with his arm resting along the tiller being still alive. Then he began to look about him, and presently, laying in his oar, relieved the old man at the tiller. He had to take his arm off it. The other never said a word.

===Chinese===
科斯莫感到了这一番恳求里那种压抑着的震颤，虽然他对那些话本身并未怎么留意。它们无需回答。阿蒂利奥紧迫着他，仿佛一直在反驳种种异议似的。他当然不是暴政的朋友，也不是奥地利压迫者的朋友，他总不至于拒绝效劳一个被某种隐藏的力量抛到他面前的人吧。他阿蒂利奥并没有主动去找他。他本可一辈子不见他也心甘。他今夜身上一定没有什么能把他唤回岸上去的东西，因为他到热那亚不过三天。三天还不够他有什么牵挂。这些话从他唇间倾泻出来，落进科斯莫的耳朵里，而那白发苍苍的船夫端坐在那一股耳语的激流之上，仿佛一位可敬的法官在那儿听着。又有什么能阻挡他借出自己的运气和胳膊上的力气呢？总不会是爱情吧，既然他是独自一人旅行的。

「够了，」科斯莫说，这话仿佛是被疼痛从他嘴里逼出来的，可阿蒂利奥却感到自己的目的已经达到，于是他赶忙为这番话的不妥赔罪，又向这位英国老爷（milord Inglese）保证，明天白天里要送他上岸，再容易不过。

「您看如何，大人？离热那亚不远的里维耶拉·迪·波南特（Riviera di Ponente）海岸上，便是我本乡的村子，您将置身于一群朋友之中，由他们来照办您可能下达的任何吩咐；或者一个接一个地传您，快得像骡子爬山、马儿小跑那样，把您送回热那亚。而且从意大利任何一个地方也都一样。他们能送您乔装、或不乔装地进了热那亚，一直送进坎泰洛奇的家，让您能出现在那里，没有一个人知道您是怎么进去的，又是怎么回来的。」

科斯莫忽然感到一阵轻松，自己也纳罕：他竟从这样一个决断里找到了轻松——仅仅穿着身上这一身衣服，身无分文、不带一件值钱的东西、连块表也没有、连顶帽子也没有，便偷偷地随一个身负秘密使命——天知道去哪里、为了什么——的人走掉，而那人除了告诉他他在意大利处处都有表亲、又与一个卖菜姑娘（ortolana）有段恋情之外，什么也没向他说明。这件事天大的荒唐反倒叫他迫不及待地要动起来，于是他表示想动身，阿蒂利奥便说了一句：「您在这儿发号施令，先生，」便吩咐手下：该走了。

不到半个钟头，那只船——全体船员都蹲伏在船底，沿着岸边浅水里用桨撑船，小心翼翼到了极点，生怕碰出一声磕碰，仿佛船、桨，以及船里的一切都是玻璃做的——已从塔边挪出足够远，把船头朝向外海了。划过头几桨之后，科斯莫觉得自己又被拽向那正在远去的海岸。可已太迟了。他似乎深切地感到，自己并不——也许任何人都并不——是个自由的人。当他重新挥动双桨时，他感到了一种恐惧，一种四肢百骸的战栗。随后他的眼睛捕捉到了那条桨帆船，事实上船上每个人的眼睛都转向了那边，唯有那位年迈的舵手的眼睛例外——那个白发的身影，姿态出乎意料地挺直，他体内几乎再无一缕气息，纯粹是别人意志下一个无助的牺牲品，却奇怪地显出一副像是发号施令的那个人似的模样。

不到十分钟，桨帆船已看不见了，连防波堤长长的影子也沉到了与海面平齐。有那么一刻，其中一个汉子不带一点激动地察觉道：「它追上来了，」可这话没引来一句回答，结果也证明是看错了；此后又有一个钟头，阿蒂利奥划着领桨，注视着那幽幽的磷光尾迹，那黑色平滑海面下转瞬即逝的火光，在星辰朦胧的倒影之间，像彗星的尾巴一般飘忽难定。那尾迹笔直地拖在船后，是那位沉默的舵手——胳膊搁在舵柄上——仍旧活着的唯一证据。然后他开始四下张望，不久便收了桨，去接替老人掌舵。他不得不把老人的胳膊从舵柄上移开。老人一声没吭。

===Original===
The boat moved slowly now. The problem was to discover the awaiting felucca without lights and with her sails lowered. Several times Attilio stood up to have a look without being able to make out anything. He was growing uneasy. He spoke to Cosmo.

“I hope we haven’t passed her by. If we once get her between us and the land it will be hopeless to catch sight of her till the day breaks. Better rest on your oars.”

He remained standing himself. His eyes roamed to and fro patiently and suddenly he emitted a short laugh.

“Why, there she is.”

He steered, still standing, while the others pulled gently. The old man, who had not emitted a sound, had slipped off the seat on to the stem sheets. Attilio said quietly, “Take your oars in,” and suddenly Cosmo felt the boat bump against the low side of the felucca, which he had never tinned his head to see. No hail or even murmur came from her. She had no lights. Attilio’s voice said, “You first, signore,” and Cosmo, looking up, saw three motionless heads above the bulwarks. No word was spoken to him. He was not even looked at by those silent and shadowy men. The first sound he heard were the words, “Take care,” pronounced by Attilio in connection with getting the old boatman on board. Cosmo, standing aside, saw a group carry him over to the other side of the deck. While the sails were being hoisted he sat on the hatch and came to the very verge of believing himself invisible till suddenly Attilio stood by his side.

“Like this we will catch the very first breath of daybreak, and may a breeze follow it to take us out of sight of that town defiled by the Austrians and soon to be the prey of the nobles and the priests.” He paused. “So at least Cantelucci says. There are bed places below, if you want to take some rest, signore.”

“I am not sleepy,” said Cosmo. If no longer invisible, he could still feel disembodied, as it were. He was neither sleepy nor tired, nor hungry, nor even curious, as if altogether freed from the weaknesses of the body, and not indifferent but without apprehensions or speculations of any sort to disturb his composure as if of a fully informed wisdom. He did not seem to himself to weigh more than a feather. He was suffering the reaction of the upheaval of all his feelings and the endless contest of his thoughts and that sort of mental agony which had taken possession of him while he was descending the great staircase of the Palazzo under the eye of the Count of Montevesso. It was as though one of those fevers in which the victim watches his own delirium had left him irresponsible, like a sick man in his bed. Attilio went on:

“Cantelucci’s an experienced conspirator. He thinks that the force of the people is such that it would be like an uprising of the ground itself. May be, but where is the man that would know how to use it?”

Cosmo let it go by like a problem that could await solution or as a matter of mere vain words. The night air did not stir, and Attilio changed his tone.

“They had their lines out ever since the calm began. We will have fish to eat in the morning. You will have to be one of ourselves for a time and observe the customs of the common people.”

“Tell me, Attilio,” Cosmo questioned, not widely but in a quiet, almost confidential tone, and laying his hand for the first time on the shoulder of that man only a little older than himself. “Tell me, what am I doing here?”

Attilio, the wanderer of the seas along the southern shores of the earth and the pupil of the hermit of the plains that lie under the constellation of the southern sky, smiled in the dark, a faint friendly gleam of white teeth in an overshadowed face. But all the answer he made was:

“Who would dare say now that our stars have not come together? Come to sit at the stern, signore. I can find a rug to throw over a coil of rope for a seat. I am now the padrone of that felucca, but of course barring her appointed work you are entirely the master of her.”

These words were said with a marked accent of politeness such as one uses for a courtesy formula. But he stopped for a moment on his way aft to point his finger on the deck.

“We have thrown a bit of canvas over him. Yes, that is the old man whose last bit of work was to steer a boat, and strange to think perhaps it had been done for Italy.”

“Where is his star now?” said Cosmo, after looking down in silence for a time.

“Signore, it should be out,” said Attilio with studied intonation. “But who will miss it out of the sky?”

===Chinese===
这会儿船行得很慢。难处在于要找到那只等着接应的、不点灯、帆也落下未张的三桅小帆船（felucca）。阿蒂利奥站起来望了好几回，什么也辨不出来。他渐渐不安起来。他朝科斯莫开了口。

「但愿我们没有从她旁边划过去了。一旦让她落到我们和陆地之间，那么天亮之前就别想再瞥见她了。还是停桨歇一歇吧。」

他自己仍站着。他的目光耐心地来回梭巡，忽然发出一声短笑。

「咦，她就在那儿。」

他仍站着掌舵，其余的人轻轻划着。那始终没发出一点声响的老人，已从座板上滑到了船尾座板处。阿蒂利奥平静地说：「收桨，」忽然间科斯莫感到小船撞上了那只三桅帆船低矮的船舷——他自始至终没有回过头去看它一眼。船上传不来一声招呼，连一声低语也没有。它没有点灯。阿蒂利奥的声音说：「先生先上，」科斯莫抬头一看，舷墙上方露着三颗纹丝不动的人头。没有人对他说一句话。那些沉默而朦胧的人甚至连看也不看他一眼。他所听到的第一声声响，是阿蒂利奥说的「当心」二字，那是与把老船夫弄上船有关的。科斯莫让到一旁，看见一群人把他抬着横穿过甲板，送到另一侧。升帆的当儿，他坐在舱口上，几乎要相信自己是个隐形的人了，直到阿蒂利奥忽然站到了他身边。

「这样我们就能赶上破晓的头一缕气息，但愿随后便来一阵风，把我们带出那座被奥地利人玷污了、眼看就要沦为贵族和神父的猎物的城池的视野。」他顿了顿。「至少坎泰洛奇是这么说的。下面有铺位，先生若是想歇一歇。」

「我不困，」科斯莫说。即便已不再隐形，他仍觉得自己仿佛脱了形骸。他既不困，也不累，也不饿，甚至连好奇也谈不上，仿佛已全然摆脱了肉身的种种孱弱，并非冷漠，而是既无任何忧惧、也无任何揣测来搅扰他那一身仿佛无所不知的智慧般的从容。他觉得自己仿佛比一根羽毛还轻。他正承受着那一番感情大动荡和无尽的思绪交锋的反作用，承受着那种在他于蒙特韦索伯爵（Count of Montevesso）的目光下走下府邸大楼梯时攫住他的精神苦痛。这情形，仿佛一个人得了那种病人能旁观自己呓语的热病，病后落得个浑然忘我，像床上的病人一般。阿蒂利奥接着说：

「坎泰洛奇是个老练的密谋者。他认为人民的力量大到足以叫大地震动而起来。也许是吧，可懂得怎样驾驭这股力量的人，又在哪儿呢？」

科斯莫让它像一道可以留待将来再去求解的难题、又像一番空洞的言词似地从身边滑了过去。夜里的空气纹丝不动，阿蒂利奥换了个口气。

「打无风那时起，他们就把钓线放下水了。早上我们有鱼吃了。先生有一阵子得做我们中间的一个，见识见识平民百姓的规矩。」

「告诉我，阿蒂利奥，」科斯莫问道，问得不远，却用一种平静的、几乎推心置腹的口吻，头一回把手搭在这位只比他稍长几岁的人的肩上。「告诉我，我在这儿做什么？」

阿蒂利奥——他曾沿着大地的南端海岸漂泊过四海，是南天星座之下那片平原上的隐士的门徒——在黑暗里笑了，那是阴影笼罩的一张脸上、一闪白牙的、淡淡的友善的笑。可他所回答的，只是：

「这会儿谁还敢说，我们的星没有走到一起呢？到船尾来坐吧，先生。我可以找块毯子铺在一盘缆绳上，权当座位。我如今是这只三桅帆船的船主（padrone）了，不过当然啦，除了它分内的活计之外，它完全听凭先生做主。」

这番话说得带着一种明显的客气的口吻，一如人说客套话时那样。可他在往后艄走的路上停了停，用手指了指甲板。

「我们给他盖了一块帆布。是的，那就是那位老人，他最后一桩活儿是给一只船掌舵，而说来奇怪，这桩活儿也许是为意大利做的。」

「他的星如今在哪儿？」科斯莫沉默地俯视了一阵之后说。

「先生，它该是熄了，」阿蒂利奥以刻意经营的语调说。「可谁又会因为它从天上熄了，便觉得少了什么呢？」
